\title{A Full Complexity Dichotomy for Complex-Valued\\[0.15em]
		Boolean Holant Problems}
	\author[1,*]{Chenghua Liu}
	\author[2,\textdagger]{Boning Meng}
	\author[1,\textdaggerdbl]{Juqiu Wang}
	
	\affil[1]{Institute of Software, Chinese Academy of Sciences, Beijing, China}
	\affil[2]{University of Regensburg, Regensburg, Germany}
	
	\affil[ ]{%
		\textsuperscript{*}\texttt{liuch.russell@gmail.com}\quad

		\textsuperscript{\textdaggerdbl}\texttt{wangjuqiu104@gmail.com}%
	}
	
	\date{}

\hypersetup{pageanchor=false}
\begin{titlepage}
\maketitle
\thispagestyle{empty}
	
	
	\begin{abstract}\normalsize
		A Boolean Holant problem evaluates a graph partition function specified
        by local signatures at its vertices.  We prove a complexity dichotomy
        for arbitrary finite sets of algebraic complex-valued signatures on
        general graphs: an explicit, decidable criterion characterizes the
        polynomial-time cases, and every remaining case is \(\SharpP\)-hard
        under polynomial-time Turing reductions.  The proof resolves the
        all-even case by constructing signatures with nonzero endpoints and
        all remaining support on the central layer, then classifying them
        according to half their arity. Binary transfer groups and matching-product
        reconstruction provide the main tools in the small- and large-height cases,
        respectively.
	\end{abstract}
\end{titlepage}

\clearpage
\pagenumbering{arabic}
\hypersetup{pageanchor=true}

% BEGIN SHORT PAPER
\section{Introduction}
\label{short:sec:introduction}

Counting problems ask how many global configurations satisfy a collection
of local constraints. Counting proper graph colourings, for example,
sums over vertex colours subject to a constraint on each edge. In a
weighted problem, each configuration contributes the product of its local
weights, and their sum is a partition function. Although this sum has
exponentially many terms, some choices of local rules permit efficient
evaluation. A complexity dichotomy identifies all such choices and proves
hardness for every remaining choice.

A Boolean Holant problem places local functions, called signatures, at
the vertices of a graph. Each edge receives a bit, and the partition
function sums the product of the vertex signatures over all edge
assignments. We prove a dichotomy for arbitrary finite sets of algebraic
complex-valued Boolean signatures on general graphs, with an explicit,
decidable tractability criterion. The main case addressed here consists
entirely of even-arity signatures, with neither a symmetry restriction nor
a prescribed auxiliary binary signature.

\subsection{From graph homomorphisms through counting CSP to Holant}
\label{short:subsec:intro-history}

Counting graph homomorphisms (\(\#\mathrm{GH}\)) asks how many maps from an
input graph to a fixed target graph preserve adjacency.  In the weighted
version, a fixed matrix specifies the interaction between the labels at
the two ends of each edge.  Counting constraint satisfaction problems
(\(\#\mathrm{CSP}\)) allow constraints of arbitrary arity, so one local
function can constrain several variables at once.  The weighted problem
sums the product of the constraint values over all variable assignments.

Dichotomies for unweighted graph homomorphisms and Boolean counting CSP
\cite{DyerGreenhill2000,CreignouHermann1996} established early classifications
in these frameworks.  Subsequent work extended the allowed domains and
weights, from nonnegative weights to signed-real and complex weights,
where cancellation must also be accounted for.  The general-domain complex
classification of Cai and Chen covers both weighted graph homomorphisms
and counting CSP \cite{CaiChen2017}.
\Cref{short:tab:intro-gh-csp-history} records this development.  A dash means that
the case is covered by a more general entry, with no separate reference
listed.

Holant changes which local constraints are available.  A counting-CSP
instance can be represented by a bipartite graph with variable vertices
and constraint vertices.  At each variable vertex, an equality signature
forces all occurrences of that variable to agree.  In a Holant problem,
these equality signatures need not be available: every vertex must use a
signature from the prescribed set. The signatures needed in a reduction
must be obtained by gadgets or other justified reductions. This distinction brings
problems such as counting perfect matchings into the classification and
changes the constructions available for proving hardness.

Motivated by Valiant's holographic algorithms \cite{Valiant2008}, the
Holant framework was introduced in 2009 \cite{CaiLuXia2009}.
A holographic transformation is an invertible simultaneous change of basis
on the signatures and edge tensors that preserves their contraction.
Such transformations relate apparently different counting problems and
expose tractable signature sets.

On the Boolean domain, Holant classifications developed along two
complementary directions: relaxing restrictions on symmetry and weights,
and reducing the auxiliary signatures assumed available.  Unary
signatures are useful because they allow an input of a signature to be
fixed or summed with a chosen weight.  The auxiliary assumptions range
from all unary signatures (\(\Holant^*\)), through four distinguished
unaries (\(\Holant^+\)), to the two unaries fixing a bit to 0 or 1
(\(\Holant^c\)).  Later work treated any signature set containing a
nonzero odd-arity signature (\(\Holant^{\mathrm{odd}}\)).
\Cref{short:tab:intro-boolean-holant-history} separates these assumptions from
restrictions on symmetry and weights, using the same dash convention as
\cref{short:tab:intro-gh-csp-history}.

Without auxiliary unary signatures, dichotomies were obtained for
symmetric complex-valued signatures \cite{CaiGuoWilliams2016}, arbitrary
nonnegative signatures \cite{LinWang2018}, and arbitrary real-valued
signatures \cite{ShaoCai2020}.

In 2025, Meng, Wang, and Xia proved an \(\FP^{\mathsf{NP}}\) versus
\(\SharpP\)-hard dichotomy for weighted Eulerian orientations
\cite{MengWangXia2025EO}.  Meng, Wang, Xia, and Zheng combined this result
with tensor decomposition to obtain the same complexity bounds for complex
Boolean Holant with a nonzero odd-arity signature
\cite{MengWangXiaZheng2025}.  In 2026, Guan, Shao, and Shi gave a
polynomial-time algorithm based on linear programming for the former
\(\FP^{\mathsf{NP}}\) cases, yielding \(\FP\) versus \(\SharpP\)-hard
dichotomies for both problems \cite{GuanShaoShi2026}.

These results direct attention to the all-even case.  Every gadget built
from even-arity signatures has even arity, since each internal edge consumes
two inputs.  Thus no such gadget supplies a unary signature.  Binary
signatures become the smallest positive-arity objects available for further
constructions.  Xia proposed a framework organized by the finite groups
associated with binary signatures, analyzing several branches in which all
realizable nonzero quaternary signatures decompose \cite{Xia2026Framework}.
Liu and Meng then classified complex Boolean Holant with binary
disequality available, for arbitrary finite sets of algebraic
complex-valued signatures, and gave an explicit decidable tractability
criterion \cite{Liu2026DEQ2Holant}.

Our proof removes the prescribed-binary assumption in the all-even case.
It combines the binary and quaternary dichotomies of Liu and Meng with a
decomposition based on Hamming imbalance.  This decomposition produces
signatures of controlled support whose arity determines the remaining
cases, as described in \cref{short:sec:mountain,short:sec:height-one,short:sec:higher}.  Together with the
odd-arity dichotomy, it completes the unrestricted complex-valued entry in
\cref{short:tab:intro-boolean-holant-history}.

\paragraph{Independent work.}
Huang and Fu established a dichotomy for Holant problems with a single
complex-valued quaternary signature \cite{HuangFu2026FourRegular}.
Guan, Shao, and Tang proved a dichotomy for complex-valued Boolean Holant
problems whose signature sets are closed under complex conjugation
\cite{GuanShaoTang2026ConjugateClosed}.
Zhou proved a dichotomy for finite sets of algebraic complex-valued Boolean
signatures of even arity whose nonzero binary gadget signatures are
nonsingular and whose projective matrix classes form the cyclic group
\(C_2\) of order two under series composition \cite{ZhouC2Holant}.

We were also informed of the following independent work before the
public release of the present paper.
Jiayi Zheng informed us of unpublished work on the complex-valued Holant
dichotomy in the case where the projective binary transfer classes form an
odd dihedral group.
Mingji Xia informed us of work concerning the twisted Klein-group case
and the \(C_1\) case, under the condition that all quaternary signatures
are reducible.
The results and proofs in the present paper were developed independently
of these works.

\subsection{Our result: the full dichotomy}
\label{short:subsec:main}

In their odd-arity dichotomy, Meng, Wang, Xia, and Zheng
\cite[conference version, Theorem~30; full version, Theorem~33]{MengWangXiaZheng2025}
formulate condition~(PC) as seven structural alternatives for signature
sets. These specify support restrictions, tensor decompositions, or
membership in prescribed function classes under a common change of basis.
We write \(\Tract(\cF)\) when \(\cF\) satisfies at least one of these
seven alternatives, using the equivalent formulation in
\cref{sec:predicate}. Each alternative concerns
the whole set: in a transformability case, one basis must work
simultaneously for the edge and every member of \(\cF\).

The algorithms for these seven forms, together with Guan, Shao, and Shi's
\(\FP\) improvement for the cases previously known to lie in
\(\FP^{\mathsf{NP}}\) \cite{GuanShaoShi2026}, give polynomial-time
evaluation whenever this structural condition holds. Our contribution is
to prove hardness outside these seven forms without the odd-arity
assumption.

\begin{theorem}[Full dichotomy]
\label{short:thm:main}
Let \(\cF\) be a finite set of algebraic complex-valued Boolean signatures,
given by exact algebraic numbers. The predicate \(\Tract(\cF)\) is
decidable. If it holds, then \(\Holant(\cF)\in\FP\). Otherwise,
\(\Holant(\cF)\) is \(\SharpP\)-hard under polynomial-time Turing reductions.
\end{theorem}

For evaluation and hardness reductions, \(\cF\) is fixed and running time
is measured in the encoded instance size. For recognition, \(\cF\) itself
is the input. Exact algebraic data may be represented by minimal polynomials
and isolating data for the chosen roots. Decidability of the criterion and
evaluation in its tractable cases are supplied by
\cite[Theorem~2.22]{Liu2026DEQ2Holant}. We prove the remaining hardness
direction. After the known cases are removed, a construction based on
Hamming imbalance produces a signature with two nonzero endpoints and all
other support on its central layer. We call this signature a mountain and
classify the remaining problem according to its height.

The full paper follows the shared references. References with prefix F
point to its detailed statements and proofs.

\section{Known classifications and reductions}
\label{short:sec:known}

We first specify when an existing result completes a branch of the proof.
A signature of arity \(r\) is a function
\(f:\{0,1\}^r\to\AlgNums\), where \(\AlgNums\) is the field of algebraic
complex numbers. Its arguments follow the fixed order of the incident
edges. For a graph \(G=(V,E)\) labelled by signatures \(f_v\), its Holant is
\(\sum_{\sigma:E\to\{0,1\}}\prod_{v\in V}f_v(\sigma|_{E(v)})\).
We allow parallel edges and self-loops. Zero signatures and nullary
constants are handled directly, so subsequent sets contain nonzero
positive-arity signatures.

For counting problems \(A,B\), write \(A\leT B\) if a polynomial-time
algorithm with oracle access to \(B\) computes \(A\), and write
\(A\equivT B\) when both directions hold.
Put \(X=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&1\\1&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)\) and
\(K=2^{-1/2}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&1\\i&-i\end{smallmatrix}\right)\).
For a matrix \(D\), write \(Df=D^{\otimes r}f\) and
\(D\cF=\{Df:f\in\cF\}\). Since \(K^{\mathsf T}K=X\), the holographic
transformation gives
\[
\Holant(\cF)\equivT\Holant(X\mid K^{-1}\cF)
=\KHolant(K^{-1}\cF).
\]
Here each edge joins opposite bits at its two ends. From now on, \(\cF\)
denotes the current set in these \(K\)-coordinates. The structural
condition is therefore \(\Tract(K\cF)\).
A gadget is a fixed network with ordered dangling edges, whose signature
is obtained by summing over the internal bits. A directly realized
signature can be substituted at every occurrence. More generally, a finite
signature set \(S\) can be \emph{simulated} from \(\cF\) if
\[
\KHolant(\cF\cup S)\leT\KHolant(\cF).
\]
Simulation may use several oracle queries, as in interpolation, without
a gadget realizing each signature of \(S\).
Known nonzero scalar factors are retained in these reductions.

\paragraph{Odd arity and Hamming layers.}
If \(\cF\) contains a nonzero odd-arity signature, the dichotomy of
\cite{MengWangXiaZheng2025}, together with \cite{GuanShaoShi2026}, completes
the classification. In particular, this applies whenever a reduction
supplies a nonzero unary. We may therefore consider only even arities.
For an arity-\(r\) input \(\mathbf x\), define its \emph{half-charge} by
\(\chi_r(\mathbf x)=\wt(\mathbf x)-r/2\), where \(\wt\) is Hamming weight.
Every internal \(X\)-edge removes one zero and one one, so half-charge is
conserved: in a gadget it equals the boundary half-charge, and in a closed
instance the total is zero. An \emph{EO signature} is supported on the
central layer \(\wt(\mathbf x)=r/2\).

If all supported half-charges in the entire set are nonnegative, or all
are nonpositive, only central entries contribute to a closed instance.
The EO dichotomy \cite{MengWangXia2025EO,GuanShaoShi2026} then classifies
the problem. The case in which each signature is supported on one Hamming
layer is also classified \cite[Corollary~68]{MengWangXia2025EO}.
Its reduction appends fixed zero or one bits to balance each signature's
charge. In each charge-balanced component these bits can be paired by
\(X\)-edges, with a unique extension of every original assignment.
The remaining set is \emph{two-sided}, meaning both signs occur among
its supported half-charges, and \emph{multilayer}, meaning at least one
member has support on different Hamming layers.

\paragraph{Factors, equality, and four variables.}
A tensor product uses disjoint variable blocks. A nonzero positive-arity
signature is \emph{irreducible} if it cannot be written as a tensor product
of two positive-arity signatures, after a permutation of variables.
The tensor-factor extraction theorem
\cite[full version, Theorem~32 and Remark~39]{MengWangXiaZheng2025}
allows the factors of a nonzero tensor product to be simulated,
outside the common-one-sided case just handled. Initial factor
extraction preserves two-sidedness and the multilayer property. An odd
factor invokes the odd-arity result. If all factors have arity at most
two, tensor decomposition reduces evaluation to paths and cycles.
Otherwise we start with irreducible even-arity signatures, at least one
of arity greater than two.

Two further results apply to the whole current signature set. If binary
equality \(I\) can be simulated, the dichotomy of
\cite[Theorem~1.2]{Liu2026DEQ2Holant} applies. The same is true for a
nonsingular weighted equality \(\diag(p,q)\): a common diagonal change of
basis makes it \(I\) and preserves \(X\) up to a nonzero scalar.
An \emph{eight-vertex signature} is a quaternary signature supported on
even Hamming weights. If an irreducible such signature has both endpoint
values nonzero, \cite[Theorem~1.3]{Liu2026DEQ2Holant} applies. A reducible
one with these endpoint values has a nonsingular weighted-equality
factor, so that case is classified as well. These consequences are stated in \cref{thm:eq2-kholant-dichotomy} and
\cref{cor:eight-vertex-complete-closure}.

In the remaining hardness proof we assume \(\neg\Tract(K\cF)\).
This assumption persists when the set is enlarged by simulated signatures,
since the seven structural alternatives are inherited by subsets. It also persists under
common changes of basis in
\(GO(X)=\{D\in\GL_2(\AlgNums):D^{\mathsf T}XD=\lambda X,
\ \lambda\ne0\}\). Thus each applicable classification above gives
hardness, which transfers back along the reductions. Tensor reconstruction
transfers any tractability conclusion for the initial factor set back to
the original set (\cref{sec:assembly}).

\section{Obtaining a mountain}
\label{short:sec:mountain}

The remaining signatures may have arbitrary arity and complex
coefficients. We seek a signature with two nonzero endpoint values whose
other support lies on its central Hamming layer. Such a signature retains
both signs of half-charge, while its contractions have much more
restricted support. For \(d\ge1\), a \emph{mountain of height \(d\)} is an
arity-\(2d\) signature \(M\) satisfying
\[
\supp(M)\subseteq HW_{2d}^{=}\cup\{\zero^{2d},\one^{2d}\},
\qquad M(\zero^{2d})M(\one^{2d})\ne0,
\]
where \(HW_{2d}^{=}\) is the set of weight-\(d\) strings.

\begin{lemma}[Mountain construction]
\label{short:lem:mountain}
Let \(\cF\) be a finite two-sided set of nonzero even-arity signatures, and
let \(d\) be the gcd of all its nonzero supported half-charges. Then either
a mountain \(M\) of height \(d\) can be simulated, or both
pinning signatures \(\Delta_0=(1,0)\) and \(\Delta_1=(0,1)\) can be simulated.
In the mountain case, every supported half-charge in
\(\cF\cup\{M\}\) belongs to \(d\mathbb Z\).
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}[Proof outline]
Let \(C\) be the finite set of nonzero supported half-charges. Tensor
products add half-charges, so the first task is to express both \(d\) and
\(-d\) as sums of elements of \(C\) with nonnegative multiplicities.
The gcd is an integer linear combination
\(\sum_{c\in C}u_cc=d\), but some coefficients may be negative.
For every \(a,-b\in C\), with \(a,b>0\), the positive multiplicities
\(b/\gcd(a,b)\) and \(a/\gcd(a,b)\) give total charge zero.
Adding these zero-sum combinations over all positive--negative pairs
gives \(\mathbf v\in\mathbb Z_{>0}^C\) with \(\sum_cv_cc=0\).
Consequently \(\mathbf u+k\mathbf v\) and \(-\mathbf u+k\mathbf v\)
are nonnegative for sufficiently large \(k\), while their total charges
are \(d\) and \(-d\).

Choose a supported input witnessing each charge used in these two tensor
products. The endpoint-preserving self-loop lemma
\cite[full version, Lemma~40]{MengWangXiaZheng2025} gives two
arity-\(2d\) gadget signatures \(P,N\) with
\(P(\one^{2d})N(\zero^{2d})\ne0\). Charge conservation keeps every
supported charge divisible by \(d\). At this arity only \(-d,0,d\)
are possible, so both signatures already have the required support.
It remains to put the two nonzero endpoints into the same signature.

If either signature already has both endpoints, we are done. Otherwise
\(P(\zero^{2d})=N(\one^{2d})=0\). If both have nonzero central
restrictions, permute supported central strings to
\((\zero^d,\one^d)\) in \(P\) and \((\one^d,\zero^d)\) in \(N\).
Join their last \(d\) variables by \(X\)-edges and expose the first
\(d\) variables of each. The resulting signature \(Q\) satisfies
\[
\begin{split}
Q(\zero^{2d})&=P(\zero^d,\one^d)N(\zero^{2d}),\\
Q(\one^{2d})&=P(\one^{2d})N(\one^d,\zero^d).
\end{split}
\]
Each endpoint has a unique nonzero extension, so cancellation is
excluded. If just one central restriction is nonzero, use two copies of
that signature and the pure endpoint of the other to force the connected
bits. The same unique-extension argument gives both nonzero endpoints.
Charge conservation retains the support restriction.

If both central restrictions are zero, \(P\) and \(N\) are nonzero
scalar multiples of tensor powers of \(\Delta_1\) and \(\Delta_0\).
Factor extraction supplies both pinnings. The original two-sided set
remains present, so the one-sided exception is excluded. The full
construction is \cref{lem:charge-bridge}.
\end{proof}

The pinning outcome is classified by \cref{short:sec:known}. We therefore
retain the mountain and the whole-set divisibility condition.
At height one the mountain is binary, so the available binary operations
control the next steps. At larger heights, contractions below arity
\(2d\) have half-charge zero and hence are EO. This gives two different
ways to constrain the remaining signatures.

\section{Height one: binary transfers and contractions}
\label{short:sec:height-one}

A height-one mountain is a binary
\(B=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a&b\\c&e\end{smallmatrix}\right)\) with
\(ae\ne0\). If a nonzero singular binary can be realized, its unary
factors invoke the odd-arity classification. Binary interpolation gives
the same conclusion when a nonsingular binary transfer has infinite
projective order (\cref{lem:binary-interpolation}).
Here the \emph{transfer} of a binary \(B\) is \(BX\): series composition
satisfies \((BXC)X=(BX)(CX)\). A projective class \([T]\) identifies
matrices differing by a nonzero scalar, and its order is the least
\(m\ge1\) with \(T^m\) scalar, if such an \(m\) exists.

In the remaining case the \emph{complete transfer group} is
\[
G(\cF)=\{[BX]: B\ne0\text{ is an ordered binary directly realizable over }\cF\}.
\]
All these binaries are nonsingular and their transfers have finite order.
The algebraicity of the fixed signature set makes \(G(\cF)\) finite
\cite[Lemma~5.2]{Liu2026DEQ2Holant}. The group analysis in \cref{thm:finite-group-split} gives either a nonsingular
weighted equality, which completes the classification, or a common
\(GO(X)\) change of basis after which the group is odd cyclic or odd
dihedral. We now explain how these two remaining group forms constrain
signatures of higher arity.

\subsection{From restricted contractions to tensor factors}

Fix one directly realized binary representative of each transfer class.
Contract two variables of any current signature with such a
representative, and extract the factors of the nonzero output. A new
binary may enlarge the complete transfer group. A new nonbinary
irreducible factor may lower the minimum such arity. Both changes supply
new information and restart the analysis with an equivalent set.
An odd factor or a binary covered by \cref{short:sec:known} completes
the classification immediately.
If neither occurs, recursive contractions give a finite set
\(\cF^{\mathrm{cl}}\): every indicated contraction has been processed,
and all its irreducible factors are retained. This closure is finite
because a new nonbinary factor has arity at least two less than its
parent (\cref{lem:height-one-card-saturation}).

Choose a nonbinary irreducible \(f\) of minimum arity \(n\) in this
set. Every nonzero two-variable contraction of \(f\) must be a tensor
product of binary factors whose transfer classes lie in the current
group. We call a tensor product of binaries on the pairs of a perfect
matching a \emph{matching product}. The difficulty is to recover information about \(f\) itself:
different contractions could initially use different perfect matchings
of the remaining variables.

Walsh coordinates make the group representatives diagonal or
anti-diagonal. With
\(P=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&1\\1&-1\end{smallmatrix}\right)\), put
\(\widetilde f=(P^{-1})^{\otimes n}f\). Each binary factor of a
nonzero contraction is supported on two complementary assignments, with
the ratio of its ordered nonzero coefficients in \(-\mu_N\).
Here \(\mu_N=\{s:s^N=1\}\) and \(N\ge3\) is odd.
Fix two contracted variables \(i,j\), written first, and let
\(\mathbf y\) denote the remaining variables. After removing known
nonzero scalars, the rotation classes in either group realize
\[
q_s^+(\mathbf y)=\widetilde f(0,0,\mathbf y)
-s\widetilde f(1,1,\mathbf y),\qquad s\in\mu_N.
\]
The reflection classes in the dihedral group additionally realize
\[
q_s^-(\mathbf y)=\widetilde f(0,1,\mathbf y)
-s\widetilde f(1,0,\mathbf y),\qquad s\in\mu_N.
\]
Each family has the form \(q_s=A-sB\), where \(A,B\) are the indicated
slices obtained by fixing \(i,j\). We call it a \emph{contraction pencil}.
Thus the dihedral case permits comparisons between the equal-bit and
opposite-bit restrictions, while the cyclic case has only the first family.

In the dihedral case, we distinguish \(n=4,6\) from \(n\ge8\).
The low-arity cases are treated directly. At arity four, coefficient
analysis gives either a tensor factorization or a signature covered by
the equality reductions in \cref{short:sec:known}. At arity six, a finite
support classification, including computer-assisted enumeration for the
order-three group, followed by coefficient analysis forces a tensor
factorization (\cref{thm:odd-dih-q6-base} and
\cref{proofsubsec:cert-odd-dih-q6}). For \(n\ge8\), we instead recover
a binary factor of \(f\) from the compatibility of its contractions.
Although every nonzero contraction is already a product of binaries,
its matching---which variables are paired---and the coefficients of its
binary factors may initially depend on the contraction.

Fix a contracted pair \(i,j\) and one pencil \(q_s=A-sB\). Its linear
dependence on \(s\) links these otherwise separate factorizations.
Comparing three phases shows that their matchings either coincide or
differ only on four variables, with common binary factors elsewhere.
In the latter case, closing the common external pairs leaves a
six-variable proper contraction of \(f\), which is itself a matching
product. Oddness of \(N\) makes these closures nonzero. Its fixed
matching cannot produce different residual pairings as the phase varies,
excluding this possibility. Thus the matching of \(q_s\) is fixed.
On that matching, the coefficients form a rank-one tensor; linear
dependence on \(s\) then allows at most one binary factor to vary.
Consequently,
\[
q_s=H\otimes h_s,
\]
where \(H\) is a fixed product of binaries and \(h_s\) occupies one
remaining pair. Recovering \(A,B\) from two phases shows that \(H\)
factors out of both slices. Applying this argument to the equal-bit and
opposite-bit pencils gives factorizations of the two corresponding
restrictions of \(\widetilde f\). To compare them, we use contractions
that are nonzero on both restrictions: their residual decompositions
must come from the same matching product. This compatibility identifies
a binary factor shared by the two restrictions, and hence by their sum
\(\widetilde f\). Inverting the Walsh coordinates gives a factorization
of \(f\), contradicting irreducibility (\cref{prop:odd-dih-support-circuit-rigidity} and
\cref{lem:odd-dih-common-binary-factor}).

In the cyclic case, only the equal-bit pencils are available. We use them
to prove that the minimum nonbinary arity is four, then handle that
arity by binary attachments. For \(n\ge6\), the contraction supports
consist of assignments equal on each matched pair. The pencil relation
again fixes the matching as the phase varies. A pair whose entire pencil
vanishes would force its two bits to be opposite on every supported input
of \(\widetilde f\); comparing this constraint with contractions on other
pairs either gives incompatible matchings or a proper tensor
factorization. Thus every pair has a nonzero phase contraction. These
comparisons also force \(\widetilde f(\zero^n)\ne0\), so we normalize it
to one. The weight-two coefficients now reveal which original variables
must be paired. Put
\[
W=\{\{p,q\}:\widetilde f(\mathbf e_p+\mathbf e_q)\ne0\},
\]
where \(\mathbf e_p\) is the \(p\)-th unit vector. Any edge of \(W\)
survives in the fixed matching of a pencil contracted outside that edge.
Two adjacent edges would therefore have to belong to one matching,
which is impossible. Moreover, each edge \(pq\in W\) forces
\(x_p=x_q\) throughout the support of \(\widetilde f\): a violating
assignment could be preserved by a contraction outside \(p,q\), in
conflict with that contraction's matching.

If \(W\ne\varnothing\), compatibility with contractions meeting its
edges makes \(W\) a perfect matching. We may then regard
\(\widetilde f\) as a coefficient tensor \(h(t_1,\ldots,t_{n/2})\),
with one bit for each equal pair. Contracting variables from two pairs
forces their bits to agree and gives a rank-one restriction of \(h\).
These overlapping restrictions determine a product decomposition of
\(h\), and hence of \(f\). The six-variable case requires one further
coefficient check using the fact that squaring permutes \(\mu_N\).
If \(W=\varnothing\), a different obstruction occurs: the constant
coefficient of every contraction is one, while the rank-one identity on
two residual pairs would equate a linear polynomial in \(s\) with a
nonzero quadratic at all \(N\ge3\) phases. This is impossible. Both
alternatives exclude \(n\ge6\). At arity four, binary attachments first
align the coefficients on complementary Walsh inputs. The resulting
forms give an equality reduction, a classified eight-vertex signature,
or a tensor factorization, apart from one exceptional coefficient
pattern. A two-copy construction in that last case yields a binary
that is singular or has a transfer outside the allowed finite cyclic
group. It therefore invokes an earlier classification or contradicts
completeness of the group. This completes the cyclic branch (\cref{thm:odd-cyc-higher-collapse} and
\cref{thm:odd-cyc-quaternary-closure}).

\subsection{Completing the height-one classification}

These arguments complete a fixed cyclic or dihedral case once the
contraction procedure has stabilized. Either a known classification
applies, or no nonbinary irreducible signature remains. In the latter
case, the same Walsh matrix works for every signature: all binary
factors belong to the product class \(\cP\), consisting of products of
unary functions and equality or disequality constraints, and the edge
becomes \(P^{\mathsf T}XP=2\diag(1,-1)\in\cP\). Reconstructing the
signatures from these factors gives one common \(\cP\)-transformation
of the whole set, satisfying one of the seven structural alternatives.

It remains to ensure that the restarts preceding this analysis terminate.
A contraction may reveal a new binary transfer or a smaller nonbinary
irreducible factor. Express every transfer group
back in the original coordinates over one fixed number field. These
groups are nested, and their orders have a uniform bound depending only
on that field. Meanwhile, the minimum nonbinary irreducible arity never
increases. Each strict update increases the former or decreases the
latter. Between updates, the finite contraction recursion above
terminates by arity descent. Thus a classification is eventually reached
(\cref{lem:height-one-progress-well-founded} and
\cref{thm:height-one-transfer-interface}). All changes retain the
original signatures up to one common invertible transformation, so
hardness or a common tractable basis transfers back to the initial set.

\section{Higher heights: local contractions and reconstruction}
\label{short:sec:higher}

Write the mountain as
\(M=a\delta_{\zero^{2d}}+b\delta_{\one^{2d}}+Q\), where \(ab\ne0\),
\(\delta_{\mathbf x}\) is the indicator of the single assignment
\(\mathbf x\), and \(Q\) is its central restriction. Height two is
already covered by the eight-vertex classification in
\cref{short:sec:known}. For \(d\ge3\), the whole-set divisibility
condition makes every gadget output of positive arity less than \(2d\)
EO: its half-charge lies in \(d\mathbb Z\), but has absolute value less
than \(d\). In particular, an \(X\) self-loop gives
\(\partial_{ij}M=\partial_{ij}Q\), where
\((\partial_{ij}f)(\mathbf x)=f(0,1,\mathbf x)+f(1,0,\mathbf x)\)
and the other variables retain their order.

We seek an EO output whose presence forces hardness. Height three uses
a finite family of local constructions. At larger heights, constraints
on all pair contractions reconstruct the central restriction, finishing
heights at least five and leaving one exception at height four.

\subsection{Which EO outputs suffice?}

An EO output sits inside the current set, which also contains the
non-EO mountain. We therefore need a conclusion about this entire set.
Let \(\mathcal B_{\rm ARS}\) consist of nonzero scalar multiples of
tensor products, on oriented pairs of a perfect matching, of binaries
\((0,z,\overline z,0)\) with \(z\ne0\). The subscript refers to
arrow-reversal symmetry, the identity
\(h(\mathbf x)=\overline{h(\overline{\mathbf x})}\), where
\(\overline{\mathbf x}\) is bitwise complement.

\begin{lemma}[An EO output in the ambient set]
\label{short:lem:relative-eo}
Let \(\cF\) be a finite two-sided set of nonzero even-arity signatures
with \(\neg\Tract(K\cF)\). If \(\cF\) contains a nonzero positive-arity
EO signature outside \(\mathcal B_{\rm ARS}\), then
\(\KHolant(\cF)\) is \(\SharpP\)-hard.
\end{lemma}

The proof uses the constructions in the EO classifications
\cite{MengWangXia2025EO,CaiFuShao2020ARS} to obtain either a signature
classified by \cref{short:sec:known} or the asserted binary factorization.
The first stage forces arrow-reversal symmetry up to a scalar; the
second handles signatures with that symmetry that fail to factor.
To use this argument in the present problem, each fixed gadget must
remain valid in the ambient set, which also contains the non-EO
mountain. Gadget substitution supplies this extension for direct
constructions, and two-sidedness permits the separate factor-extraction
reductions. The extension is proved in
\cref{subsec:hc-relative-eo}. Thus, unless hardness
is obtained, every nonzero low-arity EO output has the specified
matching-product form. This turns a complexity classification into
structural information about the mountain's contractions.

\subsection{Height three and a two-copy construction}

Let \(\mathcal B_\times\) be the nonzero scalar multiples of matching
products of nonsingular anti-diagonal binaries
\(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&p\\q&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)\), with
\(pq\ne0\). It contains \(\mathcal B_{\rm ARS}\).
A matching-product central restriction already suffices to finish the
branch. If \(Q\in\mathcal B_\times\), join two copies of the complete
mountain so that their central matching edges and connecting edges form
two odd-length alternating paths between four exposed variables. Central
assignments supply four boundary patterns, with one bit equal to one on
each exposed pair. The two complementary endpoint choices supply two
additional patterns. Mixed endpoint--central assignments are excluded
by an internal matching edge, and each supported boundary has a unique
nonzero extension. Thus the quaternary EO output has all six weight-two
entries nonzero, whereas a quaternary member of \(\mathcal B_\times\)
has only four supported strings. By \cref{short:lem:relative-eo} this
gives hardness. The construction uses the whole mountain and works at
every height \(d\ge3\) (\cref{lem:hc-transversal-cube-terminal}).

Height three is handled directly, without first reconstructing \(Q\).
Self-loops test its four-variable restrictions; to obtain stronger
constraints, we also join two copies of the six-variable mountain along
four pairs of variables, again leaving four variables exposed. A finite
coefficient classification shows that if all fifteen self-loops and all
\(5400\) labelled two-copy outputs are zero or in \(\mathcal B_\times\),
then \(Q\) is supported on at most one complementary pair. This support
is too small to satisfy the same tests: if \(Q\ne0\), a self-loop
joining positions with opposite bits gives a nonzero output supported on
at most two strings; if \(Q=0\), a two-copy output has that property.
Either contradicts the four-string support of a quaternary member of
\(\mathcal B_\times\). One of the tested outputs must therefore give
hardness by \cref{short:lem:relative-eo}.
The finite classification uses computer-assisted exhaustive enumeration
and exact polynomial solver checks for arbitrary complex coefficients
and every \(ab\ne0\). The polynomial infeasibility steps rely on Z3's
\texttt{UNSAT} answers, as documented in \cref{thm:hc-q6-complete} and
\cref{proofsubsec:cert-high-charge-q6}.

\subsection{Normalization and reconstruction at larger heights}

For \(d\ge4\), we reconstruct the central restriction from its pair
contractions. We use the reconstruction theorem of Shao and Cai
\cite[full version, Lemma~2.11]{ShaoCai2020} after making the central
tensor real and complement-invariant and all its contractions products
of \(X\). The task is to obtain these simultaneous properties from our
complex-valued mountain. If \(Q=0\), the mountain is a generalized
equality, already classified by the auxiliary reductions (\cref{lem:pure-ge}). Otherwise, \cref{short:lem:relative-eo} makes
every nonzero contraction a product of EO--ARS binaries. Factor
extraction simulates these binaries. Attaching an appropriately oriented
copy of a factor \(B(z)=(0,z,\overline z,0)\) to one of its variables
turns that factor into \(|z|^2X\). The issue is to normalize all
contractions of the same mountain, since an attachment can affect
several of them. The resulting central restriction \(F\) must satisfy
\[
\partial_eF=\lambda_e X_{P_e},\qquad
X_{P_e}:=\bigotimes_{t\in P_e}X_t,\qquad \lambda_e\ne0
\quad\left(e\in\binom{[2d]}2\right),
\]
where \(P_e\) is a perfect matching of the remaining variables. To reach
this form, first normalize one nonzero contraction. Contracting a
disjoint pair in either order gives the same tensor, so the other
contraction must also be nonzero, and any binary factor surviving the
comparison must be proportional to \(X\). These comparisons constrain
the remaining factors sufficiently to choose further attachments that
preserve the first normalized contraction. They then propagate the
\(X\)-matching form to all pairs, including those whose original
contractions might have vanished. This simultaneous normalization is
proved in \cref{lem:hc-port-normalization}. All
attachments act on the complete mountain; its central restriction is
the tensor analyzed here.

The scalars \(\lambda_e\) may still be complex. To recover symmetry of
the central tensor, we need one common scalar normalization. Commuting
contractions on disjoint pairs \(e,f\) compares \(\lambda_e\) and
\(\lambda_f\): closing a pair in an \(X\)-matching product contributes
only a factor one or two, so their ratio is positive real. Since the
graph of disjoint pairs is connected, all \(\lambda_e\) have the same
phase. Dividing by one scalar makes every contraction real and
complement-invariant. These properties also hold for the central tensor
itself: its differences from its conjugate and from its bit-reversed
tensor are EO and have all pair contractions zero, which forces both
differences to vanish \cite[Lemma~2.7]{CaiFuShao2020ARS}. This establishes
the required symmetry from the contractions (\cref{lem:hc-global-phase}).

We can now apply reconstruction. At arity at least ten, it makes the
central restriction itself an \(X\)-matching product. The two-copy
construction above then gives hardness, completing every height
\(d\ge5\). At arity eight, reconstruction needs an additional overlap:
two distinct contracted pairs \(A,B\) must satisfy
\(A\cap B\ne\varnothing\) and their residual matchings share an edge,
\(P_A\cap P_B\ne\varnothing\). Such an overlap gives the same
matching-product conclusion and finishes that height-four branch.
The only remaining case has no such overlap.

\subsection{The height-four exception}

At height four, we first determine the remaining matching family, then
recover the coefficients of the mountain. Without the preceding overlap,
commutation of double contractions still requires the matchings to agree
after deleting any two disjoint variable pairs in either order. A
computer-assisted exhaustive classification of these compatible families
identifies the eight variables with \(\mathbb F_2^3\): contracting a pair
with difference \(t\ne0\) leaves the other three pairs \(\{x,x+t\}\).
This specifies the contraction matchings, but the coefficients of the
central tensor remain to be recovered. A contraction coefficient is the
sum of two entries whose supports differ by swapping the deleted bits.
The prescribed matching determines which such sums vanish and which
equal its nonzero scalar. The resulting linear relations force all
coefficients outside the fourteen affine planes of \(\mathbb F_2^3\)
to vanish and all plane coefficients to agree (\cref{lem:q8-collision-free-atlas} and
\cref{lem:q8-canonical-support}).

Consequently the mountain has the form
\(M^\circ=A\delta_{\zero^8}+B\delta_{\one^8}+cR_8^{\rm cen}\), with
\(ABc\ne0\), where \(R_8^{\rm cen}\) indicates those fourteen planes.
These fourteen weight-four strings, together with the two constant
strings, are the truth tables of the affine functions on \(\mathbb F_2^3\).
Their set is the Reed--Muller code
\[
RM(1,3)=\{(\alpha\cdot x+\beta)_{x\in\mathbb F_2^3}:
\alpha\in\mathbb F_2^3,\ \beta\in\mathbb F_2\}.
\]
Write \(\RMcore\) for its indicator. To obtain this signature up to
scale, we must also control the endpoint coefficients \(A,B\).
Join two copies of \(M^\circ\) along four coordinates that determine
each affine function uniquely. The remaining coefficients distinguish
endpoint contributions \(AB\) from central contributions \(c^2\).
One further closure produces a six-variable EO tensor whose coefficients
cannot factor over a matching unless \(AB=c^2\). Failure gives hardness
by \cref{short:lem:relative-eo}; equality permits a common diagonal
change of basis in \(GO(X)\) taking the mountain to \(c\RMcore\)
while preserving the whole-set charge condition (\cref{lem:q8-canonical-endpoint-repair}).

We now simulate binary equality, which allows the known dichotomy in
\cref{short:sec:known} to finish the branch. The Reed--Muller identity of
\cite[full version, Lemma~8.9]{ShaoCai2020} replaces three occurrences
of \(I\) by one occurrence and one Reed--Muller vertex, up to a known
nonzero scalar. Repetition leaves at most two occurrences, with only
linear growth in the instance size. Thus it suffices to evaluate
instances with one or two equality vertices. After removing these
vertices, the remaining network is over the current set \(\cF\), whose
supported half-charges all belong to \(4\mathbb Z\). Its boundary tensor
is therefore anti-diagonal on two variables and EO on four variables.
One equality occurrence contributes zero, since it requires equal
boundary bits. For two occurrences, only the weight-two entries matter,
and on that support
\[
I_{12}I_{34}
=\frac{X_{13}X_{24}+X_{14}X_{23}-X_{12}X_{34}}2.
\]
Here \(I_{ij}\) and \(X_{ij}\) act on the indicated pair of variables.
The right-hand side uses only existing \(X\)-edges, so three oracle
queries evaluate the two-occurrence instance. Adjacent equality vertices
collapse directly, and equality self-loops have value zero, covering
arbitrary instances. We have therefore proved
\(\KHolant(\cF,I)\leT\KHolant(\cF)\), and the binary-equality
dichotomy completes the classification. The full version proves the
stronger simulation of all four Bell signatures in
\cref{thm:q8-normalized-bell-lift}.

\section{Completing the dichotomy}
\label{short:sec:completion}

\begin{proof}[Proof of \cref{short:thm:main}]
Decidability and polynomial-time evaluation follow from
\cite[Theorem~2.22]{Liu2026DEQ2Holant}. Suppose none of the seven
structural alternatives holds. By \cref{short:sec:known}, it remains to
treat a two-sided, multilayer set of irreducible even-arity signatures
in \(K\)-coordinates. \Cref{short:lem:mountain} either simulates
pinnings, invoking the odd-arity classification, or supplies a mountain
of height \(d\) and the whole-set charge condition.
\Cref{short:sec:height-one} handles \(d=1\), and
\cref{short:sec:higher} handles \(d\ge2\). The simulations and common
changes of basis preserve failure of the structural condition, so the
applicable dichotomies give hardness. Composing the reductions with
factor reconstruction and the initial holographic transformation proves
the theorem (\cref{sec:synthesis}).
\end{proof}

Appendix~\ref{sec:exact-finite-certificates} specifies the finite classifications used
in the odd-dihedral, height-three, and height-four arguments, including
their coverage and computational dependencies. It distinguishes
enumeration and solver checks used in the proofs from supplementary
computations.

\section*{AI methodology statement}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{AI methodology statement}

We used OpenAI's GPT-5.6 Sol to assist with mathematical exploration and
proof development throughout this paper, including exact symbolic
calculations and finite-case checks. All companion verification code was
generated by OpenAI Codex and subsequently reviewed by the authors.

We used OpenAI's GPT-6 Astra to improve the writing and presentation and
to assist with the Lean formalization. We completed the Lean formalization
of the proof. The authors take full responsibility for the correctness
and originality of the paper.

\section*{Full paper}

\section{Introduction}
\label{sec:introduction}

Counting problems ask how many global configurations satisfy a collection
of local constraints.  For example, counting proper colourings of a graph
means counting assignments of colours to vertices such that adjacent
vertices receive different colours.  In a weighted version, each local
configuration contributes a weight, and the weight of a global
configuration is the product of its local weights.  Summing these products
over all configurations gives a partition function.  Ordinary counting
is the special case in which the local weights are zero or one.

A short description of the local rules can therefore specify a sum with
exponentially many terms.  Some rules allow this sum to be evaluated
efficiently without enumerating the configurations, whereas others lead
to computationally hard counting problems. A complexity dichotomy
identifies all tractable choices of local rules within a given framework
and proves hardness for every remaining choice. Holant provides a
graph-based framework for studying this boundary.

A Boolean Holant problem evaluates a graph partition function specified by
local functions, called signatures, at its vertices.  Each edge receives a
bit, and the partition function sums the product of the vertex signatures
over all edge assignments.  The classification problem asks which fixed
signature sets permit polynomial-time evaluation and which give
\(\SharpP\)-hard problems.

We prove a dichotomy for arbitrary finite sets of algebraic complex-valued
Boolean signatures on general graphs, with an explicit, decidable
tractability criterion.  The main case addressed here consists entirely of
even-arity signatures, with neither a symmetry restriction nor a prescribed
auxiliary binary signature.  Its resolution completes a line of
classification results extending from graph homomorphisms through counting
constraint satisfaction problems to Holant problems.

\subsection{From graph homomorphisms through counting CSP to Holant}
\label{subsec:intro-history}

Counting graph homomorphisms (\(\#\mathrm{GH}\)) asks how many maps from an
input graph to a fixed target graph preserve adjacency.  In the weighted
version, a fixed matrix specifies the interaction between the labels at
the two ends of each edge.  Counting constraint satisfaction problems
(\(\#\mathrm{CSP}\)) allow constraints of arbitrary arity, so one local
function can constrain several variables at once.  The weighted problem
sums the product of the constraint values over all variable assignments.

Dichotomies for unweighted graph homomorphisms and Boolean counting CSP
\cite{DyerGreenhill2000,CreignouHermann1996} established early classifications
in these frameworks.  Subsequent work extended the allowed domains and
weights, from nonnegative weights to signed-real and complex weights,
where cancellation must also be accounted for.  The general-domain complex
classification of Cai and Chen covers both weighted graph homomorphisms
and counting CSP \cite{CaiChen2017}.
\Cref{tab:intro-gh-csp-history} records this development.  A dash means that
the case is covered by a more general entry, with no separate reference
listed.

	\begin{table}[!htbp]
		\centering
		\caption{Development of general-graph dichotomies for weighted graph
			homomorphisms and counting CSP.}
		\label{tab:intro-gh-csp-history}
		\small
		\setlength{\tabcolsep}{3.5pt}
		\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.16}
		\begin{tabularx}{\textwidth}{@{}l
				>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X
				>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X
				>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X
				>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X@{}}
			\toprule
			Weight range & Symmetric \(\#\mathrm{GH}\) & \(\#\mathrm{GH}\) &
			Boolean-domain \(\#\mathrm{CSP}\) &
			General-domain \(\#\mathrm{CSP}\) \\
			\midrule
			\(\{0,1\}\)
			& \cite{DyerGreenhill2000}
			& acyclic targets \cite{DyerGoldbergPaterson2007}
			& \cite{CreignouHermann1996}
			& \cite{Bulatov2013,DyerRicherby2013} \\
			\(\mathbb Q_{\ge0}\)
			& -- & -- & --
			& \cite{BulatovDyerGoldbergJalseniusJerrumRicherby2012} \\
			\(\mathbb R_{\ge0}\)
			& \cite{BulatovGrohe2005}
			& \cite{CaiChen2019}
			& \cite{DyerGoldbergJerrum2009}
			& \cite{CaiChenLu2016} \\
			\(\mathbb R\)
			& \cite{GoldbergGroheJerrumThurley2010}
			& --
			& \cite{BulatovDyerGoldbergJalseniusRicherby2009}
			& -- \\
			\(\mathbb C\)
			& \cite{CaiChenLu2013}
			& \cite{CaiChen2017}
			& \cite{CaiLuXia2014}
			& classification \cite{CaiChen2017},
			decidability \cite{LiuMeng2026CSPDecidability} \\
			\bottomrule
		\end{tabularx}
	\end{table}

Holant changes which local constraints are available.  A counting-CSP
instance can be represented by a bipartite graph with variable vertices
and constraint vertices.  At each variable vertex, an equality signature
forces all occurrences of that variable to agree.  In a Holant problem,
these equality signatures need not be available: every vertex must use a
signature from the prescribed set. The signatures needed in a reduction
must be obtained by gadgets or other justified reductions. This distinction brings
problems such as counting perfect matchings into the classification and
changes the constructions available for proving hardness.

Motivated by Valiant's holographic algorithms \cite{Valiant2008}, the
Holant framework was introduced in 2009 \cite{CaiLuXia2009}.
A holographic transformation is an invertible simultaneous change of basis
on the signatures and edge tensors that preserves their contraction.
Such transformations relate apparently different counting problems and
expose tractable signature sets.

On the Boolean domain, Holant classifications developed along two
complementary directions: relaxing restrictions on symmetry and weights,
and reducing the auxiliary signatures assumed available.  Unary
signatures are useful because they allow an input of a signature to be
fixed or summed with a chosen weight.  The auxiliary assumptions range
from all unary signatures (\(\Holant^*\)), through four distinguished
unaries (\(\Holant^+\)), to the two unaries fixing a bit to 0 or 1
(\(\Holant^c\)).  Later work treated any signature set containing a
nonzero odd-arity signature (\(\Holant^{\mathrm{odd}}\)).
\Cref{tab:intro-boolean-holant-history} separates these assumptions from
restrictions on symmetry and weights, using the same dash convention as
\cref{tab:intro-gh-csp-history}.

	\begin{table}[!htbp]
		\centering
		\caption{Development of Boolean-domain Holant dichotomies on general
			graphs.}
		\label{tab:intro-boolean-holant-history}
		\small
		\setlength{\tabcolsep}{3pt}
		\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.18}
		\begin{tabularx}{\textwidth}{@{}l
				>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X
				>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X
				>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X@{}}
			\multicolumn{4}{@{}l}{\textit{(a) Symmetric signature sets}}\\[0.2em]
			\toprule
			Weight range & \(\Holant^*\) & \(\Holant^c\) & Unrestricted \(\Holant\) \\
			\midrule
			\(\mathbb R_{\ge0}\) & -- & -- & -- \\
			\(\mathbb R\) & -- & \cite{CaiLuXia2009}
			& \cite{HuangLu2016} \\
			\(\mathbb C\) & \cite{CaiLuXia2009}
			& \cite{CaiHuangLu2012}
			& \cite{CaiGuoWilliams2016} \\
			\bottomrule
		\end{tabularx}
		
		\medskip
		\begin{tabularx}{\textwidth}{@{}l
				>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X
				>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X
				>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X
				>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X
				>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X@{}}
			\multicolumn{6}{@{}l}{\textit{(b) Arbitrary signature sets}}\\[0.2em]
			\toprule
			Weight range & \(\Holant^*\) & \(\Holant^+\) & \(\Holant^c\) &
			\(\Holant^{\mathrm{odd}}\) & Unrestricted \(\Holant\) \\
			\midrule
			\(\mathbb R_{\ge0}\) & -- & -- & -- & --
			& \cite{LinWang2018} \\
			\(\mathbb R\) & -- & --
			& \cite{CaiLuXia2018RealHolantc}
			& \cite{CaiFuShao2020Entanglement}
			& \cite{ShaoCai2020} \\
			\(\mathbb C\)
			& \cite{CaiLuXia2011}
			& \cite{Backens2017}
			& \cite{Backens2021,Backens2025}
			& \cite{MengWangXiaZheng2025,GuanShaoShi2026}
			& This paper \\
			\bottomrule
		\end{tabularx}
	\end{table}

Without auxiliary unary signatures, dichotomies were obtained for
symmetric complex-valued signatures \cite{CaiGuoWilliams2016}, arbitrary
nonnegative signatures \cite{LinWang2018}, and arbitrary real-valued
signatures \cite{ShaoCai2020}.

In 2025, Meng, Wang, and Xia proved an \(\FP^{\mathsf{NP}}\) versus
\(\SharpP\)-hard dichotomy for weighted Eulerian orientations
\cite{MengWangXia2025EO}.  Meng, Wang, Xia, and Zheng combined this result
with tensor decomposition to obtain the same complexity bounds for complex
Boolean Holant with a nonzero odd-arity signature
\cite{MengWangXiaZheng2025}.  In 2026, Guan, Shao, and Shi gave a
polynomial-time algorithm based on linear programming for the former
\(\FP^{\mathsf{NP}}\) cases, yielding \(\FP\) versus \(\SharpP\)-hard
dichotomies for both problems \cite{GuanShaoShi2026}.

These results direct attention to the all-even case.  Every gadget built
from even-arity signatures has even arity, since each internal edge consumes
two inputs.  Thus no such gadget supplies a unary signature.  Binary
signatures become the smallest positive-arity objects available for further
constructions.  Xia proposed a framework organized by the finite groups
associated with binary signatures, analyzing several branches in which all
realizable nonzero quaternary signatures decompose \cite{Xia2026Framework}.
Liu and Meng then classified complex Boolean Holant with binary
disequality available, for arbitrary finite sets of algebraic
complex-valued signatures, and gave an explicit decidable tractability
criterion \cite{Liu2026DEQ2Holant}.

Our proof removes the prescribed-binary assumption in the all-even case.
It combines the binary and quaternary dichotomies of Liu and Meng with a
decomposition based on Hamming imbalance.  This decomposition produces
signatures of controlled support whose arity determines the remaining
cases, as described in \cref{subsec:proof-sketch}.  Together with the
odd-arity dichotomy, it completes the unrestricted complex-valued entry in
\cref{tab:intro-boolean-holant-history}.

\paragraph{Independent work.}
Huang and Fu established a dichotomy for Holant problems with a single
complex-valued quaternary signature \cite{HuangFu2026FourRegular}.
Guan, Shao, and Tang proved a dichotomy for complex-valued Boolean Holant
problems whose signature sets are closed under complex conjugation
\cite{GuanShaoTang2026ConjugateClosed}.
Zhou proved a dichotomy for finite sets of algebraic complex-valued Boolean
signatures of even arity whose nonzero binary gadget signatures are
nonsingular and whose projective matrix classes form the cyclic group
\(C_2\) of order two under series composition \cite{ZhouC2Holant}.

We were also informed of the following independent work before the
public release of the present paper.
Jiayi Zheng informed us of unpublished work on the complex-valued Holant
dichotomy in the case where the projective binary transfer classes form an
odd dihedral group.
Mingji Xia informed us of work concerning the twisted Klein-group case
and the \(C_1\) case, under the condition that all quaternary signatures
are reducible.
The results and proofs in the present paper were developed independently
of these works.

\subsection{Our result: the full dichotomy}
\label{subsec:intro-main}

The predicate \(\Tract(\cF)\), defined in \cref{sec:predicate}, is an
equivalent formulation of the seven conditions in the odd-arity dichotomy
of Meng, Wang, Xia, and Zheng
\cite[conference version, Theorem~30; full version, Theorem~33]{MengWangXiaZheng2025}.
Our result applies this criterion without an odd-arity assumption.
Each condition concerns the signature set as a whole.  In each transformability
case, one basis must work simultaneously for the edge and every
member of \(\cF\).

	\begin{theorem}[Full dichotomy theorem]
		\label{thm:main}
		Let \(\cF\) be a finite set of algebraic complex-valued Boolean signatures,
		given in the exact algebraic representation fixed in
		\cref{subsec:complexity-conventions}.  The predicate \(\Tract(\cF)\)
		is decidable, and the following complexity classification holds:
		\[
		\begin{aligned}
			\Tract(\cF)
			&\quad\Longrightarrow\quad \Holant(\cF)\in\FP,\\
			\neg\Tract(\cF)
			&\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
			\Holant(\cF)\text{ is }\SharpP\text{-hard}
		\end{aligned}
		\]
		under polynomial-time Turing reductions.
	\end{theorem}

For evaluation and hardness reductions, \(\cF\) is fixed and running time
is measured in the encoded instance size. For recognition, \(\cF\) itself
is the input.
The proof of \cref{thm:main} is completed in \cref{sec:assembly}, where
\cref{thm:K-structural} is transferred back to ordinary coordinates.

\subsection{Proof organization}
\label{subsec:proof-sketch}

We first apply the known odd-arity dichotomy.  For the all-even case,
we reduce to known classifications or construct a \emph{mountain}, whose
height determines the remaining analysis.
An input \({\mathbf x}\) to an arity-\(r\) signature has \emph{half-charge}
\(\chi_r({\mathbf x})=\wt({\mathbf x})-r/2\), where \(\wt({\mathbf x})\) is its number of 1-bits.
In the \(K\)-basis, edges carry disequality, so the vertex charges in a
closed instance sum to zero.  If all supported charges are nonnegative,
or all are nonpositive, only the central Hamming layers contribute, and
the weighted Eulerian-orientation classification applies.  That
classification also handles sets in which each signature is supported on
a single Hamming layer, meaning inputs of one fixed weight.

The remaining set is \emph{multilayer}: some signature has nonzero entries at
different Hamming weights.  It is also \emph{two-sided}: positive and
negative half-charges both occur, possibly in different signatures.
We extract tensor factors and handle the cases covered by known
classifications.  For the remaining set, let \(d\) be the gcd of its
nonzero supported half-charges.  The construction in \cref{lem:charge-bridge}
first uses tensor
products and self-loops to obtain two signatures, one nonzero at its
all-zero input and the other at its all-one input.  We combine these
endpoint properties by further connections, or obtain unary pins and
apply the odd-arity dichotomy.  In the first case, the output is a
\emph{mountain of height \(d\)}: an arity-\(2d\) signature with both
endpoint coefficients nonzero and all other support on the weight-\(d\)
layer.  Every supported half-charge in the current set remains divisible
by \(d\), a condition used in the higher-height arguments.

At height one, the mountain is binary.  We use interpolation and finite
projective groups to analyze binary gadgets.  Binary equality is handled
by Liu and Meng's dichotomy \cite[Theorem~1.2]{Liu2026DEQ2Holant}.
The remaining groups are odd cyclic or odd dihedral.  Contraction analysis
and a terminating sequence of signature additions settle these two cases.

Height two is resolved by the eight-vertex dichotomy and factor extraction.
Height three uses self-loop and two-copy constructions and computer-assisted
exhaustive enumeration.

For the remaining cases at heights at least four, we normalize the
mountain and reconstruct its central restriction from its self-loop
contractions.  At heights at least five, the recovered matching structure
yields a hard two-copy gadget.  Height four has an exceptional
Reed--Muller structure, which we handle by a reduction adding binary
equality.  Some base cases in the odd-dihedral and higher-height arguments
depend on exact enumeration and solver checks.

Sections~\ref{sec:preliminaries}--\ref{sec:factor-charge} present the basic
conventions, tractability criterion, auxiliary reductions, and mountain
construction, respectively.
Sections~\ref{sec:binary-dynamics}--\ref{sec:height-at-least-four} treat
the height cases, \cref{sec:synthesis} completes the main proof, and
\cref{sec:conclusion} concludes.
Appendix~\ref{sec:exact-finite-certificates} records the finite
calculations and their verification.

\subsection{Related work}
\label{subsec:related-work}

The historical progression above concerns exact counting on general
graphs.  Further work studies recognition of the tractable cases, larger
domains, specific vertex models, and planar restrictions.

The last cell of \cref{tab:intro-gh-csp-history} distinguishes a
classification for each fixed signature set from an algorithm that decides
whether the tractability criterion holds.  Cai and Chen's 2017 theorem expressed tractability
through three conditions on an unbounded generated family of partial sums
\cite{CaiChen2017}.  Liu and Meng subsequently gave a total exact algorithm
deciding these conditions from a finite set of algebraic complex-valued
constraints.  They proved that Block Orthogonality implies the other two
conditions and also decided the corresponding degree-multiple
counting-CSP criterion \cite{LiuMeng2026CSPDecidability}.

For Holant on larger domains, classifications are available under further
restrictions on the signatures.  A domain-three theorem treated one
symmetric ternary signature with all unary signatures available
\cite{CaiLuXia2013Domain3}. Its journal version appeared in 2026
\cite{CaiLuXia2026Domain3}.  Subsequent classifications covered special
edge-colouring signatures and restricted domain-three and domain-four
settings \cite{CaiGuoWilliams2016EdgeColoring,LiuFanCai2025}.  In 2025,
Cai and Ihm proved a dichotomy for real-valued symmetric signatures on
domain three with all unary signatures available, allowing an arbitrary
finite signature set \cite{CaiIhm2025}.

More specific Boolean models include a single ternary signature on
3-regular graphs, the six-vertex and eight-vertex models, and weighted
Eulerian orientations with arrow-reversal symmetry
\cite{YangHuangFu2024,CaiFuXia2018,CaiFu2023,CaiFuShao2020ARS}.

	Restricting the input graph to be planar can make additional counting
	problems tractable.  For Boolean counting CSP, Cai, Lu, and Xia classified
	real-valued symmetric constraints \cite{CaiLuXia2017PlanarCSP}.
	Guo and Williams extended the classification to symmetric constraints with
	complex weights \cite{GuoWilliams2020PlanarCSP}, and Cai and Fu removed the
	symmetry assumption \cite{CaiFu2022PlanarCSP}.  In this planar model, the
	incidence graph is embedded in the plane and the argument order follows
	the cyclic order around each constraint vertex.  Every case that is hard
	on general graphs but tractable in this planar setting admits a holographic
	reduction to matchgates.  These reductions evaluate the partition function
	through weighted perfect-matching sums computed by the FKT algorithm.
	
	Graph homomorphism problems are a subclass of counting CSP, so these
	Boolean-domain results also classify planar graph homomorphisms with
	complex symmetric weight matrices.  For larger domains, Cai and Maran
	proved a dichotomy for real symmetric \(3\times3\) matrices in 2023
	\cite{CaiMaran2023PlanarGH}.  Their 2024 preprint classified nonnegative
	real symmetric \(4\times4\) matrices of full rank
	\cite{CaiMaran2024PlanarGH}.  In a 2026 preprint, Cai, Maran, and Young
	obtained a dichotomy for nonnegative real symmetric matrices of arbitrary
	finite size whose diagonal entries are pairwise distinct
	\cite{CaiMaranYoung2026}.
	
	For planar Holant with symmetric complex-valued Boolean signatures,
	Cai, Fu, Guo, and Williams gave a classification that includes tractable
	cases beyond holographic reductions to the FKT algorithm
	\cite{CaiFuGuoWilliams2022PlanarHolant}.  In 2026, Cai, Fan, Shao, and Tang
	classified the eight-vertex model into three cases: tractable on general
	graphs, hard on general graphs but tractable on planar graphs, and hard
	even on planar graphs \cite{CaiFanShaoTang2026}.
	
	\section{Preliminaries}
\label{sec:preliminaries}

\subsection{Basic notation}
\label{subsec:foundational-algebra}
\label{subsec:graph-code-walsh-terminology}

We use \(\mathbb Q,\mathbb R\),
and \(\mathbb C\) for the rationals, reals, and complex numbers, and write
\[
\AlgNums:=\{\alpha\in\mathbb C:p(\alpha)=0
\text{ for some }0\ne p\in\mathbb Q[t]\},
\qquad
\Ftwo:=\mathbb Z/2\mathbb Z
\]
for the algebraic complex numbers and the two-element field.
For a field \(F\), put \(F^\times=F\setminus\{0\}\). For a finite set
\(V\), \(F^V\) denotes the functions \(V\to F\).
We write \([m]=\{1,\ldots,m\}\), with \([0]=\varnothing\), and
\(\Mat_{m\times n}(F)\) for matrices over \(F\), abbreviated to
\(\Mat_n(F)\) when \(m=n\).  Transposes are denoted by \(M^{\mathsf T}\)
and do not include complex conjugation.  The group of invertible matrices
is \(\GL_n(F)\), and \(I_n\) is its identity.
For a vector space \(V\) over \(F\), tensor products use the convention
\(V^{\otimes0}=F\), with empty product \(1\).

Vector symbols are bold, while scalar coordinates and variable or vertex labels
are not.  For a Boolean vector \({\mathbf x}\in\Ftwo^V\), put
\(\wt({\mathbf x})=|\{v:x_v=1\}|\).  A Hamming layer fixes \(\wt({\mathbf x})\), and parity
means \(\wt({\mathbf x})\bmod2\).  All binary-vector arithmetic is over \(\Ftwo\).
We use \(\oplus\) for coordinatewise addition and \(\bar {\mathbf x}\) for bitwise complement.  For \({\mathbf y}\in\Ftwo^V\), the dot product
\({\mathbf x}\cdot {\mathbf y}=\sum_{v\in V}x_vy_v\) is taken in \(\Ftwo\).
Binary strings on ordered index sets are ordered lexicographically with
\(0<1\).  For \(A\subseteq V\), \({\mathbf x}_{\widehat A}\) denotes the restriction
of \({\mathbf x}\) to \(V\setminus A\), in the inherited order when \(V\) is ordered.
In particular, \({\mathbf x}_{\widehat{pq}}\) deletes positions \(p,q\).

\subsection{Signatures and Holant instances}
\label{subsec:holant-definitions}

For an integer \(r\ge0\), an arity-\(r\)
	Boolean signature is a
function \(f:\{0,1\}^r\to\AlgNums\). Its truth table, listed as a column
vector in lexicographic order, identifies it with a tensor in
\((\AlgNums^2)^{\otimes r}\). Its variables have labelled positions whose
order is part of the signature. We
	write \(\arity(f)=r\) and \(\supp(f)=\{{\mathbf x}:f({\mathbf x})\ne0\}\), and use
	\(\one[\mathsf P]\) for the indicator of a proposition \(\mathsf P\).
	A signature set is \emph{all-even} when each member has even
arity. The terms nullary, unary, binary, ternary, and quaternary mean
arity \(0,1,2,3,4\), respectively.
	For an ordered binary signature \(B\), we identify \(B\) with the matrix
	\((B(a,b))_{a,b\in\Ftwo}\), with rows indexed by \(a\) and columns by \(b\).

The symbols \(\zero^r\) and \(\one^r\) denote the all-zero and all-one
strings. At \(r=0\), both denote the unique empty string.  More generally,
\(\zero^V,\one^V\in\Ftwo^V\) are the constant vectors on a finite index set
\(V\).  The values \(f(\zero^r)\) and \(f(\one^r)\) are the two
\emph{endpoint coefficients}.  For \(T\subseteq[r]\),
\(\one_T\in\{0,1\}^r\) is the incidence vector of \(T\), so that
\(\one_\varnothing=\zero^r\).  We write \({\mathbf e}_j=\one_{\{j\}}\) for the
\(j\)-th standard basis vector when the ambient arity is clear.

Graphs may have parallel edges and loops. Each loop contributes two to
\(\deg_G(v)\).  A perfect matching of a finite set \(V\) is a partition
into pairs.  The mate of a vertex is the other endpoint of its matching
edge.  We write \(K_n\) for the complete graph and \(\binom Vk\) for the
\(k\)-element subsets of \(V\).

For a signature set \(\cF\), a signature grid over \(\cF\) is a finite
	multigraph \(G=(V,E)\),
	together with a signature \(f_v\in\cF\) satisfying
	\(\arity(f_v)=\deg_G(v)\) and an ordering of the incident
	edge-ends at every vertex.  Let \(\mathbf x_v\) list the local variables at \(v\) in this order.
	An edge assignment \(\sigma:E\to\{0,1\}\) induces the string
	\(\left.\mathbf x_v\right|_\sigma\) by assigning each variable the bit on its edge.
	The two ends of a loop receive the same edge bit.
	For the resulting grid \(\Omega\),
	\begin{equation*}
		\Holant_\Omega
		=
		\sum_{\sigma:E\to\{0,1\}}
		\prod_{v\in V}
		f_v(\left.\mathbf x_v\right|_\sigma).
	\end{equation*}
	The problem \(\Holant(\cF)\) asks for this value on an input grid over
	\(\cF\).
	Throughout, comma-separated signature or signature-set arguments mean adding
	their union. For example, \(\Holant(\cF,a,b)\) abbreviates
	\(\Holant(\cF\cup\{a,b\})\).

We use the unary pinnings
	\[
	\DeltaZero=[1,0]^{\mathsf T},
	\qquad
	\DeltaOne=[0,1]^{\mathsf T},
	\]
	and the binary equality and disequality tensors
	\[
	I=I_2=(\Eq_2)=
	\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\0&1\end{pmatrix},
	\qquad
	X=(\Neq_2)=
	\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\1&0\end{pmatrix}.
	\]
	We also fix the sign and skew matrices
	\[
	Z=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\0&-1\end{pmatrix},
	\qquad
	Y=ZX=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\-1&0\end{pmatrix}.
	\]
	More generally, for \(r\ge1\), let
	\[
	(\Eq_r)(x_1,\ldots,x_r)=\one[x_1=\cdots=x_r].
	\]
	For signature sets \(\cG\) and \(\cF\), the bipartite problem
	\(\Holant(\cG\mid\cF)\) is defined on a bipartite graph
	\(H=(U,V,E)\): vertices in the left part \(U\) are assigned signatures
	from \(\cG\), and vertices in the right part \(V\) are assigned signatures
	from \(\cF\).

Subdividing every ordinary edge by an \(\Eq_2\)-vertex preserves the
partition value, as does suppressing these degree-two equality vertices.
Under this correspondence between instances, we identify
\[
\Holant(\cF)=\Holant(\Eq_2\mid\cF).
\]

For an integer \(r\ge0\) and \({\mathbf a}\in\{0,1\}^r\), define the
\emph{indicator signature} \(\delta_{\mathbf a}\) by
	\[
	\delta_{\mathbf a}({\mathbf x})=\one[{\mathbf x}={\mathbf a}].
	\]

\subsection{Zero and nullary preprocessing}
\label{subsec:zero-nullary}

An instance containing an identically zero signature has value zero.
A nullary signature is a scalar: delete its vertex and multiply that scalar
back into the final value.  The empty residual instance has value \(1\).
After this preprocessing, we assume that the input signature set \(\cF\)
contains neither zero nor nullary signatures.  Intermediate contractions
may still be zero or nullary and are treated as specified in each argument.

\subsection{Complexity and exact-input conventions}
\label{subsec:complexity-conventions}

We treat counting problems as functions from finite encoded instances to
exact values.  We use \emph{tractable} to mean membership in \(\FP\), the
functions computable exactly in deterministic polynomial time.
The class \(\SharpP\) consists of functions counting accepting paths of
nondeterministic polynomial-time Turing machines.  A possibly complex-valued counting
problem is \emph{hard} if it is \(\SharpP\)-hard under polynomial-time
Turing reductions.

For counting problems \(A,B\), write \(A\leT B\) if an algorithm computes
\(A\) using polynomially many exact evaluations of \(B\) on instances of
polynomial size, with polynomial time for all other work.  This time bound
includes any known nonzero algebraic rescaling and exact algebraic
postprocessing.  Write \(A\equivT B\) when reductions exist in both
directions.  The notation \(\FP^{\mathsf{NP}}\) permits an \(\mathsf{NP}\)
decision oracle.

We fix an \emph{exact algebraic encoding} of \(\alpha\in\AlgNums\) by its
monic irreducible polynomial over \(\mathbb Q\) and an isolating rectangle
in \(\mathbb C\): its vertices have rational real and imaginary parts,
and it contains \(\alpha\) and no other complex root of the polynomial.
Equality, zero tests, conjugation, and field arithmetic are exact.
A \emph{number field} is a finite extension \(L/\mathbb Q\), and
\([L:\mathbb Q]\) is its dimension as a \(\mathbb Q\)-vector space.

For evaluation of \(\Holant(\cF)\), the signature set \(\cF\) is fixed,
and running time is measured in the encoded instance size.  For recognition,
the input is a finite list of signatures given by exact algebraic entries.
No polynomial-time bound
in the length of this list is asserted.  

\subsection{Tensor operations and projective notation}
\label{subsec:algebra-group-terminology}

For an integer \(r\ge0\), let \(S_r\) denote the symmetric group on
	\([r]\).  For an arity-\(r\) signature \(f\) and \(\tau\in S_r\),
	\((\tau f)({\mathbf x})=f(x_{\tau^{-1}(1)},\ldots,x_{\tau^{-1}(r)})\).  For signatures
	\(f:\Ftwo^r\to\AlgNums\) and \(g:\Ftwo^s\to\AlgNums\) on disjoint ordered
	variables, and \({\mathbf x}\in\Ftwo^r,{\mathbf y}\in\Ftwo^s\), define
	\((f\otimes g)({\mathbf x},{\mathbf y})=f({\mathbf x})g({\mathbf y})\).
	The signature \(f\) is \emph{symmetric} when \(\tau f=f\) for every
	\(\tau\in S_r\), equivalently when \(f({\mathbf x})\) depends only on \(\wt({\mathbf x})\).

For \({\mathbf b}\in\Ftwo^r\), the bit-flip operation sends \(f\) to
\(f^{\oplus {\mathbf b}}({\mathbf x})=f({\mathbf x}\oplus {\mathbf b})\).  Bit-flip/variable-permutation orbits use
these flips and permutations of argument positions.
A nonzero tensor has rank one when it is a product of one vector on each
of its specified factors. For two factors this is matrix rank one.
We say \(a\) divides \(f\), with cofactor \(b\), when
\(f=a\otimes b\) up to variable permutation and nonzero scalar redistribution.
Every tensor is taken to divide the zero tensor.  A proper tensor factor
has positive arity strictly smaller than its parent.

A nonzero positive-arity signature is \emph{irreducible} if, up to variable permutation and nonzero scalar, it cannot
	be written as \(a\otimes b\) with both \(a\) and \(b\) of positive arity.
	A nonzero positive-arity signature that is not irreducible is
	\emph{reducible}.
	Factorization into irreducibles is unique up to these operations and factor order
	\cite[full version, Lemma~2.13]{CaiFuShao2020ARS}.  For any class \(\cC\) of
	signatures, its tensor closure \(\angles{\cC}\) consists of tensor products
	of members of \(\cC\), up to variable permutation and nonzero scalar.  By
	convention, it also contains zero and the arity-zero empty product.

For an integer \(r\ge1\) and \({\mathbf a}\in\{0,1\}^r\), a
\emph{complementary-pair signature} (\(\CP\)) is a signature
\begin{equation*}
    c_0\delta_{\mathbf a}+c_1\delta_{\bar {\mathbf a}},
    \qquad c_0,c_1\in\AlgNums^\times.
\end{equation*}
When \({\mathbf a}=\zero^r\), it is a \emph{generalized equality} (\(\GE\)).
As a tensor identity, individual bit flips turn a CP signature into a GE signature.
The notation \(\diag(p,q)\) denotes the \(2\)-by-\(2\) diagonal matrix with diagonal entries \(p,q\). For \(t\in\AlgNums^\times\), put \(D(t):=\diag(1,t)\).  For \(p,q\in\AlgNums\), in arity two,
\(p\delta_{00}+q\delta_{11}=\diag(p,q)\) is a \emph{weighted binary
equality}, and \(p\delta_{01}+q\delta_{10}\) is a \emph{weighted binary
disequality}.  Either is nonsingular when \(pq\ne0\).

The notation \(\Span S\) denotes linear span.  An affine subspace is a
	translate \({\mathbf a}+L\) of a linear subspace. Its translation space is \(L\), and an
	affine plane has dimension two.

For a nonzero vector \(\mathbf v\) over a field \(F\), write
\([\mathbf v]=F^\times\mathbf v\) for its projective class.  We likewise
write \([A]=F^\times A\) for a nonzero tensor or matrix \(A\).  In particular,
\(\PGL_n(F)=\GL_n(F)/(F^\times I_n)\).
For tensors or matrices \(A,B\) over \(\AlgNums\), write \(A\doteq B\)
when \(A=\lambda B\) for some \(\lambda\in\AlgNums^\times\).
In gadget constructions we suppress overall nonzero algebraic factors.
Their contribution to a Holant value can be removed by exact algebraic
postprocessing.  This convention does not suppress relative coefficients
in sums of signatures.
Angle brackets denote generated subgroup in a group context and tensor closure for a signature class.

\subsection{Holographic transformations and the
  \texorpdfstring{\(K\)}{K}-basis}
\label{subsec:holographic}

In \(\Holant(\cG\mid\cF)\), represent each left-side signature \(g\)
of arity \(r\) as a row vector and each right-side signature \(f\) of
arity \(s\) as a column vector, both in lexicographic order.  For a fixed
\(M\in\GL_2(\AlgNums)\), replace them by
\[
gM^{\otimes r},\qquad (M^{-1})^{\otimes s}f,
\]
respectively.  The matrices on the two ends of each edge cancel by
\(MM^{-1}=I\), so this replacement preserves the value of every instance.
Write \(gM:=gM^{\otimes r}\) for the left-side action and
\(Mf:=M^{\otimes s}f\) for the right-side action, and put
\[
\cG M:=\{gM:g\in\cG\},\qquad M\cF:=\{Mf:f\in\cF\}.
\]
The value-preserving transformation in both directions gives
\[
\Holant(\cG\mid\cF)\equivT\Holant(\cG M\mid M^{-1}\cF).
\]
For ordinary Holant, take \(\cG=\{\Eq_2\}\).  The transformed row vector
\((1,0,0,1)M^{\otimes2}\) represents the binary signature with matrix
\(M^{\mathsf T}M\), since its \((a,b)\)-entry is
\(\sum_{x\in\Ftwo}M_{xa}M_{xb}\).  Thus
\[
\Holant(\cF)=\Holant(\Eq_2\mid\cF)
\equivT\Holant(M^{\mathsf T}M\mid M^{-1}\cF).
\]

Fix the K-coordinate matrix
	\begin{equation}
		K=\frac1{\sqrt2}
		\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\ i&-i\end{pmatrix},
		\qquad
		K^{-1}=\frac1{\sqrt2}
		\begin{pmatrix}1&-i\\1&i\end{pmatrix}.
		\label{eq:K-matrix}
	\end{equation}
	Since \(K^{\mathsf T}K=X\), putting
	\(\widehat{\cF}=K^{-1}\cF\) gives
	\begin{equation}
		\Holant(\cF)
		\equivT
		\Holant(X\mid\widehat{\cF})
		=:\KHolant(\widehat{\cF}).
		\label{eq:K-equivalence}
	\end{equation}
	For an original-coordinate signature \(f\), we likewise write
	\(\widehat f=K^{-1}f\). The hat records passage to \(K\)-coordinates.
	Once we work with a signature set already in \(K\)-coordinates, we suppress
	these hats and denote its member signatures simply by \(f\).  Unless stated
	otherwise, signatures and signature sets appearing in a K-Holant problem
	are understood to be in \(K\)-coordinates, with edge \(X\).

Write \(Z_X(\Omega)\) for the partition function of a closed grid
	whose edges carry \(X\).  To describe simultaneous changes of basis
	that preserve this edge up to scalar, define the general orthogonal
	similitude group of \(X\) by
	\begin{equation*}
		GO(X)
		=
		\{D\in\GL_2(\AlgNums):D^{\mathsf T}XD=\lambda X
		\text{ for some }\lambda\in\AlgNums^\times\}.
	\end{equation*}
	If \(E(\Omega)\) is the edge set,
	\(D\Omega\) applies \(D\) at every vertex, and
	\(D^{\mathsf T}XD=\lambda X\), then
	\begin{equation}
		Z_X(D\Omega)=\lambda^{|E(\Omega)|}Z_X(\Omega).
		\label{eq:GOX-global-scalar}
	\end{equation}
	The known edge-count scalar gives a polynomial-time equivalence.  This
	operation transforms the whole signature set. It does not by itself
	realize \(D\) as a local gadget.

\subsection{Gadgets and reductions}
\label{subsec:gadget-proof-vocabulary}
\label{subsec:reduction-conventions}

A gadget is a finite network over the allowed signatures, with ordered,
labelled dangling edges.  Its signature is obtained by summing over the
internal variables, including the edge signatures in the product.  Edges
