carry equality in ordinary coordinates and disequality in \(K\)-coordinates.
A \emph{direct} construction uses one fixed gadget.  Below we work
in \(K\)-coordinates.

A slice fixes some arguments of a tensor. Implementing it by pinning
requires the corresponding pinning signatures. Interpolation gives an
oracle reduction by solving a linear system from exact evaluations.
Formal tensor operations make a signature available only when a gadget or
a stated reduction supplies it. Whole-set changes of basis act on the
problem as described in \cref{subsec:holographic}.

Using bit flips in a gadget or reduction requires available signatures or
a stated whole-set transformation. Algebraic support analysis may also
use the relabellings defined above.

\paragraph{Equivalence convention.}
Let \(\cF\) be the current finite signature set.  Whenever a fixed
gadget construction, polynomial-time interpolation, or a cited reduction
supplies a finite set \(S\) such that
\begin{equation}
\KHolant(\cF\cup S)\leT\KHolant(\cF),
\label{eq:finite-equivalent-enlargement}
\end{equation}
the reverse reduction is immediate by restriction.  We call the signatures
in \(S\) \emph{available by reduction over \(\cF\)}.  This does not assert
membership in the original set or direct gadget realizability.
When an argument replaces the current set by an equivalent set and retains
the notation \(\cF\), that replacement is stated explicitly.
The same convention applies to ordinary Holant when
\(\Holant(\cF\cup S)\leT\Holant(\cF)\).
A use that depends on local topology or variable order still requires an
explicit direct realization over the stated signature set.  Overall nonzero
scalars are handled as in \cref{subsec:algebra-group-terminology}.
These are the gadget and interpolation conventions of
\cite[Section~2.5]{Liu2026DEQ2Holant} and
\cite[Section~2.3]{MengWangXiaZheng2025}.

If a direct \(X\)-edge gadget over \(\cF\) realizes \(h\), replacing
each vertex carrying \(h\) by that gadget gives
\begin{equation}
\KHolant(\cF\cup\{h\})\leT\KHolant(\cF).
\label{eq:direct-gadget-reduction}
\end{equation}
A fixed gadget has constant size.  If it realizes \(\rho h\), with
\(\rho\in\AlgNums^\times\), an instance with \(N\) occurrences is
rescaled by \(\rho^{-N}\).  Gadget replacement is thus one case
of \cref{eq:finite-equivalent-enlargement}.

Let \(f\) have arity \(r\).  For an ordered pair of distinct variables
	\(p=(i,j)\), a binary kernel
	\(C:\Ftwo^2\to\AlgNums\), and a residual string \({\mathbf z}\) indexed by
	\([r]\setminus\{i,j\}\) in increasing variable order, let
	\({\mathbf z}^{i\leftarrow a,j\leftarrow b}\) be the length-\(r\) string obtained by
	inserting \(a,b\) at positions \(i,j\).  Define the contraction of two variables by
	\begin{equation*}
		(\partial_p^C f)({\mathbf z})
		=
		\sum_{a,b\in\Ftwo}C(a,b)
		f\bigl({\mathbf z}^{i\leftarrow a,j\leftarrow b}\bigr).
	\end{equation*}
We also write \(\partial_{ij}^C f=\partial_{(i,j)}^C f\).

In the notation of Shao--Cai
	\cite[full version, Section~2.4]{ShaoCai2020}, their
	\(\widehat\partial_{ij}\widehat f\) and \(\partial_{ij}f\) are,
	respectively, our \(\partial_{(i,j)}^X\widehat f\) and
	\(\partial_{(i,j)}^I f\).

Let an ordered binary signature \(B\) be directly realized over the current
signature set.  Connecting its two inputs through \(X\)-edges to variables
\(i,j\) of \(f\), in that order, gives the contraction kernel
\(C=XBX\), with \(C(a,b)=B(1-a,1-b)\).  Swapping the inputs of \(B\)
gives the kernel \(XB^{\mathsf T}X\).

We also write
	\(f_{ij}^{ab}({\mathbf z})=f({\mathbf z}^{i\leftarrow a,j\leftarrow b})\) for the residual
	slice.  If an ordered binary \(B\) is placed on the ordered variable pair
	\(e=(u,v)\), we write \(B_e\).

For tensors \(A,B\) on the same ordered variables, define the bilinear
coefficient pairing
\[
\langle A,B\rangle=\sum_{\mathbf x}A({\mathbf x})B({\mathbf x}),
\]
without conjugation. For binary matrices this is the Frobenius pairing
\(\langle U,V\rangle=\operatorname{tr}(U^{\mathsf T}V)\).
Two tensors are \emph{orthogonal} for this pairing when it is zero.

For any \(T\in\Mat_2(\AlgNums)\), define
\[
T^\#:=XT^{\mathsf T}X.
\]
For an ordered binary signature \(B\), its \emph{transfer} is \(T_B:=BX\).
Swapping the two inputs of \(B\) transposes its matrix, so the resulting
transfer is
\[
T_{B^{\mathsf T}}=B^{\mathsf T}X=X(T_B)^{\mathsf T}X=(T_B)^\#.
\]
Joining the second input of \(B\) to the first input of an ordered binary
signature \(C\) through an \(X\)-edge gives \(BXC\).  Hence
\[
T_{BXC}=(BXC)X=(BX)(CX)=T_BT_C.
\]
For all \(S,T\in\Mat_2(\AlgNums)\), we have
\((ST)^\#=T^\#S^\#\) and \((T^\#)^\#=T\).
For \(T\in\GL_2(\AlgNums)\), the \emph{projective order} of \([T]\)
is the least \(m\ge1\) such that \(T^m=\lambda I\) for some
\(\lambda\in\AlgNums^\times\), and is infinite if no such \(m\) exists.

	\section{The Tractability Criterion}
	\label{sec:predicate}
	
The criterion in \cref{thm:main} is a disjunction of seven conditions on
the whole signature set.  The first two use support restrictions and EO
signatures, the next two use tensor decompositions, and the last three
require a common change of basis for the edge and all vertex signatures.
We define these conditions and then state their decidability and
polynomial-time evaluation result.  The hardness direction is proved in
\cref{sec:assembly}.

	\subsection{Charge and Hamming layers}
	\label{subsec:charge}
	
	All charge terminology in this subsection is in \(K\)-coordinates.
	For an integer \(r\ge0\), define
	the doubled-charge function \(q_r:\Ftwo^r\to\mathbb Z\) by
	\[
	q_r({\mathbf x})=2\wt({\mathbf x})-r.
	\]
	For an even integer \(r\), we also use the integer half-charge
	\[
	\chi_r({\mathbf x})=\frac{q_r({\mathbf x})}2=\wt({\mathbf x})-\frac r2.
	\]
	
	Put
	\[
	HW_r^{\ge}=\{{\mathbf x}\in\Ftwo^r:q_r({\mathbf x})\ge0\},
	\quad
	HW_r^{\le}=\{{\mathbf x}\in\Ftwo^r:q_r({\mathbf x})\le0\},
	\quad
	HW_r^{=}=\{{\mathbf x}\in\Ftwo^r:q_r({\mathbf x})=0\},
	\]
	and define \(HW_r^{>},HW_r^{<}\) by strict inequalities.  We omit the
	subscript when the arity is clear.  A signature has one of these properties when its support lies in the
	corresponding set.  A signature is \emph{single-layer} when its support lies
	in one Hamming layer. A signature set is single-layer when each member is so
	supported, with member-dependent layers allowed, and is \emph{multilayer}
	otherwise.  A signature set is \emph{common
		one-sided} when all members are \(HW^{\ge}\) or all are \(HW^{\le}\).
	Otherwise it is \emph{two-sided}, equivalently both charge signs occur among
	support strings of its members.  Here \(\EO\) abbreviates Eulerian orientation.  An
	arity-\(2d\) \emph{EO signature} is supported in \(HW_{2d}^=\). This central
	support condition is the local balance constraint for an Eulerian
	orientation.
	
	For a closed \(X\)-edge grid \(\Omega\) with vertex set \(V(\Omega)\), every edge
	contributes one \(0\)-bit and one \(1\)-bit.  Hence each contributing
	assignment \({\mathbf x}\), with induced local string \({\mathbf x}_v\) at vertex \(v\), satisfies
	\begin{equation*}
		\sum_{v\in V(\Omega)}q_{\arity(f_v)}({\mathbf x}_v)=0.
	\end{equation*}
	For a gadget with dangling edges, the same cancellation occurs on internal
\(X\)-edges: the sum of local charges equals the charge of the external
string.  This is the charge conservation used in contractions.  In a closed
instance over a common one-sided set, all local charges have the same weak
sign and sum to zero.  Hence every contributing assignment uses only
central-layer entries. Setting all other entries to zero therefore
preserves the Holant value.
	
	\begin{remark}[Notation relative to prior work]
		\label{rem:mwxz-notation}
		We follow \cite[Sections~2.1--2.3]{MengWangXiaZheng2025} in writing
		\(\widehat{\cF}=K^{-1}\cF\), \(HW^{\ge}\), \(HW^{\le}\), \(HW^=\),
		and \emph{EO signature}, as well as their equality and disequality
        notation \(\Eq_r\) and \(\Neq_2\).
		
	\end{remark}
	
	\subsection{The standard signature classes}
	\label{subsec:standard-classes}
	
	Zero signatures are included whenever their arity is permitted by the
	class.  The affine class
	\(\cA\) consists of the zero signature and the nonzero arity-\(r\) signatures
	\begin{equation}
		f({\mathbf x})=\lambda\,\one[A{\mathbf x}={\mathbf b}]\,i^{Q({\mathbf x})},
		\label{eq:affine-class}
	\end{equation}
	where \(r,m\ge0\) are integers,
	\(\lambda\in\AlgNums^\times\),
	\(A\in\Mat_{m\times r}(\Ftwo)\), \({\mathbf b}\in\Ftwo^m\), and
	\(Q:\Ftwo^r\to\mathbb Z_4\), where
	\(\mathbb Z_4=\mathbb Z/4\mathbb Z\), is represented by a multilinear
	polynomial over \(\mathbb Z_4\) of degree at most two whose mixed
	coefficients are even.
	Equivalently, the phase is a product of fourth-root phases of affine linear
	forms interpreted as \(\{0,1\}\)-valued integers.
	Here a \emph{mixed coefficient} is the coefficient of \(x_ix_j\) for
	\(i\ne j\), and an affine linear form over \(\Ftwo\) is
	\(a_0+\sum_{j=1}^r a_jx_j\) with all coefficients in \(\Ftwo\).
	
	The product class \(\cP\) consists of zero and all signatures of the form
	\(\lambda\prod_{j=1}^m h_j\), where \(m\ge0\) is an integer,
	\(0\ne\lambda\in\AlgNums\), and every factor
	\(h_j\) is a unary, binary equality, or binary disequality constraint applied
	to specified signature variables.  This is a pointwise product of
	constraints that may share variables, whereas tensor products use disjoint
	variables.  Empty products and repeated variables are allowed. An inconsistent
	product denotes the zero signature.
	
	Put \(\zeta_8:=e^{\pi i/4}=(1+i)/\sqrt2\).
	The local-affine class \(\cL\) consists of the signatures \(f\) such that
	\begin{equation*}
		\left(\bigotimes_{j=1}^{\arity(f)}D(\zeta_8)^{\alpha_j}\right)f\in\cA
		\qquad\text{for every }{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\in\supp(f).
	\end{equation*}
	Each support assignment \({\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\) specifies a phase change at every
	variable, and the condition tests all of these choices on the original support.
	
	Let \(\cT\) be the set of all unary and binary signatures, and let
	\begin{equation*}
		\cM=\{f:\text{\(f\) is a Boolean signature and }
		f({\mathbf x})=0\text{ for every \({\mathbf x}\) with }\wt({\mathbf x})>1\}.
	\end{equation*}
	


	\begin{definition}[Transformability]
		\label{def:common-transformability}
		\label{def:common-K-presentation}
		Let \(\cC\) be closed under nonzero scalars and variable permutations.  A
		finite signature set \(\cF\) is \(\cC\)-transformable if one
		\(M\in\GL_2(\AlgNums)\) satisfies
		\begin{equation*}
			M^{\mathsf T}M\in\cC,\qquad M^{-1}\cF\subseteq\cC.
		\end{equation*}
		Equivalently, for \(\widehat{\cF}=K^{-1}\cF\), one matrix
		\(U=K^{-1}M\) satisfies
		\begin{equation*}
			U^{\mathsf T}XU\in\cC,\qquad U^{-1}\widehat{\cF}\subseteq\cC.
		\end{equation*}
		Thus one matrix must satisfy the edge condition and the conditions for
		all signatures.  The tractability criterion uses this definition for \(\cC\in\{\cA,\cP,\cL\}\).
	\end{definition}

The equivalence in \cref{def:common-transformability} follows directly
from \(K^{\mathsf T}K=X\).  For \(U=K^{-1}M\),
\[
U^{\mathsf T}XU=M^{\mathsf T}M,
\qquad U^{-1}\widehat{\cF}=M^{-1}\cF.
\]
Conversely, \(M=KU\), which proves the equivalence.

	
	\subsection{The Eulerian-orientation predicate}
	\label{subsec:eo-predicate}
	
	The first two tractable cases use two different operations on
	signatures in \(K\)-coordinates.  The central restriction keeps the arity
	and retains only the zero-charge entries.  For an arity-\(r\) signature
	\(g\), define
	\begin{equation*}
		g|_{\EO}({\mathbf x})=g({\mathbf x})\one[q_r({\mathbf x})=0].
	\end{equation*}
	It is zero for odd \(r\), and zero restrictions are deleted.  For a
	signature set \(\cF\), set
	\begin{equation*}
		\cF|_{\EO}=\{g|_{\EO}:g\in\cF,\ g|_{\EO}\ne0\}.
	\end{equation*}
	  When applying the
	central restriction to a closed instance over a common one-sided set,
	an occurrence with zero central restriction makes the instance value
	zero.  The deletion above is a convention for the signature set.
	
	Let \(g\ne0\) have arity \(k\) and be supported on the single Hamming
	layer of weight \(d\).  To balance its charge while retaining its
	coefficients, its \emph{EO completion} appends pinning signatures in the
	following order:
	\begin{equation}
		g_{\to\EO}
		=
		\begin{cases}
			g\otimes\DeltaZero^{\otimes(2d-k)},
			& 2d\ge k,\\
			g\otimes\DeltaOne^{\otimes(k-2d)},
			& 2d<k.
		\end{cases}
		\label{eq:eo-completion}
	\end{equation}
	The original variables precede the added unaries. A zeroth power is the nullary
	unit.  The completed signature has even arity and central support, hence is
	EO\@.  For a signature set of nonzero single-layer signatures, write
	\begin{equation*}
		\cF_{\to\EO}=\{g_{\to\EO}:g\in\cF\}.
	\end{equation*}
  In the
evaluation reduction, a component whose fixed local charges do not sum
to zero contributes zero.  Otherwise, the appended zero and one variables are paired by
\(X\)-edges within each charge-balanced component, giving a unique
extension of each original assignment
\cite[Section~6.2, proof of Corollary~68]{MengWangXia2025EO}.  This construction uses
the explicit completion tensors in an auxiliary EO instance. It requires
no assumption that the input signature set realizes the added pinnings.
	
	For arity-\(2d\) EO signature \(g\), an oriented perfect pairing
	\(\pi=((a_1,b_1),\ldots,(a_d,b_d))\) has unordered pairs partitioning
	\([2d]\).  For \({\mathbf z}\in\Ftwo^d\), let \({\mathbf y}\in\Ftwo^{2d}\) be the unique string
	with \(y_{a_j}=z_j\) and \(y_{b_j}=1-z_j\). The pairing induces
	\begin{equation*}
		g^\pi(z_1,\ldots,z_d)=g({\mathbf y}).
	\end{equation*}
	
	\begin{definition}[The predicate \(\EOTract\)]
		\label{def:eo-tract}
		For a finite EO signature set \(\cF\), \(\EOTract(\cF)\) holds if there exist a
		sign \(\varepsilon\in\{+1,-1\}\) and a class
		\(\cC\in\{\cA,\cP\}\), both shared by the entire signature set, such that:
		\begin{enumerate}
			\item for every \(g\in\cF\) and all
			\({\boldsymbol{\alpha}},{\boldsymbol{\beta}},{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\in\supp(g)\), if
			\({\boldsymbol{\delta}}={\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\oplus{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\oplus{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\), then
			\({\boldsymbol{\delta}}\in\supp(g)\) or
			\(\varepsilon q_{\arity(g)}({\boldsymbol{\delta}})>0\).
			\item for every \(g\in\cF\) and every oriented perfect pairing \(\pi\)
			of its variables, \(g^\pi\in\cC\).
		\end{enumerate}
        In particular, \(\EOTract(\varnothing)\) holds.
	\end{definition}
	
The common-sign condition follows \cite[Theorem~26]{MengWangXia2025EO}.
The compressed signature \(g^\pi\) expresses the opposite-pair restriction
used to define the affine and product-type EO classes in
\cite[Definition~22]{MengWangXia2024TypicalEO}.  To compare the two
forms, reorder the original variables as
\((a_1,\ldots,a_d,b_1,\ldots,b_d)\), with assignments \(({\mathbf z},{\mathbf w})\).
The full restriction, extended by zero away from opposite pairs, is
\[
R_\pi g({\mathbf z},{\mathbf w}):=g({\mathbf z},{\mathbf w})\prod_{j=1}^dX(z_j,w_j)
=g^\pi({\mathbf z})\prod_{j=1}^dX(z_j,w_j).
\]
For \(\cC\in\{\cA,\cP\}\), adding these disequality factors gives
\(g^\pi\in\cC\Rightarrow R_\pi g\in\cC\).  Conversely,
\(\sum_{{\mathbf w}\in\Ftwo^d}R_\pi g({\mathbf z},{\mathbf w})=g^\pi({\mathbf z})\): each factor
\(X(z_j,w_j)\) admits exactly one value of \(w_j\).
The affine and product normal forms are preserved under this elimination,
so \(R_\pi g\in\cC\Rightarrow g^\pi\in\cC\), as detailed in
\cite[Section~2.10.2]{Liu2026DEQ2Holant}.
Reversing a pair flips the corresponding compressed variable, and
reordering pairs permutes the compressed variables.  Both operations
preserve \(\cA\) and \(\cP\), so using oriented pairings retains the
source condition and its single shared class.

	\subsection{The tractability predicate}
	\label{subsec:tract-predicate}
	
	For an arbitrary finite signature set, evaluate \(\Tract\) after the
	zero and nullary preprocessing of \cref{subsec:zero-nullary}.
	For a preprocessed signature set \(\cF\), put
	\(\widehat{\cF}=K^{-1}\cF\).  Define \(\Tract(\cF)\) to be the disjunction
	of the following seven alternatives, which may overlap.  The first two
test the \(K\)-coordinate set \(\widehat{\cF}\). The remaining five are
stated for the original-coordinate set \(\cF\).
Transformability and the EO condition are defined in
\cref{def:common-transformability,def:eo-tract}, respectively.
	
	\begin{enumerate}[label=\textnormal{(\arabic*)}]
		\item \label{case:one-sided}
		The signature set \(\widehat{\cF}\) is common one-sided, that is,
		\[
		[\,\forall\widehat f\in\widehat{\cF},\
		\supp(\widehat f)\subseteq HW_{\arity(\widehat f)}^{\ge}\,]
		\quad\text{or}\quad
		[\,\forall\widehat f\in\widehat{\cF},\
		\supp(\widehat f)\subseteq HW_{\arity(\widehat f)}^{\le}\,],
		\]
		and \(\EOTract(\widehat{\cF}|_{\EO})\) holds.
		
		\item \label{case:single-weight}
		Every \(\widehat f\in\widehat{\cF}\) is supported on one Hamming
		weight \(d_f\).  Across the signature set, some support string has negative
		charge and some (possibly other) support string has positive charge, and
		\(\EOTract(\widehat{\cF}_{\to\EO})\) holds, with the completion
		operation defined in \cref{eq:eo-completion}.
		
		\item \label{case:arity-two}
		\(\cF\subseteq\angles{\cT}\).
		
		\item \label{case:matching}
		\(\cF\subseteq\angles{K\cM}\), or
		\(\cF\subseteq\angles{KX\cM}\).
		
		\item \label{case:affine}
		\(\cF\) is \(\cA\)-transformable.
		
		\item \label{case:product}
		\(\cF\) is \(\cP\)-transformable.
		
		\item \label{case:local-affine}
		\(\cF\) is \(\cL\)-transformable.
	\end{enumerate}
	
In \cref{case:matching}, one of the two inclusions must hold for the
entire signature set.

	\begin{remark}
        For a nonzero positive-arity signature \(f\),
		\[
		\begin{aligned}
			f\in\angles{K\cM}
			&\Longleftrightarrow
			\text{ every irreducible factor of }K^{-1}f\text{ lies in }\cM,\\
			f\in\angles{KX\cM}
			&\Longleftrightarrow
			\text{ every irreducible factor of }XK^{-1}f\text{ lies in }\cM.
		\end{aligned}
		\]
		Zero and nullary signatures are covered by the tensor-closure
		conventions of \cref{subsec:algebra-group-terminology} and by the
		preprocessing in \cref{subsec:zero-nullary}.
	\end{remark}
	
	\subsection{Decidability and polynomial-time evaluation}
	\label{subsec:predicate-algorithms}

	
	\begin{theorem}[Recognition and evaluation]
		\label{thm:predicate-algorithms}
		For every finite algebraic signature set \(\cF\), the predicate \(\Tract(\cF)\),
		with the preprocessing convention of \cref{subsec:zero-nullary}, is
		decidable.  If it holds, then \(\Holant(\cF)\in\FP\).
	\end{theorem}
	
	\begin{proof}
		This is \cite[Theorem~2.22]{Liu2026DEQ2Holant}.
	\end{proof}
	
	
	\section{Auxiliary Reductions}
	\label{sec:mountain-terminals}
	
The height arguments use the reductions in this section to turn particular
gadget outputs into a classification of the whole signature set.  Factor
extraction and binary interpolation supply additional signatures, while
access to a nonzero unary gives a dichotomy.  The EO and equality results
then handle the corresponding outputs of the height constructions.
Availability by reduction is understood as in
\cref{eq:finite-equivalent-enlargement}.

	\subsection{Factor Extraction and Preservation of Tractability}
\label{subsec:dichotomy-decomposition-tools}
\label{sec:retained-normalization}

The support alternatives of \cref{subsec:tract-predicate} determine when
factors may be added to the current signature set.  We first state the EO
classification needed for the common-one-sided exception, then record factor
extraction and preservation of the tractability criterion under the reductions.
\begin{theorem}[Weighted Eulerian orientations
		{\cite[Theorem~26]{MengWangXia2025EO}, \cite[Theorem~1.1]{GuanShaoShi2026}}]
		\label{thm:external-eo}
		For every finite algebraic EO signature set \(\cF\), if \(\EOTract(\cF)\) holds,
		then \(\KHolant(\cF)\in\FP\). Otherwise
		\(\KHolant(\cF)\) is \(\SharpP\)-hard.
	\end{theorem}
	
	\begin{theorem}[Tensor-factor extraction
		{\cite[full version, Theorem~32 and Remark~39; conference version,
			Theorem~29]{MengWangXiaZheng2025}}]
		\label{thm:external-factor}
		Let \(\cF\) be a finite signature set and let
		\(h=a\otimes b\) be a nonzero tensor product.  Unless
		\(\cF\cup\{h\}\) is common one-sided,
		\[
		\KHolant(\cF,a,b)\equivT\KHolant(\cF,h).
		\]
	\end{theorem}

In the common-one-sided exception, \(\KHolant(\cF,h)\) is in \(\FP\)
		when \(\EOTract((\cF\cup\{h\})|_{\EO})\) holds and is
		\(\SharpP\)-hard otherwise, by charge conservation and
		\cref{thm:external-eo}.
	
	\subsubsection{Irreducible factors}
	\label{subsec:factor-saturation}

The common-one-sided case is already classified by \cref{thm:external-eo}.
In the remaining case, \cref{thm:external-factor} permits tensor-factor
extraction, so the analysis of individual signatures reduces to their
irreducible factors.  The iterations below also retain previously
constructed signatures.  For those arguments, we use the following finite
closure.
	
	For a preprocessed finite signature set \(\cF\), choose an exact
	irreducible factorization of every member and let \(\Pi(\cF)\) be the
	finite set of all irreducible factors, with their ordered variable blocks.  We call
	\(\cF\) \emph{factor-closed} when \(\Pi(\cF)\subseteq\cF\).
	
	\begin{lemma}[Factor closure]
		\label{lem:factor-saturation}
		If \(\cF\) is not common one-sided, then
		\begin{equation}
			\KHolant(\cF)
			\equivT
			\KHolant(\cF\cup\Pi(\cF)).
			\label{eq:factor-saturation}
		\end{equation}
	\end{lemma}
	
	\begin{proof}
		Iterate \cref{thm:external-factor}, retaining the original set at each
		step so that the common-one-sided exception cannot occur.
		This is the argument of \cite[Lemma~2.23]{Liu2026DEQ2Holant}.
	\end{proof}

Repeated factorization terminates because every proper factor has smaller
arity than its parent.  Each original signature can be reconstructed as
the tensor product of its factors, with the recorded scalar and variable
order.  Substituting these products in an instance therefore gives
\[
\KHolant(\cF)\equivT\KHolant(\Pi(\cF))
\]
outside the common-one-sided case.  Thus initial preprocessing may replace
the signature set by its irreducible factors, still denoted by \(\cF\).
This assumption applies to the initial set: later gadget outputs may be
reducible and must be processed again.

Some later iterations retain both earlier signatures and their factors.
When such an iteration requires factor closure, we instead use
\(\cF\cup\Pi(\cF)\), retaining \(\cF\) for the current set after this
explicit replacement.
	
	\subsubsection{Taking subsets and changing basis}

The next two lemmas transfer the tractability criterion through taking
subsets and through a common change of basis.
	
	\begin{lemma}[Preservation under taking subsets]
		\label{lem:downward-inheritance}
		If \(\cF\subseteq\cF'\) are preprocessed signature sets and
		\(\Tract(K\cF')\) holds, then \(\Tract(K\cF)\) holds.
	\end{lemma}
	
	\begin{proof}
		This is \cite[Lemma~2.24]{Liu2026DEQ2Holant}.
	\end{proof}
	
	\begin{lemma}[\(GO(X)\)-invariance of tractability]
		\label{lem:GOX-tract-invariance}
		Let \(\cF\) be a finite preprocessed signature set and let
		\(D\in GO(X)\).  Then
		\begin{equation*}
			\Tract(K\cF)
			\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
			\Tract\bigl(K(D\cF)\bigr).
		\end{equation*}
	\end{lemma}
	
	\begin{proof}
		This is \cite[Lemma~2.25]{Liu2026DEQ2Holant}.
	\end{proof}
	
	
	\subsection{Binary Interpolation}
	\label{proofsubsec:central-descent}

For an ordered binary \(B\), the transfer \(T_B=BX\) records composition
along \(X\)-edges, as in \cref{subsec:reduction-conventions}.
The lemma below has two ways to obtain a unary: the singular case uses
factor extraction, while the infinite-order case first supplies a rank-one
binary by interpolation.  Both conclusions are reductions over the
original signature set.
	
	\begin{lemma}[Binary interpolation]
		\label{lem:binary-interpolation}
		Let \(\cF\) be a finite algebraic signature set whose supported doubled
		charges include both signs, let
		\(0\ne B\in\cF\) be ordered binary, with transfer \(T_B=BX\).
		\begin{enumerate}
			\item If \(B=u\otimes v\) is singular, where \(u,v\ne0\), then
			\[
			\KHolant(\cF,u,v)\leT\KHolant(\cF).
			\]
			\item If \(T_B\) is nonsingular and has infinite projective order, there is
			a nonzero rank-one binary \(H\) such that
			\[
			\KHolant(\cF,H)\leT\KHolant(\cF).
			\]
		\end{enumerate}
	\end{lemma}
	
	\begin{proof}
		This is \cite[Lemma~3.1]{Liu2026DEQ2Holant}.
	\end{proof}
	
	\subsection{Single-occurrence Reductions and Nonzero Unaries}
	\label{subsec:single-occurrence-carriers}

	\begin{theorem}[Odd-arity Holant
		{\cite[full version, Theorem~33; conference version, Theorem~30]{MengWangXiaZheng2025}}]
		\label{thm:external-odd}
		Let \(\cF\) be a finite preprocessed algebraic signature set containing an
		odd-arity signature.  Then \(\Holant(\cF)\in\FP\) if
		\(\Tract(\cF)\) holds, and is \(\SharpP\)-hard otherwise.
	\end{theorem}
	
	Its first two alternatives were originally in \(\FP^{\mathsf{NP}}\). The
	stated \(\FP\) conclusion follows from
	\cite[Theorem~1.1 and Corollary~1.2]{GuanShaoShi2026}.
	
	
	
The single-occurrence replication argument below originated in an
expanded version of \cite{MengWangXiaZheng2025} that is not publicly
available.  The version we use is also stated in
\cite[Lemma~3.3]{Liu2026DEQ2Holant}. We reproduce it with its ordered
variable connections explicit.

If a nonzero odd-arity signature is realized or made available by a
reduction, this dichotomy settles that case.  The same holds in
\(K\)-coordinates by \cref{eq:K-equivalence}.  After zero and nullary
preprocessing, the remaining analysis may therefore assume that every
signature in \(\cF\) has even arity.  In particular, there are no unaries.
	Write
	\[
	\Delta=\DeltaZero\otimes\DeltaOne,
	\]
	with the displayed order of its two variables.  For a signature \(g\), the
	superscripts \(g^{=1}\) and \(g^{\leq1}\) mean that an oracle instance contains,
	respectively, exactly one or at most one \(g\)-vertex.
	
	\begin{lemma}[Reduction with at most one \(\Delta\)-vertex]
		\label{lem:universal-directed-pair}
		For every fixed finite algebraic signature set \(\cF\),
		\[
		\KHolant(\cF,\Delta^{\leq1})
		\leT
		\KHolant(\cF).
		\]
	\end{lemma}
	
	\begin{proof}
		This is \cite[Lemma~3.2]{Liu2026DEQ2Holant}.
	\end{proof}
	
The next lemma turns one-occurrence access to \(g\) into unrestricted
access to \(h\).  Each copy of a fixed gadget propagates the boundary
signature \(g\) and exposes an output block with signature \(h\).
A fixed nonzero evaluation then closes
the retained end of the chain, as illustrated in
\cref{fig:single-occurrence-chain}.
The lemma applies separately to ordinary Holant and to K-Holant.

	\begin{lemma}[Single-occurrence replication]
		\label{lem:single-occurrence-replication}
		Let \(\cF\) be a fixed finite algebraic signature set, and let \(g\) and
		\(h\) be nonzero signatures of arities \(r\) and \(s\), respectively.  For ordinary Holant,
		all connections below use \(\Eq_2\).  Suppose that
		\begin{enumerate}
			\item \(\Holant(\cF,g^{=1})\leT\Holant(\cF)\).
			\item there is a fixed gadget \(C\) over \(\cF\) whose dangling edges are
			the three disjoint ordered blocks
			\[
			A=(a_1,\ldots,a_r),\qquad
			P=(p_1,\ldots,p_r),\qquad
			Y=(y_1,\ldots,y_s).
			\]
			Connecting one \(g\)-vertex coordinatewise to \(A\) realizes
			\[
			\rho\,g(P)h(Y)
			\]
			on \(P,Y\), for a known \(\rho\in\AlgNums^\times\).  Moreover, for
			consecutive copies \(C_i,C_{i+1}\), the block \(P_i\) can be connected
			coordinatewise to \(A_{i+1}\), with no other connections.
			Thus a chain of \(k\) copies, beginning with one \(g\)-vertex,
			contains exactly that one \(g\)-vertex and realizes
			\[
			\rho^k g(P_k)\prod_{i=1}^k h(Y_i),
			\]
			where the blocks \(Y_1,\ldots,Y_k\) are pairwise disjoint.
			\item there is a fixed instance \(J\) over \(\cF\cup\{g\}\), containing
			exactly one \(g\)-vertex, whose exactly computable partition value
			\(Z(J)\) is nonzero.
		\end{enumerate}
		Then
		\[
		\Holant(\cF,h)\leT\Holant(\cF).
		\]
		For K-Holant, if all connections use \(X\) and assumption~1 is
		\(\KHolant(\cF,g^{=1})\leT\KHolant(\cF)\), then the same
		gadget and nonzero-evaluation hypotheses give
		\[
		\KHolant(\cF,h)\leT\KHolant(\cF).
		\]
	\end{lemma}
	
	\begin{proof}
		This is \cite[Lemma~3.3]{Liu2026DEQ2Holant}.  An input with no \(h\)-vertex
		is already an instance over \(\cF\).  Let \(\Omega\) be an input with
		\(k\ge1\) occurrences of \(h\).  Delete those vertices, retain their incident
		edges as \(k\) ordered boundary blocks, and attach the blocks
		\(Y_1,\ldots,Y_k\) of the displayed chain coordinatewise in the corresponding
		orders.  Let \(J^\circ\) be the network obtained by deleting the
		unique \(g\)-vertex of \(J\), and join its boundary coordinatewise to
		\(P_k\) in the variable order inherited from that removed vertex.  Denote the
		resulting instance by \(\Omega'\).  It contains exactly one
		\(g\)-vertex, and the two tensor identities give
		\[
		Z(\Omega')=\rho^k Z(J)Z(\Omega).
		\]
		Divide by the known nonzero scalar \(\rho^kZ(J)\) and apply assumption~1.
The gadgets \(C\) and \(J\) and the scalars \(\rho\) and \(Z(J)\)
are fixed.  The replacement uses \(k\) copies of \(C\), so its size
grows linearly with the number of replaced vertices, and the scalar
recovery uses exact algebraic arithmetic.
	\end{proof}
	
\begin{figure}[htbp]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[font=\small,box/.style={draw,minimum width=10mm,minimum height=8mm}]
\node[draw,circle,minimum size=7mm] (g) at (0,0) {$g$};
\node[box] (c1) at (1.65,0) {$C_1$};
\node (dots) at (3.15,0) {$\cdots$};
\node[box] (ck) at (4.65,0) {$C_k$};
\node[box] (j) at (6.55,0) {$J^\circ$};
\draw (g) -- node[above] {$A_1$} (c1);
\draw (c1) -- node[above] {$P_1$} (dots);
\draw (dots) -- node[above] {$A_k$} (ck);
\draw (ck) -- node[above] {$P_k$} (j);
\draw (c1.south) -- node[right] {$Y_1$} (1.65,-1.1);
\draw (ck.south) -- node[right] {$Y_k$} (4.65,-1.1);
\node at (3.15,-1.1) {$\cdots$};
\node[align=center] at (3.15,-1.65)
  {ordered boundary blocks of the removed \(h\)-vertices};
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{The replacement in \cref{lem:single-occurrence-replication}.
Each horizontal connection represents \(r\) edges in the specified
variable order. Each \(Y_i\) represents \(s\) edges attached to the original
boundary of one removed \(h\)-vertex.  The network contains only the one
displayed \(g\)-vertex.  Here \(J^\circ\) is obtained by removing the
unique \(g\)-vertex from \(J\).}
\label{fig:single-occurrence-chain}
\end{figure}

	\begin{lemma}[Dichotomy with a nonzero unary]
		\label{lem:common-unary-exit}
		Let \(\cF\) be a finite algebraic signature set, and let
		\(u\ne0\) be unary.  If \(u\in\cF\), then
		\(\Holant(\cF)\) is \(\SharpP\)-hard unless \(\Tract(\cF)\) holds, and
		\(\KHolant(\cF)\) is \(\SharpP\)-hard unless \(\Tract(K\cF)\) holds.
	\end{lemma}
	
	\begin{proof}
		The ordinary-coordinate assertion is the unary case of
		\cref{thm:external-odd}.  For the K-coordinate assertion, apply the
		same theorem after the transformation in \cref{eq:K-equivalence},
		which preserves the arity and nonzeroness of the unary signature.
		This is the argument recorded in \cite[Lemma~3.4]{Liu2026DEQ2Holant}.
	\end{proof}
	
	\begin{corollary}[Higher equality supplies unaries]
		\label{cor:higher-equality-unary}
		Let \(d\ge4\), and let \(\cF\) be a finite algebraic signature set
		containing \((\Eq_d)\).  Then
		\[
		\begin{aligned}
		\Holant(\cF\cup\{\DeltaZero,\DeltaOne\})&\leT\Holant(\cF),\\
		\KHolant(\cF\cup\{\DeltaZero,\DeltaOne\})&\leT\KHolant(\cF).
		\end{aligned}
		\]
	\end{corollary}
	
	\begin{proof}
		Both reductions follow from \cite[Corollary~3.6]{Liu2026DEQ2Holant}.
		Restriction supplies the reverse reductions.
	\end{proof}

Together with \cref{lem:common-unary-exit}, these reductions settle the
		complexity of the original problem in either setting.
	
	\subsection{Hardness from an Eulerian-orientation Signature}
	\label{subsec:hc-relative-eo}

The height constructions produce EO signatures inside a set that may
also contain non-EO signatures. We show that, under the assumptions stated
before \cref{thm:hc-relative-eo-terminal}, an EO signature outside the
matching-product class defined below forces hardness.

	All gadget connections in this subsection use the edge \(X\).

	Arrow-reversal symmetry (ARS) means
	\(h({\mathbf x})=\overline{h(\bar {\mathbf x})}\) for every input \({\mathbf x}\).
	An EO signature \(h\) has \emph{projective arrow-reversal symmetry}
	(projective ARS) if, for some \(c\in\AlgNums^\times\),
	\[
	(c^{-1}h)({\mathbf x})=\overline{(c^{-1}h)(\bar {\mathbf x})}
	\qquad\text{for every \({\mathbf x}\in\{0,1\}^{\arity(h)}\)}.
	\]
	Here \(\bar {\mathbf x}\) is bitwise complement and the overline on the coefficient is
	complex conjugation.  An EO--ARS binary is both EO and ARS without rescaling.

	Let \(M\) be a perfect matching of a finite variable set \(V\).
	After choosing an orientation of every edge, write \(\vec M\) for
	the resulting set of ordered pairs.  A nonzero \emph{matching product} on
	\(V\) is a function \(f:\Ftwo^V\to\AlgNums\) of the form
	\[
	f({\mathbf x})=\lambda\prod_{(u,v)\in\vec M}b_{uv}(x_u,x_v),
	\qquad
	\lambda\in\AlgNums^\times,
	\quad b_{uv}:\Ftwo^2\to\AlgNums.
	\]

	Let \(\mathcal B_{\rm ARS}\) consist of the nonzero scalar multiples of
	products on arbitrary perfect matchings, with an orientation chosen for each
	matched pair, of binary EO--ARS signatures
	\begin{equation}
		(0,a,\overline a,0),\qquad a\in\AlgNums^\times.
		\label{eq:hc-rel-B}
	\end{equation}
	We write
	\((\Neq_4)=\delta_{0011}+\delta_{1100}\).
	Nonzero scalars and permutations of the output variables are suppressed according to the
	equivalence convention.  The required matching-product factorization constrains both the support and the coefficients.

We first record how fixed direct gadgets over a subset apply to the ambient signature set.
	
	\begin{lemma}[Replacing vertices by fixed gadgets in a larger signature set]
		\label{lem:ambient-eo-lifting}
		Let \(\cF_0\subseteq\cF\) be finite algebraic signature sets in
		K-coordinates.  Suppose that a finite sequence of fixed gadget constructions starting from
		\(\cF_0\) adds a finite set \(S\) using only fixed direct \(X\)-gadgets.
		Then the same derivation is valid over \(\cF\), and
		\[
		\KHolant(\cF\cup S)\leT\KHolant(\cF).
		\]
		If, at some stage, the problem \(\KHolant(\cF_0')\), with
		\(\cF_0'\subseteq\cF_0\cup S\), is \(\SharpP\)-hard, then
		\(\KHolant(\cF)\) is \(\SharpP\)-hard.
	\end{lemma}
	
	\begin{proof}
		Each direct gadget is substituted independently at every occurrence of its
		output signature, leaving vertices labelled from \(\cF\setminus\cF_0\)
		unchanged.  Induction over the finite sequence of fixed gadget constructions proves the displayed
		reduction.  For the final claim, restriction and that reduction give
		\[
		\KHolant(\cF_0')
		\leT\KHolant(\cF\cup S)
		\leT\KHolant(\cF).
		\]
        The lemma applies to the fixed direct gadgets in a literature proof.
        Tensor-factor extraction is supplied separately by \cref{thm:external-factor}.
	\end{proof}
	
	The common-one-sided case and the tractable cases have already been handled.
For the following theorem and
\cref{lem:hc-rel-complementary-unary,lem:hc-rel-mwx,lem:hc-rel-cfs},
fix a finite preprocessed algebraic set \(\cF\) of even-arity signatures in
\(K\)-coordinates satisfying:
	\begin{enumerate}[label=\textnormal{(\roman*)},leftmargin=*,labelindent=0pt]
		\item Among the values \(\chi_{\arity(f)}({\mathbf x})\), with \(f\in\cF\)
		and \({\mathbf x}\in\supp(f)\), at least one is positive and at least one
		is negative.
		\item \(\neg\Tract(K\cF)\).
	\end{enumerate}
Finite enlargements retain witnesses of both charge signs, so (i) persists.
By \cref{lem:downward-inheritance}, (ii) also persists under enlargement.
A nonzero unary then gives hardness by \cref{lem:common-unary-exit}.

	\begin{theorem}[Hardness from an EO signature outside \(\mathcal B_{\rm ARS}\)]
		\label{thm:hc-relative-eo-terminal}
		Assume (i)--(ii). Let \(h\in\cF\) be an EO signature.  Suppose there are no
		\(\lambda\in\AlgNums^\times\), perfect matching \(P_h\) of its variables
		together with an orientation \((i,j)\) of each matched pair, and coefficients
		\(a_{ij}\in\AlgNums^\times\) such that
		\[
		h=\lambda
		\bigotimes_{(i,j)\in P_h}(0,a_{ij},\overline{a_{ij}},0)_{ij}.
		\]
		Then \(\KHolant(\cF)\) is \(\SharpP\)-hard.
	\end{theorem}
	
The proof first obtains projective ARS or hardness, then proves hardness when the signature satisfying projective ARS lies outside \(\mathcal B_{\rm ARS}\).

	\begin{lemma}[Quaternary EO CP signatures supply a unary]
		\label{lem:hc-rel-complementary-unary}
		Under the assumptions preceding \cref{thm:hc-relative-eo-terminal},
		suppose \(D\in\cF\), where
		\[
		D=p\delta_{\mathbf s}+q\delta_{\bar {\mathbf s}},
		\qquad
		{\mathbf s}\in\{0,1\}^4,\quad \wt({\mathbf s})=2,\quad p,q\in\AlgNums^\times.
		\]
		Then there is a nonzero unary \(u\) such that
		\[
		\KHolant(\cF,u)\leT\KHolant(\cF).
		\]
	\end{lemma}
	
	\begin{proof}
		First reduce \(D\) to the unweighted quaternary CP signature.  After a
		variable permutation take \({\mathbf s}=0101\), and connect the same last two variable
		positions of two copies through \(X\)-edges.  The resulting ordered
		quaternary is
		\[
		\begin{aligned}
			Q(x_1,x_2,y_1,y_2)
			&=\sum_{u,v\in\Ftwo}
			D(x_1,x_2,u,v)D(y_1,y_2,1-u,1-v)\\
			&=pq\bigl(\delta_{0110}+\delta_{1001}\bigr).
		\end{aligned}
		\]
		Thus, in output order \((x_1,y_2,x_2,y_1)\), the output is the nonzero
		scalar multiple \(pq\,(\Neq_4)\). Hence \((\Neq_4)\) is directly
		realizable over \(\cF\), up to the displayed nonzero scalar.
		
		Put \(E=(\Neq_4)\).  Joining the fourth variables of
		\(E(a_1,a_2,a_3,u)\) and \(E(b_1,b_2,b_3,v)\) through \(X(u,v)\)
		realizes, in output order \((a_1,a_2,b_3,a_3,b_1,b_2)\), the six-ary
		generalized disequality
		\[
		\delta_{000111}+\delta_{111000}.
		\]
		Attach the \(\DeltaZero\)-variable of one \(\Delta\)-vertex through
		\(X\) to \(a_1\), and its \(\DeltaOne\)-variable through \(X\) to \(a_3\).
		This selects the string \(111000\). In remaining-variable order
		\((b_1,a_2,b_2,b_3)\), the output is
		\(\delta_{0101}=\Delta\otimes\Delta\).  In the notation of
		\cref{lem:single-occurrence-replication}, delete the unique
		\(\Delta\)-vertex to obtain a gadget with input block \(A=(a_1,a_3)\)
		and output blocks \(P=(b_1,a_2)\), \(Y=(b_2,b_3)\).
		Connecting a \(\Delta\)-vertex to \(A\) realizes \(\Delta(P)\Delta(Y)\).
		Connect \(P\) of each copy to \(A\) of the next, in the displayed orders,
		with no other connections.  A chain of \(k\) copies therefore
		uses exactly one \(\Delta\)-vertex and realizes
		\(\Delta(P_k)\prod_{i=1}^k\Delta(Y_i)\) on pairwise disjoint output blocks.
		Closing a single \(\Delta\)-vertex by
		\(X\) has value one.  The reductions in
		\cref{lem:universal-directed-pair,lem:single-occurrence-replication}
		therefore give
		\[
		\KHolant(\cF,\Delta)\leT\KHolant(\cF).
		\]
		
		Now use \(\Delta=\DeltaZero\otimes\DeltaOne\).
		Since both charge signs occur, the common-one-sided exception in
		\cref{thm:external-factor} is impossible.  Factor extraction and the
		preceding reduction give
		\[
		\KHolant(\cF,\DeltaZero,\DeltaOne)\leT\KHolant(\cF).\qedhere
		\]
	\end{proof}
	
	Write \(\mu_m=\{\omega\in\AlgNums:\omega^m=1\}\) for \(m\ge1\).

	\begin{lemma}[Projective ARS or hardness]
		\label{lem:hc-rel-mwx}
		Under the assumptions preceding \cref{thm:hc-relative-eo-terminal}, let \(h\in\cF\) be an
		EO signature.  Then either there is a
		\(c\in\AlgNums^\times\) such that
		\[
		(c^{-1}h)({\mathbf x})=\overline{(c^{-1}h)(\bar {\mathbf x})}
		\qquad({\mathbf x}\in\{0,1\}^{\arity(h)}),
		\]
		or \(\KHolant(\cF)\) is \(\SharpP\)-hard.
	\end{lemma}
	
	\begin{proof}
		We first show that projective ARS can be witnessed by an algebraic
		scalar.  Suppose an algebraic signature \(h_0\) is
		ARS after multiplication by an arbitrary nonzero complex scalar.  Then, for
		some \(\rho\in\mathbb C^\times\),
		\[
		h_0({\mathbf x})=\rho\,\overline{h_0(\bar {\mathbf x})}.
		\]
		Choose \({\mathbf x}\) with \(h_0({\mathbf x})\ne0\).  The same identity implies
		\(h_0(\bar {\mathbf x})\ne0\), and hence
		\[
		\rho=\frac{h_0({\mathbf x})}{\overline{h_0(\bar {\mathbf x})}}\in\AlgNums^\times,
		\qquad \rho\overline\rho=1.
		\]
		Set \(c=1+\rho\) if \(\rho\ne-1\), and set \(c=i\) if
		\(\rho=-1\).  Then \(c\in\AlgNums^\times\) and
        \(c/\overline c=\rho\), so \(c^{-1}h_0\) is ARS with the required
        algebraic scalar.
		
		We next handle the binary case.  Write \(h=(0,a,b,0)\).  If \(ab=0\), then \(h\) is a
		nonzero singular binary, so \cref{lem:binary-interpolation} and factor
		extraction give a nonzero unary.  By \(\neg\Tract(K\cF)\) and
		\cref{lem:common-unary-exit}, this is hard.  Suppose \(ab\ne0\), and put
		\[
		T_h=hX=\operatorname{diag}(a,b).
		\]
		If \(T_h\) has infinite projective order, \cref{lem:binary-interpolation}
		first gives a rank-one binary. Applying its singular case and factor
		extraction again gives a unary and hence hardness.  Otherwise \(a/b\) is a
		root of unity, so \(|a|=|b|\).  With
		\(\rho=a/\overline b\), one has
		\(h({\mathbf x})=\rho\overline{h(\bar {\mathbf x})}\) for all \({\mathbf x}\), and the preceding scalar
		argument proves the required projective ARS conclusion.
		
        Assume now that \(\arity(h)\ge4\). Let \(L\) be the number field generated
		over \(\mathbb Q\) by all entries of \(\cF\) and their complex conjugates.
		It is a finite extension of \(\mathbb Q\).  Every output of a fixed direct
		\(X\)-gadget over signatures already generated from \(h\) has entries in
		\(L\), and every nonzero ratio used to normalize such an output also lies in
		\(L\).
		
		Run the generating process of
		\cite[arXiv v2, Definition~29]{MengWangXia2025EO} starting from \(\{h\}\),
		with \(X\) as its initial binary.  All numbered references to this
		paper below use the same version.  Each self-loop and each binary path in that
		definition is a fixed direct \(X\)-gadget.  We therefore add its output to
		\(\cF\) by \cref{lem:ambient-eo-lifting}. Zero outputs are
		discarded.  If a generated nonzero binary is singular, the singular case of
		\cref{lem:binary-interpolation} gives unary factors.  If it is nonsingular
		but its transfer has infinite projective order, the other case of that lemma
		gives a rank-one binary and then unary factors.  Either event is hard by
		\(\neg\Tract(K\cF)\) and \cref{lem:common-unary-exit}.  The original witnesses
		of both charge signs exclude the factor-extraction exception at every
		stage.
		
		Otherwise every generated nonzero binary is nonsingular and,
		up to a nonzero scalar, has the form
		\[
		B_r=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\ r&0\end{pmatrix},
		\qquad r\in L^\times,
		\qquad B_rX=\operatorname{diag}(1,r),
		\]
		where \(r\) is a root of unity.  Let \(R_i\) be the parameter set represented
		by the family \(B_i(h)\) at stage \(i\).  The path clause in
		Definition~29 makes each nonempty \(R_i\) the subgroup generated by the
		root-of-unity parameters available at that stage. Repeated self-loop descent
		\cite[Lemma~39]{MengWangXia2025EO} supplies nonemptiness.  To make the
		monotonicity explicit, write the stage operation as \(\Phi\).
		Its self-loop and path construction is monotone under inclusion of its
		input parameter set.  Finite order and the positive-power path clause give
		\(1\in R_1\), so \(R_0=\{1\}\subseteq R_1\).  Inductively,
		\(R_i=\Phi(R_{i-1})\subseteq\Phi(R_i)=R_{i+1}\).
		Equality at one stage forces equality thereafter by the same operation.
		Every \(R_i\) lies in the group of roots of unity of \(L\), which is finite.
		Consequently this increasing chain stabilizes at some finite stage \(i_0\).
		
        For a unique \(k\ge1\), the stabilized binary family is
		\[
		B(h)=B_{i_0}(h)=\{B_\omega:\omega\in\mu_k\}.
		\]
		Thus every gadget construction invoked below is finite, as
		required by \cref{lem:ambient-eo-lifting}.
		
		If \(k\ge3\), \cite[Lemmas~40--42]{MengWangXia2025EO} show that \(h\) is
		ARS up to a nonzero scalar.  The opening scalar argument completes this case.
		
		Suppose \(k=2\), and write \(Y=B_{-1}=(0,1,-1,0)\).  Both \(X\) and \(Y\)
		are available in this construction, and \(Y\) is realized by a direct
        gadget. The inductive argument in the proof of
        \cite[Lemma~47]{MengWangXia2025EO} uses fixed \(X\)- or
        \(Y\)-self-loops. At arity four, the proofs of
		\cite[Lemmas~45--46]{MengWangXia2025EO}, before invoking their general
		problem reduction, construct either an EO quaternary
		\(q\) for which the singleton problem is hard by
		\cite[Theorem~3.1]{CaiFuXia2018}, or a nonzero quaternary CP signature
		\[
		D=p\delta_{\mathbf s}+q'\delta_{\bar {\mathbf s}},
		\qquad {\mathbf s}\in\Ftwo^4,\quad\wt({\mathbf s})=2,\quad pq'\ne0.
		\]
		The two-copy subcase realizes \(\Neq_4\) itself.  All these signatures arise
		by the fixed self-loops and two-copy gadgets just identified, so
		\cref{lem:ambient-eo-lifting} adds them relative to \(\cF\).  The hard
		quaternary gives hardness by restriction.  The quaternary CP signature gives a
		unary by \cref{lem:hc-rel-complementary-unary}, and then hardness by
		\cref{lem:common-unary-exit}.  In the remaining case, the proof of
		Lemma~47 gives ARS up to a nonzero scalar, to which the opening argument applies.
		
		Finally suppose \(k=1\).  Follow the proof of
		\cite[Lemma~48]{MengWangXia2025EO}, and let \(C(h)\) denote its set of
		nonzero quaternary signatures obtained by successive \(X\)-self-loops.
		Every member of \(C(h)\) is therefore available by
		\cref{lem:ambient-eo-lifting}.  Moreover, every binary generated from such a
		signature is also generated from \(h\).  Hence its generated binary set is
		contained in \(B(h)=\{X\}\), and Lemma~39 makes that set nonempty. It is
		therefore exactly \(\{X\}\).  If a member of \(C(h)\) lies outside
		\(\{X^{\otimes2},Y^{\otimes2}\}\), the proof of Lemma~45 reaches exactly
		one of the hard quaternaries or quaternary CP signatures described in the preceding
		paragraph.  If \(Y^{\otimes2}\in C(h)\), add this signature and apply
		\cref{thm:external-factor}.  The presence of both charge signs excludes its exceptional case, so
		\(Y\) is available. The proof of Lemma~47 then applies because
        \(B(h)\subseteq\{X,Y\}\) and both binaries are available. Here factor
        extraction supplies the reduction otherwise obtained from
        \cite[Lemma~38]{MengWangXia2025EO}. The case \(C(h)=\varnothing\) is
		impossible for nonzero \(h\) by Lemma~39.  In the remaining case
		\(C(h)=\{X^{\otimes2}\}\), the reorganizing argument in Lemma~48, together
		with \cite[Lemma~43]{MengWangXia2025EO}, makes \(h\) a scalar multiple of a
		real complement-invariant signature, hence projectively ARS.  Its real and
		imaginary parts enter only the vector identities in that argument.  The opening scalar argument again
		makes the scalar algebraic.
	\end{proof}
	
	\begin{lemma}[Hardness outside \(\mathcal B_{\rm ARS}\) under projective ARS]
		\label{lem:hc-rel-cfs}
		Under the assumptions preceding \cref{thm:hc-relative-eo-terminal}, let \(h\in\cF\) be an
		EO signature for which there is a
		\(c\in\AlgNums^\times\) satisfying
		\[
		(c^{-1}h)({\mathbf x})=\overline{(c^{-1}h)(\bar {\mathbf x})}
		\qquad({\mathbf x}\in\{0,1\}^{\arity(h)}).
		\]
		If there are no \(\lambda\in\AlgNums^\times\), perfect matching \(P_h\)
		together with an orientation \((i,j)\) of each matched pair, and
		\(a_{ij}\in\AlgNums^\times\) such that
		\[
		h=\lambda
		\bigotimes_{(i,j)\in P_h}(0,a_{ij},\overline{a_{ij}},0)_{ij},
		\]
		then \(\KHolant(\cF)\) is \(\SharpP\)-hard.
	\end{lemma}
	
	\begin{proof}
		Put \(h'=c^{-1}h\), which is algebraic, EO, and ARS.  By uniqueness of irreducible factorization
		and the ARS factorization lemma
		\cite[Lemmas~2.13--2.14]{CaiFuShao2020ARS}, choose an irreducible
		factorization of \(h'\), up to an overall scalar, into factors
		\(g_1,\ldots,g_m\) on disjoint variable blocks, each satisfying ARS exactly.
		The coordinate restrictions called \emph{pinnings} in the proof of
		\cite[Lemma~2.14]{CaiFuShao2020ARS} are slices used to prove
		tensor-divisibility identities.  They do not supply direct pinning gadgets.
		
		We next choose algebraic representatives and restore exact ARS.  For each \(j\), choose
		\({\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_j\in\operatorname{supp}(g_j)\), let
		\({\boldsymbol{\alpha}}={\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_1\cdots{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_m\), and define on the variable block of \(g_j\)
		\[
		q_j({\mathbf z})=
		\frac{h'({\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_1,\ldots,{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{j-1},{\mathbf z},
			{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{j+1},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_m)}{h'({\boldsymbol{\alpha}})}.
		\]
		The chosen support assignments make the denominator nonzero, and
		\(q_j=g_j/g_j({\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_j)\).  Hence every \(q_j\) is algebraic, EO, irreducible,
		and projectively ARS, and
		\[
		h'=h'({\boldsymbol{\alpha}})\bigotimes_{j=1}^m q_j.
		\]
		For each \(q_j\), the algebraic-scalar argument in
		\cref{lem:hc-rel-mwx} gives
		\(c_j\in\AlgNums^\times\) such that \(p_j=c_j^{-1}q_j\) satisfies ARS
		exactly.  Thus
		\[
		h'=h'({\boldsymbol{\alpha}})\Bigl(\prod_{j=1}^m c_j\Bigr)
		\bigotimes_{j=1}^m p_j,
		\]
		with an algebraic overall scalar and algebraic EO--ARS irreducible factors \(p_j\).
        If every irreducible factor were binary, we would have
        \(h\in\mathcal B_{\rm ARS}\), contrary to the hypothesis. Thus there is a nonbinary irreducible EO--ARS factor
		\(f\notin\mathcal B_{\rm ARS}\).  Iterated use of
		\cref{thm:external-factor} adds \(f\) to the current set. Since
		both charge signs occur, its common-one-sided exception is impossible.  Whenever
		the cited descent subsequently extracts another factor, we repeat the
		displayed ratio construction and rescale by a nonzero algebraic scalar to
		obtain an ARS representative. Thus each signature used in the descent
		is a member of the current set or an extracted tensor factor.
		
		Apply the proof of
		\cite[Lemmas~3.2 and~4.1--4.7; Theorem~4.8]{CaiFuShao2020ARS} to the
		EO--ARS signature \(f\). The signature-producing operations are local: the tensor connections in Lemma~3.2 and the contractions in Lemma~4.6 are
		fixed direct \(X\)-gadgets, while the selected-variable attachments of the fixed
		binary \(iY=(0,i,-i,0)\) in Lemmas~4.5--4.6 are direct gadgets after that
		binary factor has been extracted.  These direct gadgets can be substituted over
		\(\cF\) by
		\cref{lem:ambient-eo-lifting}. Every factor-extraction step is supplied
		separately by \cref{thm:external-factor}, whose exception is again excluded
		by the presence of both charge signs.  Sections~3--4 of the cited proof use no polynomial
		interpolation.
		
		Concretely, Lemma~3.2 produces a direct EO quaternary whose singleton problem
		is hard, directly realizes \(\Neq_4\), or leaves the case treated in
		Lemmas~4.1--4.7. Using Lemmas~4.1--4.4, the descent in Lemmas~4.5--4.6
		preserves nonmembership in \(\mathcal B_{\rm ARS}\).
		Lemma~4.7 completes the base arities \(4,6,8\).
		Hence the descent adds either a hard quaternary
		or \(\Neq_4\) by the reductions just described.  The first gives hardness by restriction.
		The second gives a unary by \cref{lem:hc-rel-complementary-unary} and then
		hardness by \cref{lem:common-unary-exit}.
	\end{proof}
	
	\begin{proof}[Proof of \cref{thm:hc-relative-eo-terminal}]
		Apply \cref{lem:hc-rel-mwx}.  Either \(\KHolant(\cF)\) is
		\(\SharpP\)-hard, or \(h\) is projectively ARS.  In the latter case,
		\(h\notin\mathcal B_{\rm ARS}\), so \cref{lem:hc-rel-cfs} gives hardness.
	\end{proof}
	

	
	
	\Needspace{8\baselineskip}
	\subsection{Walsh Coordinates}
\label{subsec:walsh-local}

The equality reductions below and the height-one analysis use the same
Walsh basis to describe tensor coefficients.
For an arity-\(r\) signature \(f\), with the column-vector convention of
\cref{subsec:holant-definitions}, we fix
	the unnormalized Walsh matrix and the Walsh coordinates
	\begin{equation}
		P=\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\1&-1\end{pmatrix},
		\qquad
		\widetilde f=(P^{-1})^{\otimes r}f.
		\label{eq:Walsh-coordinates}
	\end{equation}
	Thus inversion is \(f=P^{\otimes r}\widetilde f\).  In coordinates,
	\[
	f({\mathbf x})=\sum_{{\mathbf y}\in\Ftwo^r}(-1)^{{\mathbf x}\cdot {\mathbf y}}\widetilde f({\mathbf y}).
	\]
	Consequently, if \(\widetilde f\) has even-parity support then \(f\) is
\emph{complement-invariant}, meaning \(f(\bar {\mathbf x})=f({\mathbf x})\).
If \(\widetilde f\) has odd-parity support, then \(f(\bar {\mathbf x})=-f({\mathbf x})\).  We write \(\mathbf1_{\rm even}\) for the even-parity indicator tensor,
    with its arity inferred from context.

For an ordered binary \(B\), its Walsh transfer matrix is
\[
P^{-1}T_BP=P^{-1}(BX)P=2\widetilde BZ,
\qquad \widetilde B=P^{-1}B(P^{-1})^{\mathsf T},
\]
since \(P^{\mathsf T}XP=2Z\). Its Walsh transfer class is therefore
\([P^{-1}T_BP]=[\widetilde BZ]\) when \(B\) is nonsingular.
	
	
	These coordinates describe the same tensor contractions with edge kernel \(2Z\),
    whose factor \(2\) is retained in the gadget calculations.
    This use of Walsh coordinates is distinct from a global transformation of the problem.

    \subsection{Equalities and Complementary-Pair Signatures}
	\label{proofsubsec:equality-anchor}

Three lemmas handle the equality signatures needed below.  The first
concerns a quaternary that becomes
\(\Eq_4\) under a common transformation taking the edge to equality.
The second is a generalized equality already available in
\(K\)-coordinates.  The third starts from a tensor factor with two
complementary support strings in Walsh coordinates. Its proof reduces to
the first form or obtains a
nonzero unary by interpolation.
	
	
	
	\begin{lemma}[Dichotomy from a transformed quaternary equality]
		\label{lem:equality-anchor}
		Let \(\cF\) be a finite algebraic signature set containing an ordered
		quaternary signature \(g\).  Suppose there are
		\[
		U\in\GL_2(\AlgNums),
		\qquad \kappa,\lambda\in\AlgNums^\times,
		\]
		such that
		\[
		(U^{-1})^{\mathsf T}XU^{-1}=\kappa(\Eq_2),
		\qquad
		U^{\otimes4}g=\lambda(\Eq_4).
		\]
		Then either \(\KHolant(\cF)\) is \(\SharpP\)-hard or
		\(\Tract(K\cF)\) holds.
	\end{lemma}
	
	\begin{proof} This is \cite[Lemma~3.8]{Liu2026DEQ2Holant}. \end{proof}

	
	\begin{lemma}[Dichotomy with a generalized equality]
		\label{lem:pure-ge}
		Let \(\cF\) be a finite algebraic signature set containing
		\[
		G_q=c_0\delta_{\zero^q}+c_1\delta_{\one^q},
		\qquad c_0,c_1\in\AlgNums^\times,\qquad q\ge4\text{ an integer}.
		\]
		Then either \(\KHolant(\cF)\) is \(\SharpP\)-hard or
		\(\Tract(K\cF)\) holds.
	\end{lemma}
	
	\begin{proof}
		This is \cite[Lemma~3.9]{Liu2026DEQ2Holant}.
	\end{proof}
	
	\begin{lemma}[Dichotomy from a CP factor in Walsh coordinates]
		\label{lem:walsh-ge-terminal}
		Let \(\cF\) be a finite algebraic signature set whose supported doubled
		charges include both signs.  Suppose a finite equivalent enlargement of
		\(\cF\) contains a nonzero signature with a tensor factor \(h\) of even
		arity \(2k\ge4\) with
		\[
		\widetilde h=(P^{-1})^{\otimes 2k}h
		=c_0\delta_{\mathbf a}+c_1\delta_{\bar {\mathbf a}},
		\qquad {\mathbf a}\in\Ftwo^{2k},\quad c_0c_1\ne0.
		\]
		Then \(\KHolant(\cF)\) is \(\SharpP\)-hard or \(\Tract(K\cF)\)
		holds.  If \(c_1/c_0\) is a root of unity, a finite equivalent enlargement
		contains a quaternary \(g\) satisfying the two transformation conditions of
		\cref{lem:equality-anchor} with \(U=K\).  
	\end{lemma}
	
	\begin{proof}
		Add the specified signature and then its factor \(h\) using
		\cref{eq:finite-equivalent-enlargement,thm:external-factor}. The supported
		charges of both signs exclude the common-one-sided exception.  All subsequent gadgets are
		\(K\)-gadgets.  In Walsh coordinates an edge is
		\(P^{\mathsf T}XP=2Z\).
		
        Choose two positions at which \({\mathbf a}\) has the same bit.
        Relabel the two support strings, and their coefficients, so that this
        bit is zero in \({\mathbf a}\). Take two copies of \(h\), leave these
        two positions exposed in each copy, and join all other
		corresponding positions between the copies.  Write \(J\) for the joined
		positions.  The diagonal edges allow only the two equally oriented pairs
		of support strings.  Since \(|J|=2k-2\) is even, their signs agree.  Thus the
		quaternary gadget output \(q\) has
		\[
		\widetilde q=\lambda(\delta_{0000}+\rho\delta_{1111}),
		\quad \rho=(c_1/c_0)^2,
		\quad \lambda=2^{2k-2}(-1)^{\wt({\mathbf a}|_J)}c_0^2\ne0.
		\]
		Closing two variables of \(q\) gives a binary \(B\) with
		\[
		\widetilde B=2\lambda\diag(1,-\rho),
		\qquad P^{-1}(BX)P=4\lambda\diag(1,\rho).
		\]
		The multiplicative order of \(\rho\) now determines which
		reduction applies.  If it is infinite, \cref{lem:binary-interpolation} supplies a rank-one binary.  Factor
		extraction supplies a nonzero unary, and \cref{lem:common-unary-exit}
		gives the conclusion.
		
		Otherwise let \(\ell\) be the order of \(\rho\).  A chain of \(\ell-1\)
		copies of \(B\) has Walsh transfer class \([\diag(1,\rho^{-1})]\).
		When \(\ell=1\) use the empty chain.  Attaching this chain to one variable of
		\(q\) gives a gadget output \(g\) with
		\(\widetilde g=c(\delta_{0000}+\delta_{1111})\), \(c\ne0\).
        Walsh inversion gives
		\[
		g=2c\mathbf1_{\rm even},\qquad K^{\otimes4}g=4c(\Eq_4),
		\qquad (K^{-1})^{\mathsf T}XK^{-1}=I.
		\]
		These are the conditions of \cref{lem:equality-anchor} with \(U=K\), so
		that lemma applies.  The equivalent enlargements transfer
		hardness back to \(\cF\). Tractability passes to the original
		subset by \cref{lem:downward-inheritance}.\looseness=-1
	\end{proof}
	
	\section{Obtaining a Mountain}
	\label{sec:factor-charge}

Starting from an even-arity signature set with supported half-charges of
both signs, we seek either both pinning signatures or a signature with two
nonzero endpoints and all remaining support on the central EO layer.
The latter is a mountain, defined below.  Its height will be the gcd of
the nonzero supported half-charges of the entire input set.  This choice
also retains the divisibility condition on the input signatures needed
in the later height analysis.

	\begin{definition}[Mountain]
		\label{def:mountain}
		For an integer \(d\ge1\), an arity-\(2d\) signature \(M\) in
		\(K\)-coordinates is a mountain of height \(d\) if
		\[
		\supp(M)\subseteq HW_{2d}^=\cup\{\zero^{2d},\one^{2d}\}
		\qquad\text{and}\qquad
		M(\zero^{2d})M(\one^{2d})\ne0.
		\]
		Equivalently,
		\[
		\{\chi_{2d}({\mathbf x}):M({\mathbf x})\ne0\}\subseteq\{-d,0,d\},
		\qquad
		\{-d,d\}\subseteq\{\chi_{2d}({\mathbf x}):M({\mathbf x})\ne0\}.
		\]
		Thus the height is both half the arity and the absolute integer half-charge
		of either endpoint.  No restriction is placed on the coefficients on the central Hamming layer.
	\end{definition}

	\begin{lemma}[Producing the gcd-height signature or both pinning signatures]
		\label{lem:charge-bridge}
        Let \(\cF\) be a finite preprocessed set of even-arity algebraic
        signatures.
		Assume that among the supported half-charges
		\[
        \chi_{\arity(f)}({\mathbf x})=\wt({\mathbf x})-\frac{\arity(f)}2,
		\qquad f\in\cF,\quad f({\mathbf x})\ne0,
		\]
		there is at least one positive value and at least one negative value.  Put
		\[
		d=\gcd\left\{
        \left|\chi_{\arity(f)}({\mathbf x})\right|:
        f\in\cF,\ f({\mathbf x})\ne0,\
        \chi_{\arity(f)}({\mathbf x})\ne0
		\right\}.
		\]
        Then \(d\ge1\), every supported half-charge
        \(\chi_{\arity(f)}({\mathbf x})\) lies in \(d\mathbb Z\), and at least one of the
		following reductions holds.
		\begin{enumerate}
			\item There is an arity-\(2d\) signature \(M\) such that
			\[
			\KHolant(\cF,M)\leT\KHolant(\cF),
			\]
			\[
			M(\zero^{2d})M(\one^{2d})\ne0,
			\qquad
			M({\mathbf x})=0\quad\text{if }\wt({\mathbf x})\notin\{0,d,2d\}.
			\]
			\item Both pinning signatures are available by reduction:
			\[
			\KHolant(\cF,\DeltaZero,\DeltaOne)
			\leT\KHolant(\cF).
			\]
		\end{enumerate}
	\end{lemma}
	
	\begin{proof}
		Let
		\[
		C=\{\chi_{\arity(f)}({\mathbf x}):
		f\in\cF,\ f({\mathbf x})\ne0,\ \chi_{\arity(f)}({\mathbf x})\ne0\}
		\]
		be the finite set of nonzero supported half-charges of the input set.
		The gcd assertion follows from the definition of \(d\).
		The next argument expresses \(d\) and \(-d\) as sums of elements of
		\(C\) with nonnegative multiplicities.  Tensor products add half-charges, but their factor
		multiplicities cannot be negative.  Because \(C\) contains both signs, take every pair
		\(a,-b\in C\) with \(a,b>0\).  If \(g=\gcd(a,b)\), assigning multiplicity
		\(b/g\) to \(a\) and \(a/g\) to \(-b\) gives total half-charge zero.  Summing
		these assignments over all positive--negative pairs gives a strictly
		positive multiplicity for each element of \(C\), since each participates
		in at least one pair.  We therefore obtain a vector
		\({\mathbf v}\in\mathbb Z_{>0}^C\) satisfying
		\[
		\sum_{c\in C}v_c c=0.
		\]
		Bézout's identity, iterated over the finite set \(C\), supplies an integer
		vector \({\mathbf u}\in\mathbb Z^C\) with \(\sum_{c\in C}u_c c=d\)
		\cite[Section~1.3, Theorem~1.2]{Apostol1976}.  The entries of \({\mathbf u}\) need not be
		nonnegative, but every entry of \({\mathbf v}\) is positive.  Consequently, for all sufficiently
		large nonnegative integers \(k\), both vectors
		\[
		{\mathbf n}^+={\mathbf u}+k{\mathbf v},\qquad {\mathbf n}^-=-{\mathbf u}+k{\mathbf v}
		\]
		are nonnegative integer vectors, while their total half-charges remain \(d\)
		and \(-d\), respectively.  Thus \({\mathbf n}^+\) and \({\mathbf n}^-\) are the required
		multiplicity lists.
		
For each \(c\in C\), choose a signature \(f_c\in\cF\) and an input
\({\mathbf x}_c\in\supp(f_c)\) with \(\chi_{\arity(f_c)}({\mathbf x}_c)=c\).
Form the two tensor products using \(n_c^+\) and \(n_c^-\) copies of
\(f_c\), respectively.  Concatenating the chosen inputs in these copies
gives a nonzero product of signature values, with half-charge \(d\) in
the first tensor product and \(-d\) in the second.  Every supported
half-charge of either tensor product is divisible by \(d\), since the
same is true of each input signature.

For these two supported inputs, the excess of ones over zeros, or of
zeros over ones, is \(2d\).  The endpoint-preserving self-loop lemma
\cite[full version, Lemma~40; conference version, Lemma~37]{MengWangXiaZheng2025}
therefore supplies sequences of \(X\) self-loops whose outputs
\(P,N\) have arity \(2d\) and satisfy
\(P(\one^{2d})N(\zero^{2d})\ne0\).
Both outputs are realized by fixed gadgets over \(\cF\).
Each \(X\) self-loop removes one zero and one one from every
contributing input, so the supported half-charges remain divisible by
\(d\).  At arity \(2d\), they lie between \(-d\) and \(d\), giving
\[
\supp(P)\cup\supp(N)
\subseteq
HW_{2d}^=\cup\{\zero^{2d},\one^{2d}\}.
\]
		If either \(P\) or \(N\) has both endpoints, take that signature as the
		required \(M\).
		We may therefore assume
		\[
		P(\zero^{2d})=0,
		\qquad
		N(\one^{2d})=0.
		\]
		Apart from the two nonzero endpoints already identified, all supported
		inputs have weight \(d\). Their central restrictions are \(P|_{\EO}\) and \(N|_{\EO}\).
		
If both central restrictions are nonzero, permute chosen central strings to
\((\zero^d,\one^d)\) in \(P\) and \((\one^d,\zero^d)\) in \(N\).
Expose the first \(d\) variables of each signature and join their last
\(d\) variables in the same order by \(X\) edges.  Call the resulting
arity-\(2d\) signature \(Q\).  At the all-zero boundary, the support
restriction and \(P(\zero^{2d})=0\) force the last block of \(P\) to be
\(\one^d\), and hence the last block of \(N\) to be \(\zero^d\).
At the all-one boundary, \(N(\one^{2d})=0\) similarly forces the last
block of \(N\) to be \(\zero^d\), and that of \(P\) to be \(\one^d\).
Thus each endpoint sum has exactly one nonzero term, and
\[
Q(\zero^{2d})=P(\zero^d,\one^d)N(\zero^{2d}),\qquad
Q(\one^{2d})=P(\one^{2d})N(\one^d,\zero^d),
\]
both nonzero.

If only \(P\) has a nonzero central restriction, write
\(N=n\delta_{\zero^{2d}}\) for \(n\in\AlgNums^\times\), and permute a
supported central string of \(P\) to \((\zero^d,\one^d)\).  Join the two
\(d\)-variable blocks of \(N\) to the last \(d\) variables of two copies of
\(P\), again by \(X\) edges, leaving the first \(d\) variables of
both copies exposed.  The support of \(N\) forces its variables to zero
and the connected variables of both copies of \(P\) to one.  The endpoint
extensions are therefore unique, with values
\(nP(\zero^d,\one^d)^2\) and \(nP(\one^{2d})^2\), both nonzero.
If only \(N\) has a nonzero central restriction, use the same construction
with the roles of \(P,N\) and of zero and one interchanged.
In either construction, \(X\) charge conservation preserves
divisibility by \(d\).  Since the output has arity \(2d\), its supported
half-charges belong to \(\{-d,0,d\}\). Take that output as \(M\).

It remains to consider the case in which both central restrictions are zero.
Then \(P=p\DeltaOne^{\otimes2d}\) and
\(N=n\DeltaZero^{\otimes2d}\) for \(p,n\in\AlgNums^\times\).
The set \(\cF\cup\{P,N\}\) retains the original witnesses of positive and
negative half-charge, so the common-one-sided exception in
\cref{thm:external-factor} cannot occur.  Applying that theorem to the
pure tensor powers, with nonzero scalars handled by preprocessing, and
substituting the gadgets for \(P,N\) proves the second reduction.
	\end{proof}

In the pinning outcome of \cref{lem:charge-bridge}, if
\(\neg\Tract(K\cF)\), then
\cref{lem:downward-inheritance,lem:common-unary-exit} and the displayed
reduction give \(\SharpP\)-hardness of \(\KHolant(\cF)\).
Thus the remaining analysis uses the mountain outcome.
	
\begin{remark}
The pinning case in \cref{lem:charge-bridge} is necessary.
Consider \(\{\operatorname{EXACT}^4_3,\operatorname{EXACT}^4_1\}\), where
\(\operatorname{EXACT}^4_j({\mathbf x})=\one[\wt({\mathbf x})=j]\).
These signatures have single supported half-charges \(1\) and \(-1\),
respectively.  In a fixed gadget built directly from them using
\(X\) edges, charge conservation fixes the half-charge of every
supported boundary input: it is the number of
\(\operatorname{EXACT}^4_3\)-vertices minus the number of
\(\operatorname{EXACT}^4_1\)-vertices.  Such a gadget cannot have both
endpoints of a mountain.  The lemma instead gives both pinning signatures
through factor extraction.
	\end{remark}
	\section{Mountain Height One}
	\label{sec:binary-dynamics}
	
	A height-one mountain is an ordered binary signature whose two diagonal
	entries are nonzero. We prove the dichotomy for any finite algebraic
	signature set containing one. First, a nonzero unary settles the case by
	the odd-arity dichotomy. A nonzero singular binary, or a nonsingular binary
	whose transfer has infinite projective order, leads to the same conclusion
	by \cref{lem:binary-interpolation}.

	In the remaining case, the projective transfers of all nonzero directly
	realizable binaries form a finite group. By \cref{thm:finite-group-split},
	we either obtain a nonsingular weighted equality or normalize this group
	to an odd cyclic or odd dihedral form. A weighted equality can be
	normalized to \(I=(\Eq_2)\) by a global diagonal transformation in
	\(GO(X)\), so \cref{thm:eq2-kholant-dichotomy} settles that branch.

	For the two remaining group forms, contractions and factor extraction
	either give hardness, identify a common tractable basis, or yield a strict
	update: the transfer group grows in the original coordinates, or the
	minimum nonbinary irreducible arity decreases. A uniform bound over the
	initial number field makes these updates finite. We establish the common
	contraction tools, treat the dihedral and cyclic cases separately, and
	then combine them with the termination argument.

	\begin{theorem}[Dichotomy with a height-one mountain]
		\label{cor:height-one-complete}
		Let \(\cF\) be a finite algebraic signature set containing a height-one
		mountain.  Then
		\[
		\Tract(K\cF)
		\qquad\text{or}\qquad
		\KHolant(\cF)\text{ is }\SharpP\text{-hard}.
		\]
	\end{theorem}
	
	We prove this theorem at the end of the section, after establishing the
	finite-group alternatives and the termination of their updates.  
	
	\subsection{Binary equality}
	\label{subsec:equality-quaternary-interfaces}
	
    Binary equality gives the following classification, due to
    Liu and Meng \cite{Liu2026DEQ2Holant}.
	
	\begin{theorem}[Binary-equality \(K\)-Holant dichotomy]
		\label{thm:eq2-kholant-dichotomy}
		Let \(\cF\) be a finite algebraic signature set containing
		\(I=(\Eq_2)\).  If \(\Tract(K\cF)\) holds, then
		\(\KHolant(\cF)\in\FP\). Otherwise
		\(\KHolant(\cF)\) is \(\SharpP\)-hard.
		The predicate \(\Tract(K\cF)\) is decidable.
	\end{theorem}
	
	\begin{proof}
		\cite[Theorem~1.2]{Liu2026DEQ2Holant} is stated with a reduced four-clause
		predicate.
		Since \(I\in\cF\), its coordinate image contains
		\(Z=K^{\otimes2}I\in K\cF\). Hence the binary-anchor lemma
		\cite[Lemma~2.13]{Liu2026DEQ2Holant} identifies that reduced predicate with
		\(\Tract(K\cF)\).  The displayed formulation follows.
	\end{proof}
	
	\subsection{Finite Transfer Groups}
\label{subsec:finite-transfer-groups}

Series composition turns binary gadgets into matrix products. Passing to
projective classes removes the nonzero scalars already recorded in the
reductions. We use finite-group structure to find a weighted equality or
restrict the remaining transfers to cyclic and dihedral forms. The edge
and sign matrices remain fixed throughout this classification.

We use \(H\le G\) for a subgroup, \(H\triangleleft G\) for a normal
	subgroup, and \([G:H]\) for its index.  An involution has order two.
	Our finite-group notation is
	\[
	C_m,\qquad
	D_{2m}=\langle r,s\mid r^m=s^2=1,\ srs=r^{-1}\rangle,\qquad
	V_4=C_2\times C_2,
	\]
	where \(C_m\) is cyclic of order \(m\) and \(D_{2m}\) has order \(2m\).
	The symmetric and alternating groups are \(S_m\) and \(A_m\).
	For \(S\subseteq G\), the centralizer is
    \(C_G(S)=\{g\in G:gs=sg\text{ for every }s\in S\}\).
    If \(\zeta\) is a primitive \(m\)-th root of unity,
	then \([\mathbb Q(\zeta):\mathbb Q]=\varphi(m)\), where \(\varphi\) is
	Euler's totient.
	
	\subsubsection{Ordered Bell signatures}
	\label{subsec:pauli-notation}
	
	The ordered Bell signatures are \(\mathcal B=\{I,X,Y,Z\}\).
    With truth-table order \(00,01,10,11\), they are
	\begin{equation}
		\begin{aligned}
			I&=(1,0,0,1),&
			Z&=(1,0,0,-1),\\
			X&=(0,1,1,0),&
			Y&=(0,1,-1,0).
		\end{aligned}
		\label{eq:bell-matrix-dictionary}
	\end{equation}
	Our real skew matrix \(Y\) differs from the conventional complex Pauli
	\(Y\)-matrix by a nonzero scalar.
	
	\begin{remark}[Bell notation in Shao--Cai]
		The tuple in \cref{eq:bell-matrix-dictionary} is Shao--Cai's
		\((=^+_2,=^-_2,\neq^+_2,\neq^-_2)\) \cite[Section~2]{ShaoCai2020}.
		\(Y^{\mathsf T}=-Y\).  Their holographic matrix named \(Z\) is our \(K\),
		whereas our \(Z\) is only the diagonal sign matrix.
	\end{remark}
	
	
Recall from \cref{subsec:reduction-conventions} that \(T_B=BX\),
\(T_{BXC}=T_BT_C\), and \(T_{B^{\mathsf T}}=(T_B)^\#\).
	For a general \(T\in\Mat_2(\AlgNums)\), the operation defined in
	\cref{subsec:reduction-conventions} satisfies
	\begin{equation}
		T^\#=XT^{\mathsf T}X,
		\qquad
		T^\#=(\det T)ZT^{-1}Z\quad\text{if }T\text{ is nonsingular}.
		\label{eq:transfer-reversal}
	\end{equation}
	
	\begin{remark}[Relation to binary-signature classifications]
        \Cref{lem:binary-interpolation} handles nonzero singular binaries and
        nonsingular binaries whose transfer \(T_B=BX\) has infinite projective
        order, leaving the finite-order case. This is the K-Holant form of the
        binary classification used by Cai--Lu--Xia
        \cite{CaiLuXia2009,CaiLuXia2018RealHolantc}. The four ordered Bell
		signatures in \(\mathcal B\) represent
		the projective Bell classes of Shao--Cai \cite{ShaoCai2020}.  Beyond
		the binary classification, we need the complete transfer group
		and uniform control of its higher-arity contractions.
	\end{remark}
	
	\begin{lemma}[Bounded torsion]
		\label{lem:bounded-torsion}
		Fix a number field \(L\), and let \(G\) be the set of projective
		transfers of all directly realizable nonsingular ordered
		binary gadgets over a fixed signature set with entries in \(L\).
		If \(G\ne\varnothing\) and every element of \(G\) has finite
		projective order, then \(G\) is a finite subgroup of
		\(\PGL_2(L)\).
	\end{lemma}
	
	\begin{proof}
		This is \cite[Lemma~5.2]{Liu2026DEQ2Holant}.
	\end{proof}
	
	For a current signature set \(\cF\), suppose that every nonzero directly
	realizable ordered binary \(B\) is nonsingular and that \([BX]\) has finite
	order.  Series composition and \cref{lem:bounded-torsion} then give the finite
	group
	\begin{equation}
		G(\cF)
		=\{[BX]:B\ne0\text{ is an ordered binary directly realizable over }\cF\}.
		\label{eq:complete-safe-group}
	\end{equation}
We call this the \emph{complete transfer group}, since it includes the
transfer classes of all directly realizable nonsingular binaries over \(\cF\).

We next classify this finite group. Changes of basis and comparisons
between successive groups are handled in \cref{sec:odd-residue}.
	
	\begin{proposition}[Finite projective binary groups]
		\label{prop:finite-pgl2-classification}
		Every finite subgroup of \(\PGL_2(\mathbb C)\) is conjugate to exactly one
		of the following types:
		\[
		C_m,\qquad D_{2m},\qquad A_4,\qquad S_4,\qquad A_5,
		\]
		where \(m\ge1\) for \(C_m\), \(m\ge2\) for \(D_{2m}\),
		\(|C_m|=m\), \(|D_{2m}|=2m\), and
		\(|A_4|=12\), \(|S_4|=24\), \(|A_5|=60\).
	\end{proposition}
	
	\begin{proof}
		This is the classical conjugacy classification
		\cite[Section~5.1]{Beardon1983}, also stated in
		\cite[Proposition~2.1]{Liu2026DEQ2Holant}.
	\end{proof}
	
	\begin{lemma}[Finite projective-group facts]
		\label{lem:finite-pgl2-facts}
		The following properties will be used below.
		\begin{enumerate}
			\item In
			\[
			D_{2m}=\langle r,s\mid r^m=s^2=1,\ srs=r^{-1}\rangle,
			\]
			the rotations \(\angles r\cong C_m\) form a normal index-two
			subgroup, and every element outside it is a reflection and an
			involution.  If \(m\) is odd, the rotation group is the unique
			index-two subgroup.  If \(m\) is even, the only nonidentity
			rotational involution is the central element \(r^{m/2}\).
			\item The group \(A_4\) has no index-two subgroup, and the centralizer
			of an involution is \(V_4\).
			\item The unique index-two subgroup of \(S_4\) is \(A_4\).  Every
			involution is a transposition or a double transposition. Precisely
			the double transpositions belong to \(A_4\).
			\item The group \(A_5\) is simple and has no index-two subgroup.  The
			centralizer of each involution is \(V_4\).
			\item The projective centralizer of the distinguished involution is
			\[
			C_{\PGL_2(\mathbb C)}([Z])
			=
			\left\{
			\left[\begin{pmatrix}a&0\\0&b\end{pmatrix}\right]:a,b\in\mathbb C,\ ab\ne0
			\right\}
			\sqcup
			\left\{
			\left[\begin{pmatrix}0&a\\b&0\end{pmatrix}\right]:a,b\in\mathbb C,\ ab\ne0
			\right\}.
			\]
			Thus \([Z]\) is the only nonidentity diagonal involution, while
			every nonsingular anti-diagonal class is an involution.
		\end{enumerate}
	\end{lemma}
	
	\begin{proof}
		The first four items follow from the cyclic and dihedral presentations
		and the standard permutation models \cite{DixonMortimer1996}.
		The projective centralizer calculation in the last item is given in
		\cite[Lemma~2.2]{Liu2026DEQ2Holant}.
	\end{proof}
	
	The classification identifies the abstract group type. To use it in
	K-Holant, we must also preserve the edge through a change of basis in
	\(GO(X)\). The proof below keeps the distinguished classes \([X]\) and
	\([Z]\) fixed and constructs the required normalization explicitly.
	
	Let \(G\le\PGL_2(\AlgNums)\) be finite, and suppose each class has
	a directly realized ordered-binary representative.  Choose one
	nonsingular ordered binary \(B_g\) for each class \(g\), normalized by
	\(R_g=B_gX\), \([R_g]=g\), and \(R_1=I\), where \(1=[I]\).  The matrix of
	every representative of class \(g\) is \(\rho B_g\) for a unique nonzero
	scalar \(\rho\).  Fix one direct gadget for each of these finitely many
	representatives and substitute it whenever that representative is used.
	Displayed
	projective identities are read up to their known nonzero scalars. A scalar is
	shown explicitly whenever its zero/nonzero status or a coefficient ratio is
	used.
	
	
	\begin{theorem}[Weighted equality or an odd cyclic or dihedral group]
		\label{thm:finite-group-split}
		Let \(\cF\) be a finite algebraic signature set containing the height-one
		mountain
		\[
		B=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\c&d\end{pmatrix},
		\qquad ad\ne0,
		\]
		and suppose every nonzero directly realizable binary over \(\cF\) is
		nonsingular and its transfer has finite projective order.  Then one of the following
		holds.
		\begin{enumerate}
			\item A nonsingular weighted equality
			\(E=\diag(p,q)\), with \(pq\ne0\), is directly realizable over
			\(\cF\), up to a known nonzero scalar.
			\item There exist an odd \(N\ge3\) and \(D\in GO(X)\) such that the global normalization \(\cF\mapsto D\cF\) puts
			\(G(D\cF)\) in exactly one of the forms
			\[
			\{[R(s)]:s\in\mu_N\},
			\qquad
			\{[R(s)],[ZR(s)]:s\in\mu_N\},
			\qquad
			R(s)=\frac12
			\begin{pmatrix}1+s&1-s\\1-s&1+s\end{pmatrix}.
			\]
			Every displayed projective class has a fixed directly realizable
			ordered-binary representative over \(D\cF\).
		\end{enumerate}
	\end{theorem}

    For \(s\in\AlgNums\), the displayed matrix family satisfies
    \(P^{-1}R(s)P=\diag(1,s)\).
	
	\begin{proof}
		Put \(G=G(\cF)\) and
		\[
		H=\angles{G,[Z]}.
		\]
		All gadget entries lie in the number field generated by the entries of
		\(\cF\), so \cref{lem:bounded-torsion} makes \(G\) finite.  If
		\([T]\in G\), swapping the two variables and \cref{eq:transfer-reversal} give
		\([ZT^{-1}Z]\in G\).  Since inversion permutes \(G\), it follows that
		\([Z]G[Z]=G\).  Consequently
		\[
		G\triangleleft H,\qquad [H:G]\le2,
		\]
		and \(H=G\) or \(H=G\sqcup[Z]G\), so \(H\) is finite.
		
		For the distinguished mountain,
		\[
		T=BX=\begin{pmatrix}b&a\\d&c\end{pmatrix}
		\]
		has class \([T]\in G\).  Since \(a,d\ne0\), both off-diagonal entries of
		\(T\) are nonzero.  An anti-diagonal transfer will give the weighted equality we seek. Suppose \([S]\in G\)
		has an anti-diagonal representative
		\[
		S=\begin{pmatrix}0&p\\q&0\end{pmatrix},
		\qquad pq\ne0.
		\]
		By the definition of \(G\), there is a directly realizable ordered binary
		\(B_S\) and a scalar \(\rho\ne0\) such that \(B_SX=\rho S\).  Hence
		\[
		B_S=\rho SX=\rho\diag(p,q),
		\]
		which is the nonsingular weighted equality in outcome~1.
		
		We now use
		\cref{prop:finite-pgl2-classification,lem:finite-pgl2-facts}.  Their use
		here is only group-theoretic: the distinguished class is the fixed
		\([Z]\), and no conjugating matrix from the abstract classification is
		applied to the signature set.
		
		If \(H\) is cyclic, it is abelian, so \([T]\) commutes with \([Z]\).  The
		projective centralizer of \([Z]\) consists of the diagonal and
		anti-diagonal classes.  The two nonzero off-diagonal entries of \(T\)
		exclude the diagonal case. Thus \([T]\in G\) is anti-diagonal and gives the
		weighted equality.
