		Suppose \(H\cong A_4\).  Since \(A_4\) has no index-two subgroup, the
		index bound forces \(G=H\).  The centralizer of \([Z]\) in \(H\) is a
		\(V_4\), so it contains an involution \(u\ne1,[Z]\).  The class \(u\)
		belongs to \(G\) and commutes with \([Z]\).  Because \([Z]\) is the only
		nonidentity diagonal involution, \(u\) is anti-diagonal.  The same argument
		applies when \(H\cong A_5\): simplicity gives \(G=H\), and the
		centralizer of an involution is again \(V_4\).
		
		Suppose \(H\cong S_4\).  If \(G=H\) and \([Z]\) corresponds to a
		transposition, choose the disjoint transposition. If \(G=H\) and \([Z]\) corresponds
		to a double transposition, choose either of the other two double
		transpositions.  In both cases the chosen involution lies in \(G\), commutes
		with \([Z]\), and differs from it, so it is anti-diagonal.  If \(G\ne H\),
		then the unique index-two subgroup gives \(G=A_4\).  Here \([Z]\notin G\)
		is an involution outside \(A_4\), hence a transposition.  If \(z'\) is the
		disjoint transposition, then \(u=[Z]z'\) is a double transposition in
		\(A_4=G\). It commutes with \([Z]\), differs from it, and is therefore
		anti-diagonal.
		
		It remains to consider
		\[
		H=D_{2m}=\angles{r,z:r^m=z^2=1,\ zrz=r^{-1}},
		\]
		where the distinguished reflection is denoted by \(z=[Z]\) when \([Z]\)
		is a reflection.  If \(m\) is even and \([Z]=r^{m/2}\) is the central
		half-turn, then \([T]\in G\) commutes with \([Z]\). Its two nonzero
		off-diagonal entries again make it anti-diagonal.  Suppose instead that
		\([Z]\) is a reflection and \(m\) is even.  The two involutions
		\[
		r^{m/2},\qquad [Z]r^{m/2}
		\]
		commute with \([Z]\) and differ from it.  If \(G=H\), both belong to
		\(G\).  If \([H:G]=2\), then \([Z]\notin G\), and the two displayed
		elements lie in opposite cosets of \(G\). Hence exactly one belongs to
		\(G\).  In either event \(G\) contains an involution different from
		\([Z]\) that commutes with \([Z]\), and this involution is anti-diagonal.
		
		The sole unresolved case is therefore
		\[
		H\cong D_{2N},\qquad N\ge3\text{ odd},\qquad [Z]\text{ a reflection},
		\]
		with \(G=C_N\) or \(G=D_{2N}\).  Indeed, for odd \(N\) the rotation
		subgroup is the unique index-two subgroup. Thus a proper \(G\) is the
		rotation subgroup, while otherwise \(G=H\).
		
		Choose a rotation class \([R]\) of exact order \(N\), and write
		\[
		R=\begin{pmatrix}u&v\\w&x\end{pmatrix}.
		\]
		The dihedral relation gives \([ZRZ]=[R^{-1}]\), while
		\[
		ZRZ=\begin{pmatrix}u&-v\\-w&x\end{pmatrix},
		\qquad
		R^{-1}\doteq\begin{pmatrix}x&-v\\-w&u\end{pmatrix}.
		\]
		The entries \(v,w\) cannot both vanish: otherwise \([R]=[R^{-1}]\), so
		\([R]^2=1\), contrary to the odd order \(N\ge3\).  Since at least one of
		\(v,w\) is nonzero, comparison in the preceding projective equality forces
		the projective scalar to be \(1\), and hence \(u=x\).  Exactly one of
		\(v,w\) cannot vanish.  In that case nonsingularity gives \(u=x\ne0\), and
		a projective rescaling makes \(R\) a nonidentity unipotent matrix. Its
		powers are pairwise distinct in characteristic zero.  Therefore
		\[
		R=\begin{pmatrix}\alpha&\beta\\ \gamma&\alpha\end{pmatrix},
		\qquad \beta\gamma\ne0.
		\]
		
		Choose \(q\in\AlgNums^\times\) with \(q^2=\gamma/\beta\), and put
		\[
		D=\diag(q,1).
		\]
		Then
		\[
		D^{\mathsf T}XD=qX,
		\]
		so \(D\in GO(X)\). Moreover \(DZD^{-1}=Z\).  With
		\(\delta=q\beta=q^{-1}\gamma\),
		\[
		DRD^{-1}
		=\begin{pmatrix}\alpha&\delta\\ \delta&\alpha\end{pmatrix}.
		\]
		In Walsh coordinates,
		\[
		P^{-1}(DRD^{-1})P
		=\diag(\alpha+\delta,\alpha-\delta).
		\]
		Both eigenvalues are nonzero because \(R\) is nonsingular.  Put
		\[
		s_0=\frac{\alpha-\delta}{\alpha+\delta}.
		\]
		The exact projective order of \([R]\) implies that \(s_0\) is a primitive
		\(N\)-th root of unity, and
		\[
		DRD^{-1}
		=(\alpha+\delta)R(s_0),
		\qquad
		R(s)=\frac12\begin{pmatrix}1+s&1-s\\1-s&1+s\end{pmatrix}.
		\]
		Since \(R(s)R(t)=R(st)\), the normalized rotation classes are precisely
		\[
		\{[R(s)]:s\in\mu_N\}.
		\]
		The diagonal matrix \(D\) fixes \([Z]\).  Thus, when \(G=C_N\), these are
		all normalized classes, and, when \(G=D_{2N}\), the remaining classes are
		precisely
		\[
		\{[ZR(s)]:s\in\mu_N\}.
		\]
		
		To identify the normalized group exactly, we compare gadget outputs in
		both directions. If \(B_0\) is a directly realizable ordered binary over
		\(\cF\), the global change of basis gives the boundary signature
		\(B_0^D\) over \(D\cF\) with
		\[
		B_0^D\doteq DB_0D^{\mathsf T},
		\qquad
		(DB_0D^{\mathsf T})X=qD(B_0X)D^{-1}.
		\]
		Hence \(T_{B_0^D}\doteq D T_{B_0}D^{-1}\).  Applying the same argument
		with \(D^{-1}\in GO(X)\) gives the converse, so
		\[
		G(D\cF)=[D]G(\cF)[D]^{-1}.
		\]
        Every displayed class therefore has an ordered binary representative
        realizable over \(D\cF\), with the normalization \(D\in GO(X)\).
	\end{proof}
	
	\subsection{Invariants and Finite Updates}
	\label{sec:odd-residue}

	A later contraction or factor extraction can enlarge the transfer group,
so the group classification may need to be applied again. To prove that
this process terminates, we compare all groups in the original coordinates
over one fixed number field. We also retain the original height-one
mountain under the accumulated change of basis. The following lemma
records these invariants and transfers the final classification back to
the initial signature set.
\begin{lemma}[Invariants under adding signatures and changing basis]
		\label{lem:normalization-ledger}
		Let \(\cF_0\) be a finite preprocessed algebraic signature set, and let
		\(L_0\) be a
		number field containing its entries, their conjugates, and every algebraic
		constant fixed before the construction.  Consider a sequence
		\((A_i,\cF_i)\), in which every \(\cF_i\) is a finite preprocessed
		algebraic signature set, starting from the given \(\cF_0\) with
		\(A_0=I\), in which each step is one of the following.
		\begin{enumerate}
			\item Add signatures under the following conditions: set
			\(A_{i+1}=A_i\), choose a finite
			\(\cF_{i+1}\supseteq\cF_i\) with
			\[
			\KHolant(\cF_{i+1})\leT\KHolant(\cF_i),
			\]
			and require every newly added positive-arity signature \(h\) to
			satisfy
			\[
			h\doteq A_i^{\otimes\arity(h)}h_0
			\quad\text{for a tensor }h_0\text{ over }L_0.
			\]
			\item A global normalization: choose \(D_i\in GO(X)\), and set
			\[
			A_{i+1}=D_iA_i,
			\qquad
			\cF_{i+1}=D_i\cF_i,
			\]
			with the value rescaling from each transformed edge given by \cref{eq:GOX-global-scalar}.
		\end{enumerate}
		Then, at every index \(i\),
		\begin{equation}
			A_i\cF_0\subseteq\cF_i,
			\qquad
			A_i^{\mathsf T}XA_i=\mu_iX
			\quad(\mu_i\in\AlgNums^\times),
			\qquad
			\KHolant(\cF_i)\equivT\KHolant(\cF_0),
			\label{eq:normalization-ledger}
		\end{equation}
		and every positive-arity \(h\in\cF_i\) satisfies
		\[
		h\doteq A_i^{\otimes\arity(h)}h_0
		\quad\text{for a tensor }h_0\text{ over }L_0.
		\]
		
		At an index where every nonzero directly realizable ordered binary is
		nonsingular and has finite projective transfer order, let \(G(\cF_i)\)
		be the group of its projective transfer classes \([BX]\), and put
		\[
		\overline G_i=[A_i]^{-1}G(\cF_i)[A_i].
		\]
		Then \(\overline G_i\le\PGL_2(L_0)\).  On any consecutive sequence
		of such indices, a global normalization leaves \(\overline G_i\) unchanged,
		whereas adding signatures can only enlarge it.  Thus all groups compared in
		the finite-group argument lie in the single fixed field \(L_0\). Any generic
		interpolation that leaves this field is used only when it completes the
		complexity classification of the current case.
		
		If \(\Tract(K\cF_i)\) holds, then \(\Tract(K\cF_0)\) holds.
		Separately, if one matrix \(V\) satisfies the \(K\)-coordinate
		conditions of \cref{def:common-transformability} for
		\(\cF_i\) and \(\cC\), where \(\cC\) is closed under nonzero scalar multiplication,
		then
		\[
		W=A_i^{-1}V
		\]
		satisfies the same \(\cC\)-conditions for \(\cF_0\).  In particular, this
		applies to \(\cA,\cP,\cL\).
	\end{lemma}
	
	\begin{proof}
		This is \cite[Lemma~2.26]{Liu2026DEQ2Holant}, applied inductively to the two
		displayed kinds of step.  When signatures are added, restriction supplies the reverse reduction.  A normalization conjugates the complete transfer group by
		\([D_i]\), which cancels when the group is expressed in the original basis using
		\([A_{i+1}]=[D_iA_i]\).
	\end{proof}
	
	A new factor can advance the proof even when the transfer group stays
	unchanged. To measure this progress, for a factor-closed signature set
	\(\cF\) containing an irreducible signature of arity at least three, put
	\begin{equation*}
		\nu(\cF)
		=\min\{\arity(h):h\in\cF
		\text{ is irreducible and }\arity(h)\ge3\}.
	\end{equation*}
	This parameter is used only after the branch in which every irreducible factor has
	arity at most two has been removed.
	
	
	
	\begin{definition}[Equivalent updates with strict progress]
		\label{def:height-one-outer-successor}
		Let \((A,\cF)\) satisfy \cref{lem:normalization-ledger} for the initial
		set \(\cF_0\) and its fixed number field \(L_0\).  An update to
		\((A^+,\cF^+)\) must satisfy the same invariant and use only the
		additions of signatures and global changes of basis allowed there.  In particular,
		\(\cF^+\) is finite, preprocessed, algebraic, and factor-closed, and
		\[
		\begin{gathered}
			A^+\cF_0\subseteq\cF^+,
			\qquad (A^+)^{\mathsf T}XA^+=\mu_{A^+}X\quad(\mu_{A^+}\ne0),\\
			\KHolant(\cF^+)\equivT\KHolant(\cF).
		\end{gathered}
		\]
		The transformed height-one mountain and every old irreducible signature
		remain members up to the same invertible change of basis and their recorded
		nonzero scalars.  Every nonzero directly realizable ordered binary over
		both sets is nonsingular with finite projective transfer order.  Put
		\[
		H=[A]^{-1}G(\cF)[A],
		\qquad H^+=[A^+]^{-1}G(\cF^+)[A^+].
		\]
		Both groups are subgroups of \(\PGL_2(L_0)\).  When both values of \(\nu\)
		are defined, the update is required to satisfy
		\[
		H\subseteq H^+,
		\qquad \nu(\cF^+)\le\nu(\cF),
		\qquad
		H\ne H^+\ \text{or}\ \nu(\cF^+)\ne\nu(\cF).
		\]
		We call such an update a \emph{strict update}. Thus at least one comparison is strict.  If no nonbinary irreducible
		signature remains, the arity-at-most-two alternative gives tractability
		and no further update is made.
	\end{definition}
	
	Finiteness of each individual group does not by itself bound the number
	of updates. The fixed number field gives a uniform bound on group order,
	which we combine with the decreasing arity parameter.

	\begin{lemma}[Termination of the strict updates]
		\label{lem:height-one-progress-well-founded}
		Fix the number field \(L_0\) in
		\cref{lem:normalization-ledger}.  There is no infinite sequence
		\[
		(H_0,\nu_0),(H_1,\nu_1),\ldots
		\]
		such that every \(H_i\) is a finite subgroup of \(\PGL_2(L_0)\), every
		\(\nu_i\) is an integer at least three, and every update satisfies
		\[
		H_i\subseteq H_{i+1},
		\qquad
		\nu_{i+1}\le\nu_i,
		\]
		with at least one of these two inequalities strict.  In the height-one
		construction these quantities are
		\[
		H_i=[A_i]^{-1}G(\cF_i)[A_i],
		\qquad
		\nu_i=\min\{\arity(f):f\in\cF_i
		\text{ is irreducible and }\arity(f)\ge3\}.
		\]
        If the second set is empty, the arity-at-most-two case is tractable and no
        further strict update is made.
	\end{lemma}
	
	\begin{proof}
		Write \(\delta=[L_0:\mathbb Q]\).  If
		\([T]\in\PGL_2(L_0)\) has projective order \(m\), the ratio of the two
		eigenvalues of \(T\) is a primitive \(m\)-th root of unity \(\zeta_m\).
		The eigenvalues lie in an extension of \(L_0\) of degree at most two, whence
		\[
		\varphi(m)=[\mathbb Q(\zeta_m):\mathbb Q]
		\leq [L_0(\zeta_m):\mathbb Q]\leq2\delta.
		\]
		There are therefore only finitely many possible projective element orders.
		Let \(M_0\) be their maximum.  By
		\cref{prop:finite-pgl2-classification}, every finite subgroup of
		\(\PGL_2(L_0)\) has order at most
		\[
		C_0=\max\{2M_0,60\}.
		\]
		
		For a sequence in the statement, define
		\[
		\Phi_i=(C_0-|H_i|)+(\nu_i-3)\in\mathbb Z_{\ge0}.
		\]
		The inclusions and inequalities make \(\Phi_i\) nonincreasing, and the strict
		condition decreases it by at least one at every update.  Hence the sequence
		has at most \(\Phi_0\) updates.  In the application,
		\cref{lem:normalization-ledger} places every group in the original basis in
		\(\PGL_2(L_0)\), adding signatures makes the groups nested, and
		\cref{def:height-one-outer-successor} makes \(\nu\) nonincreasing.  Factor
		closure between updates is finite because every nontrivial tensor split replaces
		a signature by positive-arity factors of strictly smaller arity. It is not
		part of the displayed measure.
	\end{proof}
	
	When neither a classification nor a strict update occurs, the remaining
	signatures will share a tractable basis. For either group with odd \(N\),
	we will show that the single matrix
	\(P\) fixed in \cref{eq:Walsh-coordinates} satisfies
	\begin{equation}
		(P^{-1})^{\otimes\arity(f)}f\in\cP
		\quad(f\in\cF),
		\qquad
		P^{\mathsf T}XP=2Z\in\cP.
		\label{eq:common-Walsh-product-presentation}
	\end{equation}
	These conditions show that \(K\cF\) is \(\cP\)-transformable
	by \cref{def:common-transformability}.
	
To analyze the remaining branch, we first process contractions and their
factors. The condition below describes the case in which these factors are
binary and their transfer classes lie in the current group. Fix a signature set
	\(\cF\) for which \(G(\cF)\) is defined, a
	subgroup \(H\le G(\cF)\), and one directly realizable ordered binary
	\(B_h\) with \([B_hX]=h\) for each \(h\in H\).  We will refer to the
	following contraction condition for \(f\in\cF\):
	\begin{enumerate}[label=\textnormal{(C)},leftmargin=*]
		\item\label{cond:height-one-contractions}
		For every ordered pair of distinct variables of \(f\), every chosen \(B_h\),
		and both kernel orientations \(XB_hX\) and \(XB_h^{\mathsf T}X\), the
		contraction is zero, or all its irreducible factors are
		nonsingular binaries \(B\) with \([BX]\in H\).
	\end{enumerate}
	Any nullary factors are absorbed into the overall scalar.
	The conditions are in the current \(K\)-coordinates. After a Walsh change
	of coordinates the group membership is conjugated by \(P\) as well.
	Applications below take \(H=G(\cF)\), with the complete transfer group from
	\cref{eq:complete-safe-group}.  Before using
	condition~\ref{cond:height-one-contractions}, any factor that gives
	hardness, tractability, or an update in
	\cref{def:height-one-outer-successor} is handled separately.  The next lemma specifies the finite family of outputs and factors retained
	during this processing. We denote its finite closure under these
contractions and factor extractions by \(\cF^{\mathrm{cl}}\).
	
	\begin{lemma}[Finite recursive contraction and factor extraction]
		\label{lem:height-one-card-saturation}
		Let \((A,\cF)\) be a stage of a sequence satisfying
		\cref{lem:normalization-ledger}, over its fixed number field \(L_0\).
		Suppose that \(\cF\) is finite,
		preprocessed, algebraic, and factor-closed, contains the transformed height-one mountain,
		and every nonzero directly realizable ordered binary over \(\cF\) is
		nonsingular and has finite projective transfer order.  Let
		\(H=G(\cF)\), and fix one directly realizable ordered-binary
		representative of each class in this finite group.
		
		Start with every nonbinary irreducible factor currently in \(\cF\).  For
		each factor \(f\), process every choice of an ordered pair of
		distinct variables, a fixed representative of a class in \(H\), and either
		kernel orientation.  Add the resulting nonzero contraction of two variables and, through
		\cref{lem:factor-saturation}, all of its irreducible factors.
		Apply the same procedure to each new nonbinary factor.  This
		finite recursive procedure reaches one of the following outcomes:
		\begin{enumerate}
			\item \(\KHolant(\cF)\) is \(\SharpP\)-hard or
			\(\Tract(K\cF)\) holds.
            \item there is a strict update \((A,\cF)\mapsto(A^+,\cF^+)\)
            satisfying \cref{def:height-one-outer-successor}.
			\item there is a finite preprocessed algebraic factor-closed set
			\(\cF^{\mathrm{cl}}\supseteq\cF\), with
			\[
			\KHolant(\cF^{\mathrm{cl}})\equivT\KHolant(\cF),
			\qquad G(\cF^{\mathrm{cl}})=H,
			\]
			for which the coordinate matrix remains \(A\).  Every indicated
			contraction of two variables has been processed, every
			nonbinary irreducible factor supplied by factor closure is retained in
			\(\cF^{\mathrm{cl}}\), and every binary factor it produces is
			nonsingular with transfer class in \(H\).  A nonbinary factor produced
			from a parent \(f\) has arity at most \(\arity(f)-2\).
		\end{enumerate}
        Outcome~3 is closure under all the indicated contractions and factor
        extractions, with the same group and coordinate matrix. It may preserve
        \(\nu\), so it need not be a strict update.
	\end{lemma}
	
	\begin{proof}
		Begin with the finitely many nonbinary irreducible factors in
		\(\cF\).  For each factor, process its finite set of
		ordered-variable, representative, and orientation choices.  For each choice,
		add the resulting contraction and all irreducible factors
		under \cref{eq:finite-equivalent-enlargement,eq:factor-saturation}.
		The transformed mountain rules out the common-one-sided factor exception.
		
		An odd-arity factor gives outcome~1 by \cref{thm:external-odd,eq:K-equivalence}.
		A singular binary, or a nonsingular binary whose transfer has infinite
		projective order, is handled by \cref{lem:binary-interpolation,lem:common-unary-exit}.  After each addition, consider all directly realizable
		nonzero ordered binaries.  If any is singular or its transfer has infinite
		projective order, the preceding argument gives outcome~1.
		Otherwise the group is defined, and an element outside \(H\) strictly
		enlarges the group in the original basis,
		and a new nonbinary irreducible factor of arity below \(\nu(\cF)\)
		strictly decreases \(\nu\).  These give outcome~2, with both comparisons
		preserved as in \cref{def:height-one-outer-successor}.
		If neither occurs, retain all the binary factors and repeat the procedure
		on each new nonbinary irreducible factor.
		
		If no classification or strict update occurs, the group and its chosen
		representatives stay fixed. There are finitely many initial factors and
		finitely many choices at each step. Every newly produced nonbinary factor
		has arity at most the arity of
		its parent minus two.  The recursive depth is therefore bounded, so only
		finitely many factors and contraction choices occur.  If neither of the
		first two outcomes occurs, processing all these choices gives outcome~3.
		Each addition keeps the transformed mountain in the set and satisfies the fixed-field and scalar conditions of \cref{lem:normalization-ledger}. \end{proof}

Thus the recursion terminates by arity descent, while
\cref{lem:height-one-progress-well-founded} bounds the number of strict
updates between such recursions.
	
	\subsection{Contractions for the odd cyclic and dihedral groups}
	\label{subsec:residual-phase-toolkit}

	
	For tensors \(A,B\) of the same arity, a pencil is a family \(q_s=A-sB\).
	Its parameter range is specified at each use.
	
	
	
	We now turn the contraction condition into restrictions on the signatures
	themselves. The next two lemmas constrain four coefficients at a time and
	then the parity of the whole Walsh support. Both apply in the cyclic and
	dihedral cases.
We use the Walsh coordinates \(\widetilde f\) defined in
\cref{eq:Walsh-coordinates}.
	Each contraction below is realized by an \(X\)-edge gadget.
The matrix \(P\) expresses the same output in Walsh coordinates, where
the dependence on the phase is linear. Representative scalars are retained
in the gadget reduction.
	
	\subsubsection{Contraction kernels and the four-coefficient relation}
	\label{subsec:odd-dih-cards}
	
	Fix odd \(N\ge3\).  With \(R(s)\) as in
	\cref{thm:finite-group-split}, the rotation and reflection classes are
	\begin{equation}
		[R(s)],\qquad[ZR(s)],
		\qquad s\in\mu_N.
		\label{eq:odd-dih-native-transfers}
	\end{equation}
	
	The Walsh forms are
	\[
	P^{-1}R(s)P=D(s),
	\qquad P^{-1}ZP=X.
	\]
	
	For binary chains \(L,R\), with free inputs indexing rows and joined
	inputs indexing columns, joining their second inputs through one \(X\)-edge
	gives \(LXR^{\mathsf T}\).  Fix a direct binary-chain representative of
	each projective class in \cref{eq:odd-dih-native-transfers}.  Contracting
	two variables then gives the following Walsh kernels,
	up to a nonzero scalar:
	
	\begin{equation}
		\mathsf K^+(s)=D(-s)=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\0&-s\end{pmatrix},
		\qquad
		\mathsf K^-(s)=D(-s)X=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\-s&0\end{pmatrix},
		\qquad s\in\mu_N.
		\label{eq:odd-dih-kernels}
	\end{equation}
	
	We call contraction with \(\mathsf K^+(s)\) a \emph{phase contraction}
and contraction with \(\mathsf K^-(s)\) a \emph{reflection contraction}.
Their nonzero entries select equal and unequal bits, respectively.
They correspond to the rotation and reflection classes above.
Phase contractions occur in both groups, while reflection contractions occur
only in the dihedral group. In Walsh coordinates, swapping the two inputs acts on a
transfer matrix \(T\) by
\[
P^{-1}(PTP^{-1})^\#P=ZT^{\mathsf T}Z.
\]
This map fixes \(D(s)\) and sends \(XD(s)\) to \(-sXD(s^{-1})\).
It therefore preserves the projective rotation and reflection classes.
Attaching binary representatives to the variables adds no kernels, so
\cref{eq:odd-dih-kernels} lists every oriented kernel needed below.
	
	The next coefficient relation and lemma hold for every integer
	\(N\ge3\). For such an \(N\) and
	\(a,b,c,d\in\AlgNums\), let \(\mathcal R_N(a,b,c,d)\) mean that, for some
	\(\alpha\in\mu_N\), one of the following three systems holds:
	
	\begin{align}
		c&=-\alpha a,&d&=-\alpha b,
		&&\text{(proportional)},
		\label{eq:odd-dih-R-proportional}\\
		b&=c=0,&d&=\alpha a,
		&&\text{(diagonal monomial)},
		\label{eq:odd-dih-R-diagonal}\\
		a&=d=0,&c&=\alpha b,
		&&\text{(anti-diagonal monomial)}.
		\label{eq:odd-dih-R-antidiagonal}
	\end{align}
	
	
	\begin{lemma}[Phase-pencil relation]
		\label{lem:odd-dih-RN}
		For every integer \(N\ge3\), the relation
		\(\mathcal R_N(a,b,c,d)\) is equivalent to the following
		condition: for every \(s\in\mu_N\),
		\[
		\diag(a-sb,c-sd)Z
		\quad\text{is zero or is proportional to }D(\eta)
		\text{ for some }\eta\in\mu_N.
		\]
	\end{lemma}
	
	\begin{proof}
		This is \cite[Lemma~7.1]{Liu2026DEQ2Holant}.
	\end{proof}
	
	\subsubsection{Support parity}
	\label{subsec:odd-dih-parity}
	
	\begin{lemma}[Walsh parity from contractions]
		\label{lem:odd-dih-all-arity-parity}
		Let \(N\ge3\) be odd, and let \(H\) be the cyclic or dihedral group
		in Walsh coordinates, respectively
		\[
		H=\{[D(s)]:s\in\mu_N\}
		\quad\text{or}\quad
		H=\{[D(s)],[XD(s)]:s\in\mu_N\}.
		\]
		Let \(f\ne0\) have positive even arity. At arity two, suppose the
		Walsh transfer class \([P^{-1}(fX)P]\) belongs to \(H\).  At arity at least four, suppose that every irreducible factor of every nonzero contraction of \(f\) using an ordered pair of distinct
		variables, a chosen binary representative with Walsh transfer class in \(H\), and either kernel orientation is supported on
		one Walsh parity class. In the cyclic case suppose that class is even.  Then
		\(\widetilde f\) is supported on one parity class, which is even in the
		cyclic case.
	\end{lemma}
	
	\begin{proof}
		At arity two, a Walsh binary is, up to a nonzero scalar, a diagonal or
		anti-diagonal transfer times \(Z\), and hence has one parity.  Only the
		diagonal alternative occurs in the cyclic group.  At larger arity, the
		tensor product of the irreducible factors of a nonzero contraction has one parity. In the
		cyclic case it is even.
		
		At arity four, each \(\mathsf K^+(s)\)-contraction is zero, diagonal, or
		anti-diagonal, and every entry is affine in \(s\).  Among three phases,
		either two contractions vanish, annihilating the pencil, or two nonzero contractions have
		the same support parity, which forces the opposite entries to vanish
		identically.  In the only remaining pattern---one zero, one diagonal, and
		one anti-diagonal contraction---each opposite pair acquires two roots, with the
		same conclusion.  Hence every equal-bit slice has one parity.
		For two supported strings \({\mathbf x},{\mathbf y}\) of opposite parity,
        there are positions \(i\ne j\) with \((x_i,y_i)=(x_j,y_j)\),
        unless the four pairs \((x_k,y_k)\), \(k\in[4]\), exhaust \(\Ftwo^2\).
        In the latter case both strings have weight two, contradicting their
        opposite parities. In the former case, deleting positions \(i,j\)
        contradicts the parity of the equal-bit slices.  In the cyclic case, odd Walsh parity would
		realize a reflection and is therefore excluded.
		
		For arity at least six, fix distinct variables \(i,j\).  The phase contraction is
		\[
		\widetilde h_s({\mathbf y})=\lambda_s\bigl(\widetilde f(00,{\mathbf y})
		-s\widetilde f(11,{\mathbf y})\bigr),
		\qquad \lambda_s\ne0.
		\]
		A zero contraction makes all others proportional.  Otherwise two phases have the
		same contraction parity.  Every coordinate in the opposite parity then
		vanishes at two distinct phases, so its affine coefficient vanishes
		identically. The two nonzero phases also recover the slices \(A,B\) with
		the common parity.
		
		In the cyclic case, delete two equal coordinates of any supported string and
		choose a phase other than the at most one value cancelling its residual
		coefficient. The resulting Walsh contraction has even-parity support. In the dihedral case,
        two supported strings \({\mathbf x},{\mathbf y}\) of opposite parity at
        arity at least six have positions \(i\ne j\) with
        \((x_i,y_i)=(x_j,y_j)\).  At most one phase cancels each residual
		coefficient, so \(|\mu_N|\ge3\) leaves a phase preserving both strings.
		Deleting the chosen pair preserves their opposite parities, contradicting
		the common slice parity.
	\end{proof}
	
	For every \(f\) covered by \cref{lem:odd-dih-all-arity-parity}, Walsh
	duality gives the equivalent complement relation in the \(K\)-coordinates,
	\[
	f(\bar {\mathbf x})=f({\mathbf x})
	\quad\text{or}\quad
	f(\bar {\mathbf x})=-f({\mathbf x}),
	\]
	
	with only the plus sign in the cyclic subcase.
	

\subsubsection{Quaternary classification under phase contractions}
	Fix \(N\ge3\) and a quaternary signature \(q\) in the current set, and put
	\(\widetilde q=(P^{-1})^{\otimes4}q\).  When \(\widetilde q\) is supported
	on the even Hamming-weight positions in Walsh coordinates, write
	
	\begin{equation}
		p=\widetilde q_{0000},
		\qquad t=\widetilde q_{1111},
		\qquad x_{ab}=\widetilde q_{\one_{\{a,b\}}}.
		\label{eq:odd-dih-q4-coordinates}
	\end{equation}

For every ordering \((i,j,k,l)\) of \([4]\), the phase-contraction matrix is
\[
H_{ij}^+(s)=\begin{pmatrix}p-sx_{ij}&0\\0&x_{kl}-st\end{pmatrix}.
\]
When its nonzero Walsh transfer classes belong to \(C_N\),
\cref{lem:odd-dih-RN} gives \(\mathcal R_N(p,x_{ij},x_{kl},t)\).

	\begin{lemma}[Quaternary classification under phase contractions]
		\label{lem:odd-cyc-q4-family}
		Let \(N\ge3\), let \(\cF\) be a finite algebraic signature set in
		\(K\)-coordinates, and, for every \(s\in\mu_N\), fix a gadget-realizable ordered binary representative \(C_s\) over \(\cF\), in a specified orientation,
		whose Walsh matrix is
		\[
		\widetilde C_s=\kappa_sD(s)Z,
		\qquad \kappa_s\in\AlgNums^\times.
		\]
		Let \(q\in\cF\) be quaternary, and
		suppose \(\widetilde q=(P^{-1})^{\otimes4}q\) is supported on the even Hamming-weight
		positions in Walsh coordinates.  Define \(p,t,x_{ab}\) by
		\cref{eq:odd-dih-q4-coordinates}.  Assume that, for every contracted variable pair \(\{i,j\}\),
		both orders of the residual pair \(\{k,l\}=[4]\setminus\{i,j\}\),
		and every \(s\in\mu_N\), contraction
		with \(C_s\) gives either the zero contraction or a nonsingular binary contraction whose
		Walsh transfer class belongs to
		\[
		C_N=\{[D(\eta)]:\eta\in\mu_N\}.
		\]
		Organize the eight
		coordinates into four complementary pairs.  Put \(B_0=(p,t)\) and
		\(\mathfrak I=\{\{1,2\},\{1,3\},\{1,4\}\}\).
		For \(I=\{a,b\}\in\mathfrak I\), set \(x_I=x_{ab}\),
		put \(I^c=[4]\setminus I\), and define
		the other three complementary pairs \(B_I=(x_I,x_{I^c})\),
        \(I\in\mathfrak I\). Then exactly one of the following holds:
		\begin{enumerate}
			\item If \(p,t\ne0\), then \(\lambda=t/p\in\mu_N\), and each pair \(B_I\)
			is zero or
			\begin{equation}
				B_I=(-p\lambda\alpha_I^{-1},-p\alpha_I),
				\qquad \alpha_I\in\mu_N.
				\label{eq:odd-cyc-q4-nonzero-endpoints}
			\end{equation}
			\item If \(p=t=0\), each pair \(B_I\) is zero or
			\[
			B_I=\rho_I(1,\alpha_I),
			\qquad \rho_I\ne0,
			\quad \alpha_I\in\mu_N.
			\]
		\end{enumerate}
		In particular, exactly one of \(p,t\) cannot be nonzero.
		Conversely, every even Walsh coefficient array in these alternatives satisfies the
		stated contraction condition under every phase contraction.
	\end{lemma}
	
	\begin{proof}
		This is \cite[Lemma~7.2]{Liu2026DEQ2Holant}.
	\end{proof}
	
	\subsection{The odd-dihedral case}
	\label{proofsec:odd-dihedral}

	\begin{theorem}[The odd-dihedral case]
		\label{thm:odd-dihedral}
		Let \((A,\cF)\) be a stage of the sequence in
		\cref{lem:normalization-ledger} at which \(\cF\) is finite, preprocessed,
		algebraic, and factor-closed and contains the transformed height-one
		mountain.  Suppose that, after the normalization of
		\cref{thm:finite-group-split}, every nonzero directly realizable binary is
		nonsingular with finite projective transfer order, and
		\(G(\cF)\) is
		\[
		D_{2N}=\{[R(s)],[ZR(s)]:s\in\mu_N\}
		\]
		for an odd \(N\ge3\).
		Then at least one of the following holds.
		\begin{enumerate}
			\item \(\KHolant(\cF)\) is \(\SharpP\)-hard or \(\Tract(K\cF)\) holds.
            \item There is a strict update \((A,\cF)\mapsto(A^+,\cF^+)\)
            satisfying \cref{def:height-one-outer-successor}.
			\item There is an equivalent finite preprocessed algebraic factor-closed
			set \(\cF^{\mathrm{cl}}\supseteq\cF\), in the same
			coordinates and with the same matrix \(A\), for which the fixed
			Walsh matrix \(P\) satisfies
			\[
			(P^{-1})^{\otimes\arity(f)}f\in\cP
			\quad(f\in\cF^{\mathrm{cl}}),
			\qquad
			P^{\mathsf T}XP=2Z\in\cP.
			\]
			Hence \(K\cF\) is \(\cP\)-transformable.
		\end{enumerate}
	\end{theorem}
	
	In the remaining dihedral branch, choose a nonbinary irreducible
signature of minimum arity after the finite contraction procedure. Its
nonzero contractions factor into binary signatures whose transfer classes
lie in the group. We show that this forces a known classification at arity four or contradicts
irreducibility at larger arities. The four-variable analysis controls
support and coefficient ratios, the six-variable argument uses the finite
classification in \cref{thm:cert-odd-dih-q6-interface}, and matching pencils
supply a common binary factor at higher arities.
	
	\subsubsection{Support and coefficients on even blocks}
	\label{subsec:odd-dih-phase-product-class}
	
For a partition \(\pi\) of a finite variable set \(V\) into even blocks,
using \(\one_C\) for the incidence vector of \(C\subseteq V\), define
	\[
	L_\pi=\Span_{\Ftwo}\{\one_C:C\in\pi\}.
	\]
	Its cosets are precisely the sets obtained by toggling each block as one
	unit.
	
	The class \(\mathcal P_N(V)\) consists of the zero tensor and
	all tensors \(g\) for which there exist such a partition \(\pi\), an offset
	\({\mathbf a}\in\Ftwo^V\), a scalar \(c\in\AlgNums\setminus\{0\}\), and block ratios
	\(r_C\in(-1)^{|C|/2}\mu_N\) such that
	\begin{align*}
		\supp(g)&={\mathbf a}+L_\pi,
		\\
		g\!\left({\mathbf a}+\sum_{C\in\pi}t_C\one_C\right)
		&=c\prod_{C\in\pi}r_C^{t_C}
		\qquad\bigl((t_C)_{C\in\pi}\in\Ftwo^\pi\bigr).
	\end{align*}
	For \(C\subseteq V\), write \({\mathbf a}|_C\) for the restriction of \({\mathbf a}\) to \(C\),
	and \(\overline{{\mathbf a}|_C}\) for its bitwise complement.
	Equivalently, with the tensor factors placed on their indicated variable sets,
	\[
	g=c\bigotimes_{C\in\pi}
	\bigl(\delta_{{\mathbf a}|_C}+r_C\delta_{\overline{{\mathbf a}|_C}}\bigr).
	\]
	The block variables \((t_C)\) therefore have logical coefficient tensor
	\(c\prod_C r_C^{t_C}\). The block ratios will let us track coefficients
	as well as support through contractions.  For nonzero \(g\), its support
	determines \(\pi\): the inclusion-minimal nonzero vectors of \(L_\pi\) are
	precisely the block indicators.  We omit \(V\) when clear.  The class
	\(\mathcal P_N\) differs from the standard product class \(\cP\) of
	\cref{subsec:standard-classes}. Its even-block and coefficient-ratio
	restrictions remain part of its definition.
	
	\begin{lemma}[Closure of \(\mathcal P_N\) under contractions]
		\label{lem:phase-product-closure}
		The class \(\mathcal P_N\) is closed under nonzero scalar multiplication,
		variable permutation, tensor product on disjoint variable sets, and
		contraction of two variables against any kernel
		\(\mathsf K^+(s)\), \(\mathsf K^-(s)\), or its transpose from
		\cref{eq:odd-dih-kernels}, where \(s\in\mu_N\).  Consequently, if
		every irreducible factor of a contraction has Walsh tensor in
		\(\mathcal P_N\), then the contraction itself has Walsh tensor in
		\(\mathcal P_N\).
	\end{lemma}
	
	\begin{proof}
		For a tensor product, take the disjoint union of the two even-block
		partitions and concatenate their offsets and block ratios.  Scalars and variable
		permutations merely change the leading coefficient or relabel that data.
		
		Consider a contraction of two variables of a nonzero tensor in \(\mathcal P_N\).  If the
		variables lie in the same block \(C\), the kernel with the wrong xor gives zero.
		For the supported xor, delete the two variables.  When variables remain in \(C\), its
		new block ratio is
		\[
		-s r_C\quad\hbox{or}\quad-r_C/s,
		\]
		and therefore belongs to
		\((-1)^{(|C|-2)/2}\mu_N\).  If \(|C|=2\), the block instead contributes a
		scalar, possibly zero, which is absorbed into the leading coefficient.
		
		If the variables lie in distinct blocks \(C,D\), the kernel affinely identifies
		their two block variables.  After complementing one residual offset when
		needed, replace those blocks by
		\[
		(C\setminus\{i\})\mathbin{\dot\cup}(D\setminus\{j\}).
		\]
		Its ratio is one of
		\[
		-s^{\pm1}r_Cr_D^{\pm1}
		\in(-1)^{(|C|+|D|-2)/2}\mu_N.
		\]
		All other blocks are unchanged.  Transposing \(\mathsf K^-(s)\) only supplies
		a nonzero scalar and replaces \(s\) by \(s^{-1}\), so it is covered as well.
		Thus every nonzero contraction is again in \(\mathcal P_N\).
		The last assertion follows from factorization into irreducibles. Nullary factors are
		absorbed into the scalar.
	\end{proof}
	
	\subsubsection{Quaternary support and coefficients}
	\label{subsec:odd-dih-q4}
	

Use the even-parity coordinates in \cref{eq:odd-dih-q4-coordinates}.
For every ordering \((i,j,k,l)\) of \([4]\), the reflection-contraction matrix is
\[
H_{ij}^-(s)=\begin{pmatrix}0&x_{jl}-sx_{il}\\x_{jk}-sx_{ik}&0\end{pmatrix}.
\]
Condition~\ref{cond:height-one-contractions} for the dihedral group gives
\(\mathcal R_N(p,x_{ij},x_{kl},t)\) from the phase contraction and
\(\mathcal R_N(x_{jk},x_{ik},x_{jl},x_{il})\) from the reflection contraction.

	When \(\widetilde q\) is supported on the odd Hamming-weight positions in Walsh coordinates, write
	\[
	u_i=\widetilde q({\mathbf e}_i),
	\qquad v_i=\widetilde q(\one^4\oplus {\mathbf e}_i).
	\]
	
	For every such variable ordering, condition~\ref{cond:height-one-contractions}
	for the dihedral group gives
	\[
	\mathcal R_N(u_k,v_l,u_l,v_k),
	\qquad
	\mathcal R_N(u_j,u_i,v_i,v_j).
	\]
	
	\begin{proposition}[Dihedral quaternary classification]
		\label{prop:odd-dih-q4}
		Let \(N\ge3\) be odd, and let \(q\in\cF\) be a nonzero quaternary
		satisfying condition~\ref{cond:height-one-contractions} with
		\(H=G(\cF)=\{[R(s)],[ZR(s)]:s\in\mu_N\}\), with \(R(s)\) as in \cref{thm:finite-group-split}.  Put
		\(\widetilde q=(P^{-1})^{\otimes4}q\).  Then
		\(\widetilde q\in\mathcal P_N([4])\).  More precisely,
		\(\widetilde q\) has one Walsh parity and exactly one or two complementary
		pairs whose coefficient pairs are nonzero.  After choosing an orientation, there is a scalar
		\(\rho\in\AlgNums\setminus\{0\}\) such that, for one nonzero complementary
		pair \(\{{\mathbf x},\bar {\mathbf x}\}\), its nonzero coefficients in the order
		\((\widetilde q_{\mathbf x},\widetilde q_{\bar {\mathbf x}})\) are
		
		\begin{equation}
			\rho(1,\delta),
			\qquad \delta\in\mu_N,
			\label{eq:odd-dih-q4-one-block}
		\end{equation}
		
		or, for two nonzero complementary pairs \(\{{\mathbf x},\bar {\mathbf x}\}\) and
		\(\{{\mathbf y},\bar {\mathbf y}\}\), its nonzero coefficients in the order
		\((\widetilde q_{\mathbf x},\widetilde q_{\bar {\mathbf x}},
		\widetilde q_{\mathbf y},\widetilde q_{\bar {\mathbf y}})\) are
		
		\begin{equation}
			\rho(1,\delta,-\gamma,-\delta\gamma^{-1}),
			\qquad \delta,\gamma\in\mu_N.
			\label{eq:odd-dih-q4-two-block}
		\end{equation}
		
		Conversely, every Walsh coefficient array in
		\cref{eq:odd-dih-q4-one-block,eq:odd-dih-q4-two-block} satisfies the
		stated contraction condition under all phase and reflection contractions.
	\end{proposition}
	
	\begin{proof}
		Phase contractions give a \(\mu_N\)-ratio on every nonzero complementary
		pair.  If \(p,t\ne0\), \cref{eq:odd-cyc-q4-nonzero-endpoints} puts all nonzero central
		coefficients in \(-p\mu_N\).  For two nonzero central coefficient pairs, the
		\(2\)-by-\(2\) coefficient matrix of the reflection contraction has all
		four entries nonzero, and its only possible proportional alternative
		in \cref{lem:odd-dih-RN} requires a ratio in \(-\mu_N\), not \(\mu_N\).
		The cosets are disjoint for odd \(N\).
		
		With zero endpoints, three nonzero central coefficient pairs would require three
		pairwise ratios in \(-\mu_N\), although the product of two lies in
		\(\mu_N\).  Thus at most two complementary pairs have nonzero coefficients.  Applying the same argument
		to both orientations of the reflection kernels
		\(\mathsf K^-(s)\) gives the identical conclusion on the odd Hamming-weight positions.
		
		Substitution gives \cref{eq:odd-dih-q4-one-block,eq:odd-dih-q4-two-block}
		and proves their converse, including zeros.  The one-pair case uses \(\pi=\{[4]\}\). The two-pair case forms a \(2+2\) partition coset with ratios
		\(-\gamma,-\delta\gamma^{-1}\in-\mu_N\), whose product is \(\delta\).
		Thus both parities lie in \(\mathcal P_N([4])\), with odd parity absorbed
		by the coset offset.
		
		The one-pair case is a CP signature.  Up to flips and permutation, a
		support consisting of two complementary pairs is \(\{0000,1111,1100,0011\}\), and
		\cref{eq:odd-dih-q4-two-block} gives
		\[
		\widetilde q_{\mathbf x}\widetilde q_{\bar {\mathbf x}}
		=\widetilde q_{\mathbf y}\widetilde q_{\bar {\mathbf y}},
		\]
		
		for the two complementary pairs \(\{{\mathbf x},\bar {\mathbf x}\}\) and
		\(\{{\mathbf y},\bar {\mathbf y}\}\) in the support.  Hence it is a product of two
		nonsingular weighted equality/disequality
		binaries.  Thus \(\widetilde q\in\cP\), as required by
		\cref{eq:common-Walsh-product-presentation}.
	\end{proof}
	
	\subsubsection{Products of binaries and the arity-six case}
	\label{subsec:odd-dih-q6}
	
Given a finite variable set \(V\), a perfect matching \(M\) of \(V\), and
	\({\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}\in\Ftwo^M\), the corresponding \emph{matching coset} is
	\[
	C(M,{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}})=
	\{{\mathbf x}\in\Ftwo^V:x_u\oplus x_v=\epsilon_{\{u,v\}}
	\text{ for every }\{u,v\}\in M\}.
	\]
Equivalently, \(C(M,{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}})={\mathbf a}+L_M\), where \({\mathbf a}\in\Ftwo^V\) satisfies
\(a_u\oplus a_v=\epsilon_{\{u,v\}}\) for every matched pair.
Choose an order \((u,v)\) for each pair and use \(x_u\) as its independent
bit, so \(x_v=x_u\oplus\epsilon_{\{u,v\}}\). The coefficients indexed by
these bits form the \emph{logical coefficient tensor}.
The all-zero and all-one choices of \({\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}\) impose
equal and unequal bits on every matched pair, respectively.
The matching support alone does not impose a rank-one condition on this tensor.

	For the minimum-arity argument, contractions must be products of binaries.
	Write \(\mathcal B_N(V)\subseteq\mathcal P_N(V)\) for zero together with
	the tensors whose partition consists entirely of pairs.  Every nonzero
	member is a product of nonsingular Walsh equality/disequality binaries,
	with ratio in \(-\mu_N\) on each pair.  For the resulting perfect matching
	\(M\), its support is \({\mathbf a}+L_M\), is complement-closed, and has
	cardinality \(2^{|V|/2}\).
	The contraction formulas in \cref{lem:phase-product-closure} show that
	\(\mathcal B_N\) is itself closed under all phase and reflection contractions:
	joining different pairs makes one pair, and closing a pair gives a scalar.
	
	\begin{theorem}[Arity-six factorization from contractions]
		\label{thm:odd-dih-q6-base}
		Let \(N\ge3\) be odd.  If every nonzero oriented phase or reflection contraction
		of an arity-six signature \(f\) has Walsh tensor in \(\mathcal B_N([4])\),
		then \(f=0\) or \(f\) is a tensor product of three nonsingular binaries.
	\end{theorem}
	
	\begin{proof}
		Suppose \(f\ne0\), and write \(F=\widetilde f\).  By
		\cref{lem:odd-dih-all-arity-parity}, \(F\) has one parity.  Fix a variable pair
		and fix \(x_i\oplus x_j=\xi\), with the two corresponding slices \(A,B\), so the normalized contractions are \(q_s=A-sB\).  Put \(U=\supp(A)\cup\supp(B)\).
		Each nonzero coordinate remains nonzero at at least \(N-1\) phases, while every
		nonzero contraction has four support points.  Thus
		\[
		(N-1)|U|\le4N.
		\]
		Moreover \(U=\bigcup_s\supp(q_s)\) is complement-closed.  For \(N\ge5\)
		it is therefore empty or consists of at most two complementary pairs.  For
		\(N=3\), the same conclusion is exactly
		\cref{thm:cert-odd-dih-q6-interface}\textnormal{(i)}.  Part~\textnormal{(ii)}
		of that theorem now applies for every odd \(N\): the
		support of \(F\) is a partition coset of type \((6)\), \((4+2)\), or
		\((2+2+2)\), or belongs to the orbit of
		\begin{equation}
			S_\star=\{000000,000011,001100,110011,111100,111111\}.
			\label{eq:odd-dih-q6-nonproduct-support}
		\end{equation}
		Here and below, bit relabelings describe support coordinates only.  They
		permute the available xor kernels projectively, with possible phase
		inversion, so all contraction arguments cover the full indicated orbits.
		
		A \((6)\) support has a supported-xor contraction with exactly two
		nonzero entries, each with a unique lift, meaning a unique extension of the
		residual assignment to the original support consistent with the kernel.  This contradicts the four-point
		support required by \(\mathcal B_N([4])\).  On a \((4+2)\) support, use
		block variables and denote the four nonzero coefficients by \(c_{uv}\).
		Closing the binary block gives, after normalizing its orientation,
		\[
		(c_{00}-s c_{01})\delta_{0000}
		+(c_{10}-s c_{11})\delta_{1111}.
		\]
		At most two phases are forbidden by cancellation. Since \(N\ge3\), some
        contraction again has exactly two nonzero entries. On \(S_\star\), contract variables
		\(3,5\) with \(\mathsf K^+(s)\).  Only the two endpoints have equal bits
		there, giving
		\[
		F_{000000}\delta_{0000}-sF_{111111}\delta_{1111},
		\]
		again with two nonzero entries and unique lifts.
		
		Only the \((2+2+2)\) support remains.  Its eight coefficients
		\(c_{uvw}\) are nonzero.  Closing any one of its binary blocks leaves a
		\(2\)-by-\(2\) table whose determinant vanishes at every \(s\in\mu_N\):
		a nonzero contraction has the same four-point matching support, and a zero contraction
		also has zero determinant.  For example,
		\[
		\det\bigl(c_{uv0}-s c_{uv1}\bigr)_{u,v\in\Ftwo}=0.
		\]
		This polynomial has degree at most two and at least three roots.  Its
		constant and quadratic coefficients show that the two faces obtained by fixing that coordinate have
		rank one.  Doing this for all three axes makes every axis face minor zero.
		Since all eight entries are nonzero, the ratios along each axis are
		independent of the other two bits, so \(c_{uvw}=c r_1^u r_2^v r_3^w\).
		This is the required three-binary factorization, which pulls back through
		the Walsh change of coordinates.
	\end{proof}
	
	\subsubsection{Matching pencils}
	\label{subsec:odd-dih-support-circuits}

	At higher arities, binary factorizations of separate contractions must
	be compared as the phase varies. We call a pencil \(A-sB\) \emph{projectively nonconstant} when
	\(A,B\) are linearly independent.  Otherwise it is zero or has constant
	projective class wherever it is nonzero.
A projective line consists of the classes in a two-dimensional linear span.
Here rank one is taken over the matching blocks: it means that the logical
coefficient tensor is a product of one vector for each block.
A \emph{one-factor Segre ruling} is a linear family of such tensors in which
all but one block factor stay fixed.
A matrix flattening uses one block bit as the row index and the remaining
block bits as the column index.  The next two
results use this fixed-factor structure to pass from individual contraction
outputs to a common binary factor of the original tensor.
	
	\begin{lemma}[Three matching products on a line]
		\label{lem:odd-dih-three-coset-circuit}
		Let three pairwise nonproportional tensor products of nonsingular diagonal
		or anti-diagonal binaries on the same \(m\) variables satisfy a linear relation
		with all coefficients nonzero.  Either their supports coincide and
		the three tensors lie in a single one-factor Segre ruling, or their matchings differ only on four variables, where the three
		different pairings occur.  In the latter case all binary factors outside
		those four variables are common up to nonzero scalars.
	\end{lemma}
	
	\begin{proof}
		Let the supports be \(S_0,S_1,S_2\), matching cosets of dimension \(m/2\).
		No point can belong to just one support.  If two supports coincide, the
		third is contained in them and has the same cardinality, so all coincide.
		On their common block coordinates, every \(2\times2\) minor of a matrix flattening has degree at
		most two along the line.  Three rank-one points make these minors vanish
		identically.  A line of rank-one tensors varies in at most one factor: if
		two factors of two of its points were nonproportional, flattening across
		one of them would give rank two at a generic third point.  This is a single one-factor Segre ruling.
		
		Otherwise each \(S_i\) is the union of its intersections with the other
		two supports.  Two proper affine subspaces cover an affine space only when
		they are disjoint parallel hyperplanes: each has at most half its points,
		so both must attain that bound.  Thus the pairwise intersections are
		hyperplanes and the triple intersection is empty.  The union of two
		perfect matchings is a disjoint union of alternating even cycles, counting
		a common edge as a two-cycle.  If there are \(k=m/2\) edges and \(b\)
		components, the intersection of their translation spaces has dimension \(b\).  Here
		\(b=k-1\), so exactly one component is a four-cycle and all other edges
		coincide.  The offsets on those common edges agree because the supports
		intersect.  After removing their free binary coordinates, \(S_2\) is the
		four-point symmetric difference of the other two local supports. Its
		matching is the third pairing on the same four variables.  Finally, on each
		pairwise intersection only two tensors contribute to the dependence.
		Fixing one assignment to four variables with nonzero coefficient leaves the full remaining matching coset, on which their products are proportional.  Uniqueness of the factors
		on disjoint pairs gives the asserted common factors.
	\end{proof}
	
	\begin{proposition}[Phase-independent matching and common factors]
		\label{prop:odd-dih-support-circuit-rigidity}
		Let \(N\ge3\) be odd, and let \(f\) have even arity \(n\ge8\).  Suppose
		every nonzero result of one or more successive contractions of two variables using
		the kernels in
		\cref{eq:odd-dih-kernels} has Walsh tensor in \(\mathcal B_N\).  Then each
		contraction pencil can be written as \(q_s=H\otimes h_s\), where \(H\)
		is a fixed product of nonsingular binaries on disjoint pairs and \(h_s\)
		is a binary tensor on one fixed remaining variable pair.  Zero members and proportional
		pencils are included.
	\end{proposition}
	
	\begin{proof}
		Write a pencil as \(q_s=A-sB\).  A zero member makes all other members
		proportional, which settles that case.  If the pencil is not projectively
		constant, any three distinct phases give a linear dependence with all three
		coefficients nonzero,
		so \cref{lem:odd-dih-three-coset-circuit} applies to these members of
		\(\mathcal B_N\).  In its common-support
		case, the \(2\times2\) flattening minors vanish identically, giving the fixed
		one-factor Segre ruling with that matching for the whole pencil.
		
		In the second case of \cref{lem:odd-dih-three-coset-circuit}, the matchings
		differ on four variables and all other binary factors are common.  Close every such common pair, on the original gadget,
		with its supported-xor kernel at phase \(1\).  For a binary ratio
		\(r\in-\mu_N\), this gives a nonzero multiple of \(1-r\), nonzero because
		\(N\) is odd.  These operations multiply the three contractions by the same
		nonzero scalar.  At least one pair is closed since \(n\ge8\).  Before
		closing the original pencil variables, the resulting arity-six tensor is a
		nonzero result of contractions of \(f\), hence is itself a binary matching
		product.  Its contractions cannot have three different matchings: closing variables
		of a fixed matching deletes an edge or merges two edges, independently of
		the phase.  This contradiction proves that the matching is independent of phase.
	\end{proof}
	
	\subsubsection{A common binary factor suffices}
	\label{subsec:odd-dih-cross-sector}
	
	\begin{lemma}[A binary factor from contractions]
		\label{lem:odd-dih-common-binary-factor}
		Under the hypotheses of \cref{prop:odd-dih-support-circuit-rigidity}, every
		nonzero \(f\) is reducible.
	\end{lemma}
	
	\begin{proof}
		Put \(F=\widetilde f\), fix variables \(i,j\), and for each
		\(\xi\in\{0,1\}\) consider the restriction to \(x_i\oplus x_j=\xi\), viewed as an
		\(n\)-ary tensor that is zero outside that constraint.
		Its two slices are \(A=F_{ij}^{0,\xi}\), \(B=F_{ij}^{1,1-\xi}\).
		Exactly one of \(A,B\) cannot be nonzero.  Indeed, if \(A\ne0\) and \(B=0\), take a
		supported string of \(A\), close any two dangling edges with its supported
		xor, and choose a phase avoiding cancellation of the projected string.  The
		resulting nonzero contraction has complement-closed support.  Its
		complementary string would require an assignment in \(B\), a contradiction.
		The case \(A=0\), \(B\ne0\) is identical.
		
		For each nonzero restriction, \cref{prop:odd-dih-support-circuit-rigidity} writes
		the pencil as \(H\otimes h_s\), where \(H\) is a fixed product of external
		binaries and
		\[
		h_s=(a-sb)\delta_{\mathbf e}+(c-sd)\delta_{\bar {\mathbf e}}
		\]
		on one external pair, with \({\mathbf e}\in\Ftwo^2\).  Projectively constant pencils are included by
		choosing any pair.  Every nonzero \(h_s\) has ratio in \(-\mu_N\), so
		\cref{lem:odd-dih-RN} applies.  Restoring the variables \(i,j\), its
		proportional alternative gives a binary block \(\{i,j\}\). Either
		monomial alternative gives one quaternary CP block containing \(i,j\).
		All other blocks are nonsingular binaries.  Both slices \(A,B\) are
        nonzero, so the restored block has both coefficients nonzero.
        This factors the restriction in Walsh coordinates.
		
		If only one of the two restrictions is nonzero, this factorization of \(F\) already has an
		external binary factor, since \(n\ge8\).  Otherwise denote the restrictions for \(\xi=0,1\) by \(U,V\).  We use the following observation repeatedly.  Close
		an external pair with an xor supported by both restrictions, and choose a phase
		at which both resulting contractions are nonzero.  Such a phase exists: a contraction of
		either displayed block product with a supported xor has unique lifts, except when an entire
		binary block is closed, in which case only one phase can cancel its
		scalar.  The nonzero contraction \(G\) is a binary matching product with
		both \(ij\) xors present.  Its two \(ij\)-xor restrictions have:
		\begin{enumerate}[label=\textnormal{(O\arabic*)},leftmargin=*]
			\item the same block of four variables containing \(i,j\).
			\item the same external matching and the same offset on each external pair.
			\item proportional binary factors on each external pair, including
			equality of their ordered coefficient ratios.
		\end{enumerate}
		Indeed, \(i,j\) belong to different matching edges in \(G\).  Either xor
		restriction joins those edges into one block of four variables and leaves every
		other binary factor unchanged.  In particular, this observation identifies
		coefficients as well as supports.
		
		First, neither \(U\) nor \(V\) can have \(\{i,j\}\) as a binary block.
		If \(U\) did, close variables in two distinct external binary blocks of
		\(U\).  Both xors occur in \(U\), so one is also supported by \(V\).
		The observation would require a block of four variables through \(i,j\), whereas
		this contraction leaves their binary block unchanged.
		
		Write the blocks of four variables of \(U,V\) as
		\(K=\{i,j,a,b\}\), \(L=\{i,j,c,d\}\).  Close \(a,b\) with its fixed
		xor in \(U\).  That restriction now has \(\{i,j\}\) as a binary block, so the
		observation implies that \(V\) does not support this xor.  Therefore
		\(a,b\) lie in the same block of \(V\), with the opposite xor.  Either
		\(L=K\), or \(\{a,b\}\) is an external binary block of \(V\).  By
		symmetry, in the latter case \(\{c,d\}\) is an external binary block of
		\(U\), and the two pairs are disjoint.  Choose a variable \(e\) outside
		\(\{i,j,a,b,c,d\}\), possible since \(n\ge8\), and close \(a,e\).
		Both restrictions have unique lifts for either xor.  The block of four variables through
		\(i,j\) in the contracted \(U\) contains \(b\), whereas that in the
		contracted \(V\) remains \(\{i,j,c,d\}\).  This contradicts the
		observation.  Thus \(K=L\).
		
		Let \(M_U,M_V\) be the external matchings on \(E=[n]\setminus K\),
		and write \(M_U(e),M_V(e)\) for the respective mates of \(e\).
		For \(e\in E\), closing \(a,e\) makes the block of four variables through
		\(i,j\) equal to \(\{i,j,b,M_U(e)\}\) in one restriction and
		\(\{i,j,b,M_V(e)\}\) in the other.  The observation gives
		\(M_U(e)=M_V(e)\), so the external matchings coincide.  There are at least
		two external pairs.  For any one of them, \(R\), choose \(e\) on another
		pair and close \(a,e\).  The pair \(R\) remains external.  The observation
		now identifies its binary factor in the two restrictions, hence in
		\(U,V\), since the contraction avoids \(R\).  Therefore \(U+V=F\) has
		this common factor.  Invertible Walsh coordinates preserve tensor
		factorization, proving the lemma.
	\end{proof}
	
	\begin{proof}[Proof of \cref{thm:odd-dihedral}]
		Apply \cref{lem:height-one-card-saturation}.  Its first two outcomes are
		the first two conclusions of the theorem.  In outcome~3, work in the
		equivalent set \(\cF^{\mathrm{cl}}\), with the same matrix and
		group.  If a nonbinary irreducible signature remains, choose one, \(f\), of minimum arity
		\(n\).  Odd-arity factors have already been handled by \cref{thm:external-odd}.  Every irreducible factor of a contraction of two variables of \(f\)
		has smaller arity, and hence is binary by minimality.  Its transfer class is in
		\(D_{2N}\), so every nonzero contraction of two variables is in \(\mathcal B_N\) in Walsh
		coordinates.  Closure of \(\mathcal B_N\) gives the same assertion for
		all further contractions involving at least one variable pair.
		
		If \(n=4\), \cref{prop:odd-dih-q4} gives either a quaternary CP signature, to which \cref{lem:walsh-ge-terminal} applies, or a product of
		two binaries, contradicting irreducibility.  If \(n=6\),
		\cref{thm:odd-dih-q6-base} contradicts irreducibility.  If \(n\ge8\),
		\cref{lem:odd-dih-common-binary-factor} gives the same contradiction.
		Thus either hardness or tractability has been proved, or every irreducible
		in \(\cF^{\mathrm{cl}}\) is
		binary.  In the latter case one Walsh basis puts all signatures in \(\cP\),
		and \(P^{\mathsf T}XP=2Z\in\cP\), giving outcome~3 with the stated change of basis.
	\end{proof}
	
	\subsection{The odd-cyclic case}
	
	Only the phase-contraction kernels are available in the cyclic case. We
	first show that a minimum-arity nonbinary irreducible signature must have
	arity four. Binary attachments then turn that quaternary into a signature
	covered by an existing dichotomy or force a factorization. This gives the
	same classification-or-update alternatives as in the dihedral case.

	\begin{theorem}[The odd-cyclic case]
		\label{thm:odd-cyclic}
		Let \((A,\cF)\) be a stage of the sequence in
		\cref{lem:normalization-ledger} at which \(\cF\) is finite, preprocessed,
		algebraic, and factor-closed and contains the transformed height-one
		mountain.  Let \(N\ge3\) be odd, and suppose every nonzero directly
		realizable binary is nonsingular with finite projective transfer order, and
		\[
		[P]^{-1}G(\cF)[P]
		=C_N=\{[\diag(1,s)]:s\in\mu_N\}.
		\]
		Then at least one of the following holds.
		\begin{enumerate}
			\item \(\KHolant(\cF)\) is \(\SharpP\)-hard or \(\Tract(K\cF)\) holds.
            \item There is a strict update \((A,\cF)\mapsto(A^+,\cF^+)\)
            satisfying \cref{def:height-one-outer-successor}.
			\item There is an equivalent finite preprocessed algebraic factor-closed
			set \(\cF^{\mathrm{cl}}\supseteq\cF\), in the same
			coordinates and with the same matrix \(A\), for which the fixed
			Walsh matrix \(P\) satisfies
			\[
			(P^{-1})^{\otimes\arity(f)}f\in\cP
			\quad(f\in\cF^{\mathrm{cl}}),
			\qquad
			P^{\mathsf T}XP=2Z\in\cP.
			\]
			Hence \(K\cF\) is \(\cP\)-transformable.
		\end{enumerate}
	\end{theorem}
	
	\subsubsection{Phase-independent matching}
	\label{subsec:odd-cyc-pencil}
	
	Throughout this odd-cyclic proof, \(\widetilde f\) denotes the fixed
	Walsh coordinates, and
	\([P]^{-1}G(\cF)[P]=C_N=\{[D(s)]:s\in\mu_N\}\), with one direct representative fixed for
	each class and \(N\ge3\) odd.
	
	For the conditional analysis below, work in outcome~3 of
	\cref{lem:height-one-card-saturation}, retaining its finite, preprocessed,
	algebraic, factor-closed set, still denoted by \(\cF\), the matrix \(A\), and the
	transformed height-one mountain.  Odd-arity irreducible signatures have
	already been handled by \cref{thm:external-odd}.  If only binary irreducible factors remain, the common Walsh basis gives
	the third conclusion of the theorem. Otherwise choose a nonbinary
	irreducible \(f\in\cF\) of minimum arity \(n\), which is even.
	For distinct variables \(i,j\), use the phase kernel \(\mathsf K^+(s)\)
    from \cref{eq:odd-dih-kernels}, with \(s\in\mu_N\).
    After division by the nonzero representative scalar, the contraction has Walsh coefficients
	\begin{equation}
		(\partial_{ij}^{\mathsf K^+(s)}\widetilde f)({\mathbf y})=A({\mathbf y})-sB({\mathbf y}),
		\qquad
		A({\mathbf y})=\widetilde f(00,{\mathbf y}),
		\quad B({\mathbf y})=\widetilde f(11,{\mathbf y}).
		\label{eq:odd-cyc-card-pencil}
	\end{equation}
	By \cref{lem:height-one-card-saturation}\textnormal{(3)}, each indicated
	nonzero contraction and all its irreducible factors belong
	to \(\cF\).  A nonbinary factor would have arity at most \(n-2\),
	contradicting minimality.  Every binary factor is nonsingular and its Walsh
	transfer class belongs to \(C_N\).  Thus
	condition~\ref{cond:height-one-contractions} holds for \(f\). Each nonzero
	contraction is a product of such binaries on a perfect matching.  A binary with Walsh transfer class \([D(t)]\) has
	Walsh tensor proportional to
	\[
	D(t)Z=\diag(1,-t).
	\]
Thus the support is the all-zero matching coset on the residual variables:
	for a perfect matching \(M\),
	\begin{equation}
		C(M,\mathbf0)=\{{\mathbf y}\in\Ftwo^{[n]\setminus\{i,j\}}:
		y_a=y_b\text{ for every }\{a,b\}\in M\}.
		\label{eq:odd-cyc-matching-code}
	\end{equation}
	The logical coefficient tensor on these matching variables is nowhere zero
    and has tensor rank one.  By
	\cref{lem:odd-dih-all-arity-parity}, \(\widetilde f\) has even Walsh
	parity.
	
	\begin{lemma}[Phase-independent matching]
		\label{lem:odd-cyc-one-matching}
		Let \(N\ge3\) be odd, and let \(f\in\cF\) be a nonbinary
		irreducible signature of minimum arity under the assumptions of this
		subsection, including condition~\ref{cond:height-one-contractions} with
		\([P]^{-1}G(\cF)[P]=C_N\).  Use the contractions and matching cosets in \cref{eq:odd-cyc-card-pencil,eq:odd-cyc-matching-code}.  For each pair
		\(i,j\), either every contraction vanishes, or \(\partial_{ij}^{\mathsf K^+(s)}\widetilde f=0\) for at most one
		phase \(s\in\mu_N\) and there is a fixed perfect matching \(M_{ij}\)
		such that
		\begin{equation*}
			\partial_{ij}^{\mathsf K^+(s)}\widetilde f\ne0
			\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
			\supp(\partial_{ij}^{\mathsf K^+(s)}\widetilde f)=C(M_{ij},\mathbf0)
			\qquad(s\in\mu_N).
		\end{equation*}
	\end{lemma}
	
	\begin{proof}
		If \(A-sB\) and \(A-tB\) vanish at distinct phases, then \(A=B=0\),
		so the first alternative holds.  Otherwise at most one phase gives a zero
		contraction.  Any two distinct nonzero contractions satisfy
		\begin{equation}
			\supp(A)\cup\supp(B)
			=\supp(A-sB)\cup\supp(A-tB),
			\label{eq:odd-cyc-two-card-union}
		\end{equation}
		because a scalar affine polynomial cannot vanish at two phases unless both
		coefficients vanish.
		
		If no contraction vanishes, choose three phases.  By
		\cref{eq:odd-cyc-two-card-union}, the third matching coset lies in the union
		of the first two.  A vector space contained in \(U\cup V\) lies in one of
		them (otherwise sum vectors from its two exclusive parts), and all matching
		cosets have the same dimension.  Thus the third coset equals one of the first
		two.  Two phases with one coset and a third with another would make a string in
		the latter difference vanish at two phases, contradicting the affine-pencil
		identity.  Hence all cosets coincide.
		
		If \(A-s_0B=0\), then every other contraction is \((s_0-s)B\), and the conclusion
		is immediate.
	\end{proof}
	
	\subsubsection{Pairs with every phase contraction zero}
	\label{subsec:odd-cyc-zero-graph}
	
	The preceding lemma leaves open the possibility that every phase
	contraction on a pair is zero. We encode these pairs by a graph and show
	that none occur above arity four. Define
	\begin{equation*}
		Z_f^{\mathrm{cyc}}
		=
		\{\{i,j\}:\partial_{ij}^{\mathsf K^+(s)}\widetilde f=0\text{ for every }s\in\mu_N\}.
	\end{equation*}
	Two phases in \cref{eq:odd-cyc-card-pencil} show that an edge is exactly
	the support equation
	\begin{equation*}
		\widetilde f({\mathbf x})\ne0
		\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
		x_i+x_j=1.
	\end{equation*}
	It follows that every connected component of
	\(Z_f^{\mathrm{cyc}}\) is complete bipartite.  An odd path forces unequal
	endpoint bits and hence supplies the corresponding edge. An even path
	forces equal bits and cannot also be a zero edge.
	
	\begin{lemma}[Every variable pair has a nonzero phase contraction above arity four]
		\label{lem:odd-cyc-zero-graph-empty}
		Under the assumptions of \cref{lem:odd-cyc-one-matching}, if
		\(\arity(f)=n\ge6\), then
		\[
		Z_f^{\mathrm{cyc}}=\varnothing.
		\]
	\end{lemma}
	
	\begin{proof}
        Each color class has size at most two: if one contained \(p,q,r\)
        and the other \(b\), some phase contraction on \(p,q\) would be
        nonzero. Its matching support contains the all-zero residual string,
        contradicting the forced disequality \(x_r\ne x_b\).
		
		An isolated \(\{1\}\sqcup\{2\}\) is impossible.  For every \(k\ge3\),
		the all-zero string of a nonzero \((1,k)\)-contraction has the unique lift
		\(x_1=x_k=1\), whence
		\begin{equation*}
			\widetilde f({\mathbf e}_1+{\mathbf e}_k)\ne0
			\qquad(k=3,\ldots,n).
		\end{equation*}
		A nonzero \((2,3)\)-contraction then has each \({\mathbf e}_1+{\mathbf e}_k\), \(k\ge4\), by the
		unique lift \(x_2=x_3=0\).  Its support matching would contain every
		\(\{1,k\}\), impossible.
		
		Thus only \(2+1\) and \(2+2\) remain.  Label the size-two color
		\(\{1,3\}\) and the other \(\{2\}\) or \(\{2,4\}\).  In a \((1,3)\)
		contraction the forced equations give the unique lift
		\[
		x_1=x_3=1-x_2.
		\]
		Put
		\[
		h(x_2,\mathbf y)=\widetilde f(1-x_2,x_2,1-x_2,\mathbf y),
		\]
		where \(\mathbf y\) denotes the remaining coordinates. In the \(2+2\)
		case, also impose the support equation \(x_4=x_2\).
		Then
		\begin{equation*}
			(\partial_{13}^{\mathsf K^+(s)}\widetilde f)(x_2,\mathbf y)=u_s(x_2)h(x_2,\mathbf y),
			\qquad
			u_s(0)=-s,\quad u_s(1)=1.
		\end{equation*}
		Every contraction is nonzero and equals a nowhere-zero unary \(u_s\) times \(h\).
		Dividing this contraction by the nowhere-zero unary \(u_s\) only modifies the matching factor incident
		with variable \(2\), so \(h\) factors over that matching. In the \(2+1\) case, let \(k\)
        be the mate of variable \(2\) in this matching. Reversing the unique
		lift gives a quaternary CP factor on \(\{1,2,3,4\}\) in the \(2+2\) case,
		or on \(\{1,2,3,k\}\) in the \(2+1\) case, tensor the remaining binaries.
		For \(n\ge6\) this contradicts irreducibility.
	\end{proof}
	
	\subsubsection{Weight-two coefficients and the arity bound}
	\label{subsec:odd-cyc-weight-two}
	
	We next use the weight-two coefficients to reconstruct a matching of
	the original variables. Once that matching is fixed, contractions impose
	rank-one restrictions on its logical coefficient tensor. The following lemma
	recovers the tensor from those restrictions.

	\begin{lemma}[Rank-one diagonal restrictions]
		\label{lem:diagonal-slice-integration}
		Let \(r\ge3\), and let \(h:\Ftwo^r\to\mathbb C^\times\) satisfy
		\(h(\zero^r)=1\).  Suppose that, for every \(i\ne j\), the restriction
		\(h|_{t_i=t_j}\), viewed as a function of the common bit and the other
		\(r-2\) bits, has tensor rank one.  If \(r\ge4\), then
		\[
		h({\mathbf t})=\prod_{i=1}^r\rho_i^{t_i}
		\qquad(\rho_i\ne0).
		\]
		If \(r=3\), there are \(\rho_1,\rho_2,\rho_3,\kappa\ne0\) such that
		\begin{equation}
			h({\mathbf e}_i)=\rho_i,\qquad
			h({\mathbf e}_i+{\mathbf e}_j)=\kappa\rho_i\rho_j,\qquad
			h(111)=\kappa\rho_1\rho_2\rho_3.
			\label{eq:diagonal-slice-q3-table}
		\end{equation}
	\end{lemma}
	
	\begin{proof}
		This is \cite[Lemma~7.8]{Liu2026DEQ2Holant}.
	\end{proof}
	
	\begin{theorem}[The minimum nonbinary arity is four in the cyclic case]
		\label{thm:odd-cyc-higher-collapse}
		Let \(\cF\) be a finite preprocessed algebraic factor-closed signature
		set containing the transformed height-one mountain.  Suppose every nonzero
		directly realizable binary is nonsingular and has finite projective transfer
		order, and, in the fixed
		Walsh coordinates,
		\[
		[P]^{-1}G(\cF)[P]
		=C_N=\{[\diag(1,s)]:s\in\mu_N\},
		\qquad N\ge3\text{ odd}.
		\]
		Let \(f\in\cF\) be an even-arity nonbinary irreducible signature of
		minimum arity among the nonbinary irreducible signatures in \(\cF\).
		For every ordered pair of distinct variables of \(f\), every fixed direct
		representative of a class in \(G(\cF)\), and either kernel orientation,
		assume that the resulting contraction of two variables is zero or all of its irreducible factors are nonsingular binaries \(B\) satisfying
		\[
		[P^{-1}(BX)P]\in C_N.
		\]
		Then \(\arity(f)\le4\), and hence \(\arity(f)=4\).
	\end{theorem}
	
	\begin{proof}
		The contraction hypothesis is condition~\ref{cond:height-one-contractions}
		for the stated group, so
		\cref{lem:odd-cyc-one-matching,lem:odd-cyc-zero-graph-empty} apply.
		Assume that \(f\) has arity \(n\ge6\).  By
		\cref{lem:odd-cyc-zero-graph-empty}, every pair has a nonzero phase contraction.
		For \(S\subseteq[n]\), write \(a_S=\widetilde f(\one_S)\).
		We abbreviate \(a_{i_1\cdots i_k}=a_{\{i_1,\ldots,i_k\}}\).
		
		\emph{Step 1: the constant coefficient is nonzero.}
		If \(a_\varnothing=0\), the all-zero coefficient
		\((\partial_{ij}^{\mathsf K^+(s)}\widetilde f)(0)=-sa_{ij}\) of a nonzero matching contraction forces
		\(a_{ij}\ne0\) for every pair.  Hence all contractions are nonzero.  For disjoint
		pairs \(ij,pq\),
		\[
		(\partial_{ij}^{\mathsf K^+(s)}\widetilde f)({\mathbf e}_p+{\mathbf e}_q)=a_{pq}-sa_{ijpq}
		\]
		is nonzero at some phase.  The common matching from
		\cref{lem:odd-cyc-one-matching} would therefore contain every edge on its
		at least four residual variables, impossible.  Normalize henceforth
		\(a_\varnothing=1\), and put
		\[
		W_f=\{\{p,q\}:a_{pq}\ne0\}.
		\]
		
		\emph{Step 2: a nonempty \(W_f\) factors \(f\).}
		For deleted \(i,j\) and a disjoint edge \(pq\in W_f\), the coefficients
		\(1-sa_{ij}\) and \(a_{pq}-sa_{ijpq}\) exclude at most one phase each.
		Thus some contraction contains \({\mathbf e}_p+{\mathbf e}_q\), and its common support matching
		contains \(pq\).  Deleting two variables outside any two adjacent
		\(W_f\)-edges would put both edges in one matching, so \(W_f\) is a
		matching.
		
		Moreover, every \(pq\in W_f\) is a global equality constraint.  Indeed, if
		some supported \({\mathbf z}\) had \(z_p\ne z_q\), choose equal coordinates \(i,j\)
		outside \(p,q\), possible by even parity and \(n\ge6\).  The residual string
		violates the \(pq\)-edge in every nonzero \((i,j)\)-contraction and therefore
		vanishes at all phases.  Two phases of
		\cref{eq:odd-cyc-card-pencil} then make both lifts vanish, including \({\mathbf z}\).
		Consequently
		\begin{equation}
			pq\in W_f\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
			\supp(\widetilde f)\subseteq\{{\mathbf z}:z_p=z_q\}.
			\label{eq:odd-cyc-global-equality}
		\end{equation}
		
		Fix \(E_1=\{u,v\}\in W_f\) and \(R=[n]\setminus E_1\).  In an
		\((u,k)\)-contraction, the all-zero string has the unique lift
		\(x_u=x_k=x_v=0\). Hence, for \(p,q\in R\setminus\{k\}\), its coefficient
		at \({\mathbf e}_p+{\mathbf e}_q\) is exactly \(a_{pq}\).  Its matching therefore consists of
		the \(W_f\)-edges on \(R\setminus\{k\}\) and one edge incident with \(v\).
		Since that last edge cannot cover all \(n-2\ge4\) variables, the induced
		matching on \(R\) is nonempty.  Choose \(k\) on one of its edges, with
		partner \(\ell\).  In the \((u,k)\)-contraction, \(\ell\) has no incident
		\(W_f\)-edge left, so it must be the partner of \(v\).  Any vertex
		unmatched in \(W_f|_R\) would also have to be matched to \(v\), which is
		impossible.  Thus \(W_f=\{E_1,\ldots,E_r\}\) is perfect, where \(r=n/2\).
		
		Equation \eqref{eq:odd-cyc-global-equality} identifies \(\widetilde f\)
		with a logical coefficient tensor \(h(t_1,\ldots,t_r)\) on the common bits of the
		\(E_k\)'s.  Contracting one variable from \(E_i\) and one from \(E_j\) imposes
		\(t_i=t_j\), and the remaining partners determine the deleted bits uniquely.
		The contraction is nonzero since its all-zero coefficient is one.  Its support is
		contained in the matching coset obtained by merging \(E_i,E_j\).  Both this
		coset and the contraction's matching support have \(2^{r-1}\) points, so they
		coincide.  Removing the nowhere-zero unary phase on the merged variable
		therefore gives a full-support rank-one diagonal slice of \(h\).  Every assignment in \(\Ftwo^r\) has two equal coordinates, so these
		restrictions cover \(\Ftwo^r\) and \(h\) has full support.  With \(h(\zero^r)=1\),
		\cref{lem:diagonal-slice-integration} factors \(h\) when \(r\ge4\).  If
		\(r=3\), use \cref{eq:diagonal-slice-q3-table}.  Let \(H_i(s)\) be the
		two-bit logical coefficient matrix obtained by closing \(E_i\) at phase \(s\).  For
		\(\{i,j,k\}=[3]\),
		\begin{equation*}
			\det H_i(s)=\rho_j\rho_k(\kappa-1)
			(1-\kappa s^2\rho_i^2).
		\end{equation*}
		If \(\kappa\ne1\), the contraction is nonzero and hence rank one for every phase,
		whereas \(s\mapsto s^2\) permutes the odd-order group \(\mu_N\). The last
		factor cannot vanish at two phases.  Thus \(\kappa=1\), and the arity-six
		tensor also factors.  Both cases contradict irreducibility.
		
		\emph{Step 3: an empty \(W_f\) is impossible.}
		Fix \(i,j\).  Every contraction now has constant coefficient one and is nonzero.
		Choose two edges \(e,e'\) of its common residual matching.  Full matching
		support gives
		\(a_{\{i,j\}\cup e}a_{\{i,j\}\cup e'}\ne0\), while its two-bit rank-one
		identity is
		\begin{equation*}
			a_{e\cup e'}-s a_{\{i,j\}\cup e\cup e'}
			=s^2a_{\{i,j\}\cup e}a_{\{i,j\}\cup e'}
			\qquad(s\in\mu_N).
		\end{equation*}
		This nonzero quadratic cannot vanish at the \(N\ge3\) distinct phases.
		The contradiction completes the proof.
	\end{proof}
	
	\subsubsection{The quaternary cyclic case}
	\label{subsec:odd-cyc-quaternary}
	
    The minimum nonbinary arity is now four, but the resulting quaternary
    need not be a mountain. We first record the eight-vertex classification
    used to finish this branch, then adjust the Walsh coefficients by binary
    attachments.
	
	An eight-vertex signature has arity four and is zero on inputs of odd Hamming weight.
	
	\begin{theorem}[Irreducible quaternary \(K\)-Holant dichotomy]
		\label{thm:quaternary-kholant-dichotomy}
		Let \(\cF\) be a finite algebraic signature set containing an
		irreducible eight-vertex signature \(M\) with \(M(0000)M(1111)\ne0\).
		If \(\Tract(K\cF)\) holds, then \(\KHolant(\cF)\in\FP\). Otherwise
		\(\KHolant(\cF)\) is \(\SharpP\)-hard.  The predicate \(\Tract(K\cF)\) is decidable.
	\end{theorem}
	
	\begin{proof}
		This is \cite[Theorem~1.3]{Liu2026DEQ2Holant}.
	\end{proof}
	
	\begin{corollary}[Eight-vertex \(K\)-Holant dichotomy]
		\label{cor:eight-vertex-complete-closure}
		Let \(\cF\) be a finite algebraic signature set containing an
		eight-vertex signature \(M\) with \(M(0000)M(1111)\ne0\).
		If \(\Tract(K\cF)\) holds, then \(\KHolant(\cF)\in\FP\). Otherwise
		\(\KHolant(\cF)\) is \(\SharpP\)-hard.
	\end{corollary}
	
	\begin{proof}
		If \(\Tract(K\cF)\) holds,
		\cref{thm:predicate-algorithms,eq:K-equivalence} give the polynomial-time
		conclusion.  Assume \(\neg\Tract(K\cF)\).  If \(M\) is irreducible,
		\cref{thm:quaternary-kholant-dichotomy} gives hardness.
		
		Suppose instead that \(M\) is reducible.  Its nonzero endpoint values
		exclude a \(1+3\) factorization: setting the unary block to \(1\) and the
		other block to \(000\) would give a nonzero odd-weight entry.  Thus every
		nontrivial factorization has type \(2+2\).  In such a factorization, the
		nonzero endpoints and the odd-weight zeros force each binary factor to be
		diagonal with both diagonal entries nonzero.  Up to a variable permutation and a
		nonzero scalar, one factor is therefore
		\[
		E=\diag(p,q),\qquad p,q\in\AlgNums^\times.
		\]
		Because \(M\) contains both charge signs, the common-one-sided exception in
		\cref{lem:factor-saturation} is impossible.  Factor extraction gives
		\(\KHolant(\cF,E)\leT\KHolant(\cF)\).
		Choose algebraic \(a,b\ne0\) with \(a^2p=b^2q=1\), and put
		\(D=\diag(a,b)\).  Then
		\[
		D^{\mathsf T}XD=abX,
		\qquad DED^{\mathsf T}=I,
		\]
		so \(D\in GO(X)\) and \(D(\cF\cup\{E\})\) contains \(I\).
		By \cref{lem:downward-inheritance,lem:GOX-tract-invariance}, this transformed
		set still fails \(\Tract\).  Hence \cref{thm:eq2-kholant-dichotomy}
		gives hardness, which the change of basis and the displayed reduction
        transfer to \(\KHolant(\cF)\).
	\end{proof}
	
	The next lemma specifies the output and phase choices of these attachments.

\begin{lemma}[Attaching cyclic binary representatives to four variables]
		\label{lem:odd-cyc-four-port-dressing}
		Let \(N\ge3\), let \(\cF\) be a finite algebraic signature set in
		\(K\)-coordinates, and, for every \(s\in\mu_N\), fix a gadget-realizable ordered binary representative \(C_s\) over \(\cF\), in a specified orientation,
		whose Walsh matrix is
		\[
		\widetilde C_s=\kappa_sD(s)Z,
		\qquad \kappa_s\in\AlgNums^\times.
		\]
		Let \(q\in\cF\) be quaternary.
		For \(r_1,r_2,r_3,r_4\in\mu_N\), attach \(C_{r_i}\) to variable \(i\) of
		\(q\) through one \(X\)-edge and leave the other variable of each
		representative exposed.  After division by
		\(2^4\prod_{i=1}^4\kappa_{r_i}\), the resulting output \(h\) satisfies
		\begin{equation*}
			\widetilde h_{\mathbf y}
			=\widetilde q_{\mathbf y}\prod_{i=1}^4r_i^{y_i}.
		\end{equation*}
		Consider the map
		\begin{equation*}
			(r_1,r_2,r_3,r_4)
			\longmapsto
			(R,r_{12},r_{13},r_{14}),
			\qquad
			R=\prod_{i=1}^4r_i,
			\quad r_I=\prod_{i\in I}r_i,
		\end{equation*}
		Write \((\mu_N)^2:=\{z^2:z\in\mu_N\}\).  A target
		\((R,u,v,w)\in\mu_N^4\) occurs exactly when
		\begin{equation}
			\frac{uvw}{R}\in(\mu_N)^2.
			\label{eq:cyclic-dressing-image}
		\end{equation}
		For odd \(N\), this map is bijective.
	\end{lemma}
	
    \begin{proof}
        In Walsh coordinates an attachment acts by
        \(\widetilde C_s(2Z)=2\kappa_sD(s)\). Thus four attachments give
        the normalization \(2^4\prod_{i=1}^4\kappa_{r_i}\) in the statement.
        The phase-map calculation is \cite[Lemma~7.3]{Liu2026DEQ2Holant}.\looseness=-1
	\end{proof}
	
	\begin{theorem}[The quaternary odd-cyclic case]
		\label{thm:odd-cyc-quaternary-closure}
		Let \(\cF\) be a finite preprocessed algebraic factor-closed signature
		set containing the transformed height-one mountain.  Suppose every nonzero
		directly realizable ordered binary is nonsingular and has finite projective
		transfer order, and, in the fixed Walsh coordinates,
		\[
		[P]^{-1}G(\cF)[P]
		=C_N=\{[\diag(1,s)]:s\in\mu_N\},
		\qquad N\ge3\text{ odd}.
		\]
		Let \(0\ne q\in\cF\) be quaternary.  For every ordered pair of distinct
		variables of \(q\), every fixed direct representative of a class in
		\(G(\cF)\), and either kernel orientation, assume that the resulting
		contraction of two variables is zero or all of its irreducible factors are
		nonsingular binaries \(B\) with
		\[
		[P^{-1}(BX)P]\in C_N.
		\]
		Then at least one of the following holds.
		\begin{enumerate}
			\item \(\KHolant(\cF)\) is \(\SharpP\)-hard or
			\(\Tract(K\cF)\) holds.
			\item Up to a permutation of its variables,
			\[
			q\doteq a\otimes b,
			\]
			where \(a,b\) are nonzero positive-arity signatures of arity strictly
			below four, and
			\[
			\KHolant(\cF,a,b)\leT\KHolant(\cF).
			\]
		\end{enumerate}
	\end{theorem}
	
	\begin{proof}
		The stated contractions satisfy
		condition~\ref{cond:height-one-contractions} for the cyclic group.
		In the Walsh tensor \(\widetilde q\), put
		\[
		p=\widetilde q_{0000},\qquad
		t=\widetilde q_{1111},\qquad
		x_I=\widetilde q_{\one_I}\quad(|I|=2).
		\]
		For each such \(I\), put \(B_I=(x_I,x_{I^c})\), so
		\(B_{I^c}\) has the same two coefficients in reverse order.
		By \cref{lem:odd-dih-all-arity-parity}, \(\widetilde q\) has even-parity support. \Cref{lem:odd-cyc-q4-family} excludes exactly one nonzero
		Walsh endpoint.  Suppose first that \(p,t\ne0\), and put \(\lambda=t/p\).
		For each \(B_I\ne(0,0)\), it gives
		\((x_I,x_{I^c})=(-p\lambda\alpha_I^{-1},-p\alpha_I)\).
		For each zero pair choose \(\alpha_I\in\mu_N\) arbitrarily, using one
		index from each complementary pair as in \cref{lem:odd-cyc-q4-family}.
		By \cref{lem:odd-cyc-four-port-dressing}, prescribe
